The Low-Carb Kitchen

Desserts first — and a dairy-free way to make every recipe. 163 recipes in 15 chapters.

This page holds the whole book. To keep a copy, use your browser's Print → Save as PDF: each recipe starts on a new page, and the site's menus are left out.

None of these recipes has been kitchen-tested. Ratios follow established low-carb practice and nutrition is computed from USDA FoodData Central; see Method & guides.

Cakes

Almond basbousa

A semolina-free take on the Egyptian syrup cake, made with almond flour and coconut, soaked in a lemon and orange-blossom syrup.

Dairy-free 2.0 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 diamond prep 20 min · cook 35 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Syrup

  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • ½ cup water (118 g)
  • 2 tsp fresh lemon juice (10 g)
  • 1 tsp orange blossom water

Cake

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • ½ cup unsweetened shredded coconut (40 g)
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • ¾ cup unsweetened coconut yogurt (170 g)
  • ¼ cup coconut oil (54 g), melted
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 16 g almonds, blanched halves, one per piece

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.0 g
Total carbohydrate13.9 g
Fibre1.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.0 g
Sugars1.0 g
Fat14.1 g
Protein4.2 g
Energy157 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, orange blossom water, unsweetened coconut yogurt, water.

Method

  1. Make the syrup first so it can cool: simmer the allulose and water for 3 minutes, take off the heat and stir in the lemon juice and orange blossom water. Let it cool completely.
  2. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Grease a 20 × 20 cm (8 × 8 in) pan.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, coconut, baking powder, salt and erythritol together. In another bowl whisk the eggs, coconut yogurt, melted coconut oil and vanilla.
  4. Stir the wet ingredients into the dry until evenly mixed; the batter will be thick. Spread it in the pan and smooth the top.
  5. Score the top into 16 diamonds with a knife dipped in water and press an almond half into the centre of each.
  6. Bake 30–35 minutes, until deep golden and firm in the middle.
  7. Re-cut along the scored lines as soon as it comes out, then pour the cool syrup evenly over the hot cake. Leave at least an hour to soak before serving.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Cool syrup on hot cake (or the reverse) is the rule; both hot makes it soggy.
  • Allulose browns faster than sugar, so the oven runs lower than for a sugar basbousa.

Where it comes from

Syrup cake · Egypt · Middle East & North Africa · by the 1900s

Basbousa is a semolina cake soaked in syrup, eaten across Egypt and the eastern Mediterranean under several names, including revani in Turkey and Greece and namoura in Lebanon. It belongs to the region's long tradition of syrup-soaked sweets, and its exact origin is not recorded. This low-carb version is a modern adaptation that replaces the semolina with almond flour and coconut and is dairy-free as written.

See it on the map

American carrot cake

A moist, warmly spiced almond-flour layer studded with real grated carrot and toasted walnuts, under a thick tangy cream-cheese frosting.

Dairy-free option 4.9 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 30 min · cook 35 min · rest 1 h 30 min

Ingredients

Cake

  • 2½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (240 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp baking soda
  • 2 tsp ground cinnamon
  • ½ tsp ground ginger
  • ½ tsp ground nutmeg
  • ⅛ tsp ground cloves
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 4 large eggs, at room temperature
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g)
  • ⅓ cup avocado oil (74 g)
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 150 g carrot, finely grated, about 1 heaped cup
  • ½ cup walnuts (58 g), toasted and chopped; pecans work equally well

Cream-cheese frosting

  • 225 g full-fat cream cheese, softened
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), softened
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.9 g5.6 g
Total carbohydrate25.2 g26.1 g
Fibre3.3 g3.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose17.0 g17.0 g
Sugars2.7 g2.4 g
Fat32.0 g30.1 g
Protein8.7 g8.4 g
Energy347 kcal336 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Grease a 23 cm (9 in) round cake pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder, baking soda, spices and salt in a bowl, breaking up any lumps.
  3. In a second bowl beat the eggs with the erythritol and allulose for 2 minutes until pale, then whisk in the oil and vanilla.
  4. Fold the dry ingredients into the wet, then fold in the grated carrot and walnuts. The batter will be thick.
  5. Spread into the pan and bake 30–35 minutes, until a skewer comes out clean. Tent with foil after 20 minutes if the top darkens — allulose browns quickly.
  6. Cool 15 minutes in the pan, then turn out onto a rack and cool completely; the crumb is fragile while warm.
  7. For the frosting, beat the cream cheese and butter until smooth, then beat in the powdered erythritol, vanilla and salt. Spread thickly over the top of the cold cake and chill 30 minutes before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.
  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

The cake itself is dairy-free; only the frosting changes. A block-style dairy-free cream cheese gives a softer, slightly less tangy frosting, so keep the cake chilled until serving.

Tips

  • Grate the carrot on the fine side of a box grater: fine shreds melt into the crumb, coarse ones leave wet pockets in a grain-free cake.
  • 150 g of carrot is deliberate — it is enough to taste and see, and keeps a slice at about 5 g net carbs. Doubling it roughly adds 1 g per slice and makes the crumb soggier.

Where it comes from

Carrot cake · United States · North America · 1960s–1970s

Carrots have sweetened puddings and cakes in Europe for centuries, at times when sugar was scarce or costly, and British wartime rationing again promoted them as a sweet ingredient. The layered, spiced carrot cake with cream-cheese frosting became widely popular in the United States in the 1960s and 1970s, and is now a standard of American bakeries.

See it on the map

Black Forest cake

Layers of light cocoa sponge soaked with cherry juice, filled with whipped cream and cherries, and finished with chocolate shavings.

Dairy-free option 5.8 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 50 min · cook 25 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Cocoa sponge

  • 6 large eggs, separated, at room temperature
  • ⅔ cup granulated erythritol (129 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted and cooled
  • 1¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (120 g)
  • ⅓ cup unsweetened cocoa powder (28 g)
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt

Cherries

  • 200 g sweet cherries, pitted, fresh or frozen, halved
  • ¼ cup water (59 g)
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 2 tbsp kirsch (cherry brandy) (28 g), omit for an alcohol-free cake, or use ¼ tsp almond extract

Cream

  • 2½ cups heavy whipping cream (595 g), very cold
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp unflavored powdered gelatin, to stabilise the cream
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)

Finish

  • 30 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), shaved with a vegetable peeler

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.8 g7.7 g
Total carbohydrate24.5 g26.4 g
Fibre2.6 g2.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.0 g16.0 g
Sugars4.3 g4.5 g
Fat31.0 g31.3 g
Protein7.9 g8.1 g
Energy339 kcal342 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Line the bases of two 20 cm (8 in) round pans with parchment and grease the sides.
  2. Whisk the egg yolks with half the erythritol and the vanilla until thick and pale, then whisk in the melted butter.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, cocoa, baking powder and salt together and fold them into the yolks. The batter will be stiff.
  4. Whisk the egg whites to soft peaks, add the remaining erythritol gradually and whisk until firm. Fold into the cocoa batter in three additions.
  5. Divide between the pans and bake 22–25 minutes, until the tops spring back. Cool 10 minutes in the pans, then turn out and cool completely.
  6. Simmer the cherries, water and erythritol for 5 minutes, then take off the heat and stir in the kirsch. Drain, keeping the juice, and let both cool.
  7. Sprinkle the gelatin over the 2 tbsp cold water and leave 5 minutes, then warm it gently until just liquid (about 10 seconds in the microwave) and let it cool slightly. Whip the cream with the powdered erythritol and vanilla to soft peaks, then drizzle in the gelatin while whipping and continue to firm peaks.
  8. Brush the cut side of each sponge generously with the cherry juice. Set one layer on a plate, spread with a third of the cream and scatter with most of the cherries.
  9. Top with the second layer and cover the top and sides with the rest of the cream. Decorate with the remaining cherries and the chocolate shavings.
  10. Refrigerate at least 4 hours so the sponge softens and the cream sets before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter — melted.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the cans overnight and use only the firm cream.

Whipped coconut cream holds well with the gelatin but is softer than dairy cream and tastes of coconut; keep the cake cold and serve it straight from the fridge.

Where it comes from

Layer cake · Black Forest · Western Europe · 1900s

The cake takes its name from the cherry-growing Black Forest region of south-west Germany and its cherry brandy, kirschwasser. Its exact origin is disputed: it is often credited to a confectioner working in Bad Godesberg in the 1910s, while others place it in Tübingen or in the Black Forest itself. It was well known across Germany by the 1930s and became one of the country's most famous cakes after the Second World War.

See it on the map

Chocolate lava cakes

Individual chocolate cakes baked hot and fast so the outside sets while the centre stays molten.

Dairy-free option 4.3 g net carbs Serves 4 · 1 cake prep 15 min · cook 12 min · rest 2 min

Ingredients

  • 6 tbsp unsalted butter (85 g), plus a little for the ramekins
  • 100 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), chopped
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 large egg yolks
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (12 g)
  • 2 tsp unsweetened cocoa powder, for the ramekins

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.3 g4.3 g
Total carbohydrate24.2 g24.2 g
Fibre4.9 g4.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.0 g15.0 g
Sugars0.7 g0.7 g
Fat36.1 g36.8 g
Protein8.7 g8.6 g
Energy384 kcal393 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 220 °C (425 °F). Butter four 170 ml (6 oz) ramekins and dust them with the cocoa, tapping out the excess. Set them on a baking sheet.
  2. Melt the butter and chocolate together over barely simmering water and stir until smooth. Whisk in the powdered erythritol.
  3. In another bowl whisk the eggs, yolks, vanilla and salt for 2 minutes until slightly thickened.
  4. Whisk the warm chocolate into the eggs, then fold in the almond flour.
  5. Divide among the ramekins. (They can be covered and chilled for up to a day at this point; add 2 minutes to the baking time.)
  6. Bake 10–12 minutes, until the edges are firm and the centres still soft and slightly domed.
  7. Rest 1–2 minutes, run a knife round each, and turn out onto plates. Serve straight away.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.85 × the amount).

Coconut oil makes a slightly firmer shell and a looser centre; check the cakes a minute early.

Tips

  • Ovens vary a lot at this heat: bake one test cake first if you are serving guests.

Where it comes from

Chocolate cake · New York City · North America · 1980s

The molten-centred chocolate cake is a restaurant creation of the 1980s, and its origin is disputed. The French chef Michel Bras developed a chocolate 'coulant' with a frozen ganache centre in the early 1980s, while the New York chef Jean-Georges Vongerichten is often credited with popularising the underbaked version in 1987. By the 1990s it was on restaurant menus across the United States.

See it on the map

Gingerbread loaf

A dark, warmly spiced English-style ginger loaf, with a single spoonful of molasses for the true treacly flavour.

Dairy-free option 4.6 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 20 min · cook 50 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2½ tsp ground ginger
  • 1½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ½ tsp ground allspice
  • ¼ tsp ground cloves
  • ¼ tsp ground nutmeg
  • ⅛ tsp ground black pepper
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp baking soda
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), melted
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • 1 tbsp blackstrap or dark molasses (20 g)
  • ¼ cup water (59 g), hot
  • ¼ cup sour cream (58 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.6 g4.6 g
Total carbohydrate18.4 g18.5 g
Fibre2.7 g2.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose11.1 g11.1 g
Sugars2.9 g2.8 g
Fat22.1 g22.1 g
Protein6.6 g6.4 g
Energy247 kcal249 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F). Grease a 23 × 13 cm (9 × 5 in) loaf pan and line it with a parchment sling.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, spices, baking powder, baking soda and salt together.
  3. Stir the molasses into the hot water until dissolved. In another bowl whisk the melted butter with the erythritol and allulose, then whisk in the eggs one at a time, followed by the molasses water, sour cream and vanilla.
  4. Stir the dry ingredients into the wet until smooth.
  5. Scrape into the pan and bake 45–50 minutes, until a skewer comes out clean. Allulose browns quickly, so cover the top loosely with foil after 25 minutes.
  6. Cool in the pan for 20 minutes, then lift out and cool completely. Wrap and keep for a day before slicing; like traditional gingerbread it becomes stickier and better.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.85 × the amount) — melted.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of sour cream.

With coconut oil and coconut yogurt the loaf is just as moist; it firms more in the fridge, so keep it at room temperature.

Tips

  • The allulose is what gives a slightly sticky, treacly crumb; with erythritol alone the loaf is drier.
  • Only 1 tablespoon of molasses goes in: it is sugar, and the limit keeps each slice low-carb. It is there for flavour, not sweetness, so do not increase it; the loaf will be paler than a full treacle gingerbread.

Where it comes from

Gingerbread · England · Northern Europe · medieval

Spiced honey breads were made in medieval Europe, and gingerbread was a popular fair food in England by the late Middle Ages. Once cheap molasses and treacle arrived from the Caribbean sugar trade, English gingerbread developed into the dark, moist, sticky loaf cake known today. This low-carb version keeps a single spoonful of molasses for that flavour.

See it on the map

Italian olive-oil cake

A moist, golden single-layer cake made with fruity olive oil and orange zest, dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 2.7 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 15 min · cook 40 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 4 large eggs, at room temperature
  • ⅔ cup granulated erythritol (129 g)
  • 1 tbsp orange zest
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • ½ cup olive oil (108 g), extra-virgin, fruity rather than peppery
  • ⅓ cup unsweetened almond milk (79 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), for dusting

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.7 g
Total carbohydrate18.8 g
Fibre2.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose13.7 g
Sugars1.3 g
Fat23.1 g
Protein6.9 g
Energy249 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Oil a 23 cm (9 in) round pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. Whisk the eggs, erythritol and zests with an electric mixer for 3–4 minutes, until thick and pale.
  3. With the mixer on low, pour in the olive oil in a thin stream, then the almond milk and vanilla.
  4. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder and salt together and fold them in until just combined.
  5. Pour into the pan and bake 35–40 minutes, until golden and a skewer comes out clean.
  6. Cool 15 minutes in the pan, then turn out onto a rack. Dust with powdered erythritol before serving.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • The oil's flavour comes through, so taste it first: a harsh oil makes a bitter cake.

Where it comes from

Oil cake · Italy · Southern Europe · by the 1900s

In the olive-growing regions of Italy, olive oil has long stood in for butter in home baking, and simple oil-based cakes flavoured with citrus are made from Liguria to Sicily. There is no single recorded origin; the cake is a family of regional home recipes rather than a named invention. This version is dairy-free as written.

See it on the map

Lamingtons

Squares of light almond sponge dipped in a thin chocolate coating and rolled in unsweetened coconut.

Dairy-free option 2.8 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 45 min · cook 25 min · rest 2 h

Ingredients

Sponge

  • ⅓ cup unsalted butter (75 g), softened
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 4 large eggs, at room temperature
  • 1½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1¾ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (168 g)
  • 3 tbsp coconut flour (21 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ cup unsweetened almond milk (60 g)

Chocolate coating

  • ¾ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (90 g)
  • ⅓ cup unsweetened cocoa powder (28 g)
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g)
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)

To coat

  • 2 cups unsweetened shredded coconut (160 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.8 g2.8 g
Total carbohydrate18.2 g18.2 g
Fibre3.8 g3.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose11.6 g11.6 g
Sugars1.6 g1.6 g
Fat18.9 g18.8 g
Protein5.2 g5.2 g
Energy207 kcal207 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Grease a 20 × 20 cm (8 × 8 in) pan and line it with a parchment sling.
  2. Beat the butter and erythritol until light. Beat in the eggs one at a time, then the vanilla.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder and salt together and fold in alternately with the almond milk.
  4. Spread in the pan and bake 22–25 minutes, until golden and springy. Cool completely, then wrap and freeze for 1 hour; a firm, cold sponge is much easier to dip.
  5. For the coating, warm the powdered erythritol, cocoa, butter and almond milk in a small pan, whisking until smooth and glossy. Keep it just warm.
  6. Trim the edges and cut the cake into 16 squares. Put the coconut in a shallow dish.
  7. Using two forks, dip each square quickly into the chocolate, let the excess drip off, then roll it in coconut. Set on a rack.
  8. Leave for an hour to set. Store in an airtight box.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter works in both the sponge and the coating with little difference; the coating is slightly thinner, so you may want a second quick dip.

Tips

  • Work quickly when dipping: almond sponge soaks up the coating and can fall apart if it sits in it.

Where it comes from

Sponge cake · Queensland · Oceania · around 1900

Lamingtons are named after Lord Lamington, Governor of Queensland from 1896 to 1901, and the earliest recipes appear in Australian publications in the early 1900s. Who first made them is disputed: the most common story credits a cook at Government House in Brisbane, and New Zealand has also laid claim to the cake. They became a national favourite and a classic fundraising bake in Australia.

Lemon drizzle cake

A tender lemon loaf soaked while warm with a sharp lemon syrup that dries to a thin, crackly crust.

Dairy-free option 3.1 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 20 min · cook 45 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

Loaf

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 4 large eggs, at room temperature
  • 2 tbsp lemon zest (12 g), from about 2 lemons
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ cup sour cream (58 g)

Drizzle

  • 3 tbsp fresh lemon juice (45 g)
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.1 g3.1 g
Total carbohydrate19.2 g19.3 g
Fibre2.5 g2.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose13.6 g13.6 g
Sugars1.6 g1.4 g
Fat22.5 g21.9 g
Protein7.2 g7.0 g
Energy246 kcal243 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 170 °C (340 °F). Grease a 23 × 13 cm (9 × 5 in) loaf pan and line it with a parchment sling.
  2. Beat the butter, erythritol and lemon zest until light and fluffy. Beat in the eggs one at a time, then the vanilla.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder and salt together, fold half into the batter, then the sour cream, then the rest.
  4. Scrape into the pan and bake 40–45 minutes, until a skewer comes out clean. Cover loosely with foil after 25 minutes if the top is browning fast.
  5. While the loaf bakes, stir the lemon juice and powdered erythritol together; the sweetener will not fully dissolve, which is what gives the crunchy top.
  6. As soon as the loaf comes out, prick it all over with a skewer and spoon the drizzle slowly over it. Leave in the pan until completely cool, then lift out.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of sour cream.

With dairy-free butter and coconut yogurt the loaf is slightly moister and less rich; it slices best the next day.

Tips

  • Let the loaf cool fully in the pan: warm almond cake breaks if you lift it out early.

Where it comes from

Loaf cake · Britain · Northern Europe · late 1900s

Lemon-flavoured pound cakes and seed cakes have a long history in British baking, but the drizzle cake, soaked with sharp lemon syrup straight from the oven, became a household standard in the later twentieth century. It is now one of the most popular cakes in Britain, a staple of cafés, bake sales and home kitchens.

Piedmontese hazelnut cake (torta di nocciole)

A plain, fragrant single-layer cake made almost entirely of toasted ground hazelnuts, eggs and a little butter.

Dairy-free option 1.8 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 20 min · cook 35 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 2 cups hazelnut flour (ground hazelnuts) (192 g), or toasted hazelnuts ground fine
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 4 large eggs, separated, at room temperature
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ⅓ cup unsalted butter (75 g), melted and cooled
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 3 tbsp unsweetened almond milk (45 g)
  • 30 g hazelnuts, roughly chopped, for the top
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), for dusting

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.8 g1.8 g
Total carbohydrate14.6 g14.6 g
Fibre2.4 g2.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.4 g10.4 g
Sugars1.0 g1.0 g
Fat21.6 g22.9 g
Protein5.7 g5.6 g
Energy226 kcal239 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 170 °C (340 °F). Grease a 23 cm (9 in) round pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. If using hazelnut flour, toast it in a dry pan over medium heat, stirring, until it smells nutty, about 4 minutes; tip it out to cool. Whisk in the baking powder and salt.
  3. Whisk the yolks with half the erythritol until pale and thick, then whisk in the melted butter, vanilla and almond milk. Stir in the hazelnut mixture.
  4. Whisk the whites to soft peaks, add the rest of the erythritol and whisk until firm. Fold into the hazelnut batter in three additions.
  5. Spread in the pan, scatter the chopped hazelnuts over and bake 30–35 minutes, until a skewer comes out clean.
  6. Cool in the pan 15 minutes, then turn out and cool on a rack. Dust with powdered erythritol to serve.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter — melted; or a mild olive oil.

Made with coconut oil or olive oil the cake is just as moist; olive oil keeps it softer in the fridge, where coconut oil firms up.

Tips

  • Toasting the nuts is what gives the cake its flavour; do not skip it.

Where it comes from

Nut cake · Langhe, Piedmont · Southern Europe · by the 1900s

The Langhe hills of southern Piedmont are known for the Tonda Gentile hazelnut, and a simple cake of ground hazelnuts is a long-standing farmhouse dessert there; its exact date of origin is not recorded. The same nuts later underpinned Piedmont's gianduja chocolate and the region's hazelnut confectionery industry.

Pound cake

A plain, close-textured butter loaf built on the old equal-weights idea, with almond flour carrying the structure.

Dairy-free option 2.7 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 20 min · cook 1 h · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 225 g unsalted butter, softened
  • 200 g granulated erythritol
  • 4 large eggs, at room temperature
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • 225 g almond flour (blanched, finely ground)
  • 20 g coconut flour
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ cup sour cream (58 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.7 g2.6 g
Total carbohydrate21.8 g21.8 g
Fibre2.4 g2.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.7 g16.7 g
Sugars1.4 g1.3 g
Fat27.8 g27.1 g
Protein6.7 g6.5 g
Energy291 kcal288 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Grease a 23 × 13 cm (9 × 5 in) loaf pan and line it with a parchment sling.
  2. Beat the butter and erythritol for 4–5 minutes, until very pale and fluffy; this creaming is what lifts the cake.
  3. Beat in the eggs one at a time, scraping the bowl after each, then the vanilla and lemon zest.
  4. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder and salt together. Fold half into the batter, then the sour cream, then the rest.
  5. Scrape into the pan, level, and bake 55–60 minutes, until golden and a skewer comes out clean. Cover with foil for the last 20 minutes if it browns quickly.
  6. Cool in the pan 20 minutes, then lift out and cool completely before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of sour cream.

Use a firm stick-style dairy-free butter; a soft tub spread will not cream and the loaf turns dense and greasy.

Tips

  • Erythritol can recrystallise on standing; a powdered sweetener in the batter gives a smoother crumb the next day.

Where it comes from

Butter cake · England · Northern Europe · 1700s

Pound cake is named for its original proportions: a pound each of flour, butter, sugar and eggs. Recipes appear in English cookbooks by the eighteenth century, and the cake travelled to the American colonies, where it became a Southern classic. Later versions lightened it with chemical leaveners and changed the proportions, but the name stuck.

Rüeblitorte (Swiss carrot cake)

A light, nutty Swiss sponge made mostly of ground almonds and grated carrot, lifted by whisked egg whites and finished with a thin lemon glaze.

Dairy-free 3.7 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 30 min · cook 45 min · rest 2 h

Ingredients

Cake

  • 5 large egg yolks
  • ¾ cup granulated erythritol (144 g)
  • 2 tsp lemon zest
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 1 tbsp kirsch (cherry brandy) (14 g), optional
  • 250 g almond flour (blanched, finely ground)
  • 200 g carrot, finely grated
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 5 large egg whites

Glaze

  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • 1½ tbsp fresh lemon juice (22 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.7 g
Total carbohydrate23.3 g
Fibre2.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose17.0 g
Sugars2.0 g
Fat12.9 g
Protein7.3 g
Energy167 kcal

Contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Grease a 24 cm (9½ in) springform pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. Beat the egg yolks with the erythritol for 3–4 minutes until thick and pale. Beat in the lemon zest, lemon juice and kirsch.
  3. Mix the almond flour, baking powder, cinnamon and salt, then stir them and the grated carrot into the yolk mixture. It will be stiff.
  4. In a clean bowl whisk the egg whites to firm, glossy peaks. Stir a quarter into the batter to loosen it, then fold in the rest in two additions, keeping as much air as you can.
  5. Scrape into the pan and bake 40–45 minutes, until risen, golden and a skewer comes out clean. Cover loosely with foil if it browns too fast.
  6. Cool in the pan 10 minutes, release the side and cool completely.
  7. Stir the powdered erythritol with the lemon juice to a thick, pourable glaze and spread it over the top. Let it set before slicing.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • The cake keeps and improves for 2–3 days, well wrapped; the carrot keeps it moist.
  • Leave out the kirsch for children or swap it for a few drops of almond extract.

Where it comes from

Carrot cake · Aargau, Switzerland · Western Europe · by the 1900s

Rüebli is the Swiss German word for carrot, and the almond-and-carrot Rüeblitorte is closely associated with the canton of Aargau, sometimes called the Rüeblikanton. Made with ground nuts and little or no flour, it is a traditional birthday and Easter cake across German-speaking Switzerland, often decorated with small marzipan carrots.

See it on the map

Sachertorte

A dense, dark almond-and-chocolate cake split and spread with a thin layer of fruit jam, then sealed under a glossy chocolate glaze.

Dairy-free option 5.2 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 40 min · cook 45 min · rest 2 h

Ingredients

Cake

  • 115 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), chopped
  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • 6 large egg yolks
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 6 large egg whites
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • 1¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (120 g)
  • 2 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (11 g)
  • 1 tsp baking powder

Jam

  • 150 g peach, peeled and finely chopped; stands in for the traditional apricot
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 2 tsp fresh lemon juice (10 g)
  • 1 tbsp chia seeds (12 g)

Glaze

  • 85 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), finely chopped
  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g)
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g)
  • 1 tsp coconut oil

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.2 g5.6 g
Total carbohydrate24.2 g24.6 g
Fibre4.7 g4.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose14.3 g14.3 g
Sugars2.2 g2.3 g
Fat28.0 g27.7 g
Protein8.3 g8.2 g
Energy311 kcal310 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 170 °C (340 °F). Grease a 23 cm (9 in) springform pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. Melt the chocolate gently over barely simmering water and let it cool until just warm.
  3. Beat the butter and powdered erythritol until pale, then beat in the yolks one at a time, followed by the vanilla and the melted chocolate.
  4. In a clean bowl whisk the egg whites with the salt to soft peaks, then add the granulated erythritol a spoonful at a time and whisk to firm, glossy peaks.
  5. Whisk the almond flour, cocoa and baking powder together. Fold a third of the whites into the chocolate mixture to loosen it, then fold in the dry mix and the rest of the whites in two additions, keeping as much air as you can.
  6. Spread into the pan and bake 40–45 minutes, until a skewer comes out with a few moist crumbs. Cool in the pan for 15 minutes, then turn out onto a rack and cool completely.
  7. For the jam, simmer the peach, erythritol and lemon juice for 8–10 minutes, mashing as it softens. Stir in the chia seeds and let it thicken for 10 minutes off the heat.
  8. Split the cake horizontally into two layers. Spread the jam on the bottom layer, replace the top, and brush any remaining jam thinly over the top and sides.
  9. For the glaze, heat the cream until steaming, pour it over the chocolate, wait 2 minutes, then stir in the powdered erythritol and coconut oil until smooth.
  10. Let the glaze cool until it coats a spoon thickly, then pour it over the centre of the cake and use a palette knife to push it over the edges in one or two strokes. Leave at room temperature for 2 hours to set before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With dairy-free butter and coconut cream the cake is indistinguishable, but the glaze sets a little softer and tastes faintly of coconut; chill it 20 minutes if the kitchen is warm.

Tips

  • A thin jam layer is the point: too much makes the layers slide.
  • Serve with unsweetened whipped cream, as is done in Vienna, to offset the richness.

Where it comes from

Chocolate torte · Vienna · Western Europe · 1832

The cake is generally dated to 1832, when the young apprentice Franz Sacher is said to have made a dessert for the household of the Austrian statesman Metternich. His son Eduard later refined it, and it became the signature of the Hotel Sacher in Vienna. A long court dispute in the twentieth century between the hotel and the Demel bakery over who could call theirs the original ended with the hotel keeping the name 'Original Sacher-Torte'.

See it on the map

Tarta de Santiago

The Galician almond cake: ground almonds, eggs and lemon zest, baked flat and dusted with the cross of St James.

Dairy-free 2.4 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 15 min · cook 35 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 250 g almond flour (blanched, finely ground)
  • 180 g granulated erythritol
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • ¼ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 4 large eggs
  • ¼ tsp almond extract, optional
  • 1 tsp olive oil, for the pan
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g), for dusting

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.4 g
Total carbohydrate24.6 g
Fibre2.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose19.6 g
Sugars1.3 g
Fat15.5 g
Protein7.9 g
Energy184 kcal

Contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Oil a 23 cm (9 in) round pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. Mix the almond flour, erythritol, lemon zest, cinnamon and salt in a bowl.
  3. In another bowl whisk the eggs for about 2 minutes, until frothy and slightly paler, then whisk in the almond extract.
  4. Stir the almond mixture into the eggs until evenly combined; it will be a thick, spoonable batter.
  5. Spread in the pan and bake 30–35 minutes, until golden brown and just firm in the centre. Cool completely in the pan.
  6. Cut a cross of St James from paper, lay it in the centre of the cake, dust heavily with powdered erythritol and lift the stencil off carefully.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • The cake keeps well for 3–4 days at room temperature and tastes better on day two.
  • Without the stencil, a plain dusting is just as traditional at home.

Where it comes from

Almond cake · Santiago de Compostela · Southern Europe · 1800s

The tarta de Santiago takes its name from the pilgrimage city of Santiago de Compostela and its patron, St James. Almond cakes of this kind are recorded in Galicia by the nineteenth century, and the powdered-sugar cross of the Order of Santiago on top became the standard decoration in the twentieth. Made only of almonds, eggs and sugar, it is naturally flourless and dairy-free; it now carries protected geographical status in the EU.

Torta caprese

A flourless chocolate and almond cake from Capri with a crisp top and a soft, almost fudgy middle.

Dairy-free option 3.4 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 25 min · cook 40 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 140 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), chopped
  • ⅔ cup unsalted butter (152 g), cubed
  • 200 g almonds, blanched, toasted and finely ground
  • 2 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (11 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 5 large eggs, separated, at room temperature
  • ¾ cup granulated erythritol (144 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), for dusting

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.4 g3.4 g
Total carbohydrate20.5 g20.5 g
Fibre4.4 g4.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.7 g12.7 g
Sugars1.0 g1.0 g
Fat26.5 g26.9 g
Protein7.9 g7.8 g
Energy289 kcal294 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 170 °C (340 °F). Grease a 23 cm (9 in) springform pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. Melt the chocolate and butter together over barely simmering water, stir until smooth and let it cool until just warm.
  3. Mix the ground almonds, cocoa and salt.
  4. Whisk the yolks with half the erythritol until pale and thick, then stir in the chocolate and vanilla, followed by the almond mixture.
  5. Whisk the whites to soft peaks, add the remaining erythritol and whisk to firm peaks. Fold them into the chocolate base in three additions; the first will be hard work, the last should be gentle.
  6. Scrape into the pan and bake 35–40 minutes, until the top is crisp and cracked and a skewer in the centre comes out with moist crumbs.
  7. Cool completely in the pan; the cake sinks slightly as it cools. Unmould and dust with powdered erythritol.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.85 × the amount) — refined coconut oil has no coconut taste; use a little less because it is all fat.

With coconut oil the cake is a touch firmer and more truffle-like once cold; serve it at room temperature.

Tips

  • Grinding toasted almonds yourself gives the authentic coarse texture; almond flour also works but makes a finer, softer cake.

Where it comes from

Chocolate cake · Capri · Southern Europe · 1900s

Torta caprese is named after the island of Capri in the Bay of Naples. A popular story says a baker forgot to add the flour to an almond and chocolate cake and the result was a success; the tale is a legend and the cake's exact origin is not documented. It is naturally flourless and is widely eaten around Naples and the Amalfi Coast.

See it on the map

Tres leches cake

A light almond sponge soaked through with three plant milks and topped with whipped coconut cream and a dusting of cinnamon.

Dairy-free 5.0 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 square prep 30 min · cook 30 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

Sponge

  • 5 large eggs, separated, at room temperature
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 1½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ cup unsweetened almond milk (60 g)
  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp cream of tartar

Three milks

  • 1 cup unsweetened almond milk (240 g)
  • 1 cup full-fat coconut milk (canned) (226 g)
  • ½ cup coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (120 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Topping

  • 1¼ cups coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (300 g), chilled overnight, firm part only
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs5.0 g
Total carbohydrate22.0 g
Fibre1.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.3 g
Sugars2.3 g
Fat24.9 g
Protein7.1 g
Energy269 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Grease a 23 × 33 cm (9 × 13 in) baking dish; glass or ceramic is fine.
  2. Whisk the yolks with half the erythritol until thick and pale, then whisk in the vanilla and almond milk.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder and salt together and fold into the yolks.
  4. Whisk the whites with the cream of tartar to soft peaks, add the rest of the erythritol and whisk to firm peaks. Fold into the batter in three additions.
  5. Spread in the dish and bake 25–30 minutes, until golden and springy. Cool for 15 minutes in the dish.
  6. Warm the three milks with the allulose and vanilla just until the allulose dissolves; let it cool to lukewarm.
  7. Prick the cake all over with a fork, right to the bottom. Pour the milk mixture over slowly in three or four rounds, letting each soak in.
  8. Cover and refrigerate at least 6 hours, or overnight, so the milk is fully absorbed.
  9. Whip the coconut cream with the powdered erythritol until thick, spread over the cake and dust with cinnamon. Serve cold, in squares.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Allulose is used in the soak because it dissolves fully and does not crystallise in the fridge.
  • The cake will look flooded at first; it absorbs the milk overnight.

Where it comes from

Soaked sponge cake · Mexico · Latin America & Caribbean · 1900s

Sponge cakes soaked in sweet liquid have a long European history, but tres leches as known today is a twentieth-century dish of Mexico and Central America. Its origin is disputed: Mexico and Nicaragua both claim it, and its spread is widely linked to canned evaporated and condensed milk sold in the region, possibly through recipes printed on the cans. This version replaces the dairy milks with almond and coconut milks, so it is dairy-free as written.

See it on the map

Two buttery almond sponges sandwiched with a quick raspberry chia jam and softly whipped cream, dusted on top.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Grease two 20 cm (8 in) round pans and line the bases with parchment.
  2. Simmer the raspberries and erythritol for 5 minutes, mashing them, then stir in the chia seeds and leave to thicken while you make the cake.
  3. Beat the butter and erythritol for 3 minutes until light. Beat in the eggs one at a time, then the vanilla.
  4. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder and salt together and fold them in, then fold in the almond milk to a soft dropping consistency.
  5. Divide between the pans, level, and bake 20–25 minutes, until golden and springy. Cool 10 minutes, then turn out onto a rack and cool completely; almond sponges are fragile while warm.
  6. Whip the cream with 1 tbsp powdered erythritol to soft peaks.
  7. Spread the jam over one sponge, then the cream, and set the second sponge on top. Dust with the remaining powdered erythritol.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chilled, firm part only.

Dairy-free butter gives a slightly less rich crumb, and whipped coconut cream is softer, so fill the cake shortly before serving.

Tips

  • For the classic jam-only version, leave out the cream and use a little more jam.

Where it comes from

Sponge cake · England · Northern Europe · 1800s

The cake is named after Queen Victoria, who is said to have enjoyed sponge cake with her afternoon tea; the link to the queen herself is traditional rather than documented. Reliable baking powder from the mid-nineteenth century made a light, raised butter sponge practical, and a jam-filled sandwich of two such layers became a fixture of the English tea table.

See it on the map

Cheesecakes

Basque burnt cheesecake

A crustless cheesecake baked very hot so the outside caramelises nearly black while the middle stays soft and creamy.

Dairy-free option 5.3 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 15 min · cook 40 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • 900 g full-fat cream cheese, at room temperature
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 5 large eggs, at room temperature
  • 1½ cups heavy whipping cream (357 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.3 g9.1 g
Total carbohydrate21.5 g26.0 g
Fibre0.2 g0.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.0 g16.0 g
Sugars3.9 g2.7 g
Fat38.6 g31.2 g
Protein8.2 g7.5 g
Energy402 kcal354 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 220 °C (425 °F). Crumple two large sheets of parchment, flatten them out and use them to line a 23 cm (9 in) springform pan, pleating them so they rise 5 cm (2 in) above the rim.
  2. Beat the cream cheese, allulose, erythritol and salt on low speed until completely smooth.
  3. Beat in the eggs one at a time, then the cream and vanilla. Sift the coconut flour over and mix until just smooth.
  4. Pour into the pan and bake 35–40 minutes, until the top is deep brown, almost black in places, and the centre wobbles a lot when you nudge the pan.
  5. Cool to room temperature in the pan; it will sink and crack, which is expected. Refrigerate at least 4 hours, then bring back to room temperature for 30 minutes before serving.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese — block-style.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

The dairy-free version browns and sets well but is softer and less tangy; chill it overnight and serve it cold rather than at room temperature.

Tips

  • Allulose caramelises like sugar and gives the dark top; with erythritol alone the cake stays pale.
  • Do not use a water bath and do not wait for the centre to set; it firms as it cools.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · San Sebastián · Southern Europe · 1990

The burnt cheesecake comes from La Viña, a bar in the old town of San Sebastián in the Spanish Basque Country, where it was first served in 1990. Baked crustless in a very hot oven, it was deliberately rustic. It spread worldwide in the late 2010s and is now often called 'Basque cheesecake'.

Chocolate cheesecake

A dense, dark chocolate cheesecake on a cocoa-almond crust, baked slowly in a water bath.

Dairy-free option 5.8 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 thin slice prep 30 min · cook 1 h 15 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (120 g)
  • 3 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (16 g)
  • 3 tbsp granulated erythritol (36 g)
  • 5 tbsp unsalted butter (71 g), melted
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Filling

  • 115 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), chopped
  • 900 g full-fat cream cheese, at room temperature
  • ¾ cup granulated erythritol (144 g)
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • 3 tbsp Dutch-process cocoa powder (16 g)
  • ½ cup sour cream (115 g)
  • 4 large eggs
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp instant espresso powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.8 g7.7 g
Total carbohydrate21.5 g24.2 g
Fibre2.6 g3.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose13.1 g13.1 g
Sugars2.9 g1.8 g
Fat33.4 g27.7 g
Protein8.2 g7.4 g
Energy355 kcal320 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F). Line the base of a 23 cm (9 in) springform pan with parchment.
  2. Mix the crust ingredients, press firmly into the pan and bake 10 minutes. Let it cool.
  3. Melt the chocolate gently over barely simmering water and let it cool until just warm.
  4. Beat the cream cheese, both sweeteners and the cocoa on low speed until completely smooth. Beat in the warm chocolate, then the sour cream.
  5. Add the eggs one at a time, then the vanilla, espresso powder and salt, mixing on low only until combined.
  6. Pour onto the crust, wrap the outside of the pan in foil and set it in a roasting tin. Pour in hot water to come halfway up the side.
  7. Bake 65–75 minutes, until the edge is set and the centre wobbles slightly. Turn off the oven and leave the cake inside with the door ajar for an hour.
  8. Cool, then refrigerate at least 8 hours before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.85 × the amount) — melted.
  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese — block-style.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of sour cream.

Chocolate hides the coconut and cashew flavours well, and the melted chocolate helps the dairy-free version set firmer than most.

Tips

  • Cut it into thin slices with a hot, dry knife; it is very rich.
  • Make sure the chocolate is only warm when added; hot chocolate scrambles the eggs, cold chocolate seizes into flecks.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · New York City · North America · 1900s

Chocolate cheesecake is a twentieth-century American variation on the cream-cheese cheesecake that grew up around New York delicatessens. There is no single recorded inventor; flavoured cheesecakes became common in American bakeries and restaurants through the second half of the century.

German Käsekuchen

A tall, lightly tangy German curd-cheese cake on a short almond crust, made here with a cream cheese and yogurt blend in place of quark.

Dairy-free option 4.4 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 30 min · cook 1 h 10 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (120 g)
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg yolk
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Filling

  • 500 g plain whole-milk Greek yogurt, strained overnight in a sieve lined with a cloth
  • 250 g full-fat cream cheese, at room temperature
  • ⅔ cup granulated erythritol (129 g)
  • 4 large eggs, separated
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted and cooled
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.4 g5.1 g
Total carbohydrate18.5 g20.3 g
Fibre1.4 g2.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.7 g12.7 g
Sugars3.3 g1.7 g
Fat24.3 g25.5 g
Protein9.8 g6.2 g
Energy278 kcal288 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Line the base of a 23 cm (9 in) springform pan with parchment.
  2. Rub the butter into the almond flour, erythritol and salt, then mix in the yolk to make a soft dough. Press it over the base and 2 cm (¾ in) up the sides. Bake 10 minutes and let cool. Lower the oven to 160 °C (325 °F).
  3. Beat the strained yogurt, cream cheese and erythritol until smooth. Beat in the yolks, melted butter, zest, juice and vanilla, then sift in the coconut flour and mix until smooth.
  4. Whisk the whites to soft peaks and fold them into the cheese mixture.
  5. Pour into the crust and bake 60–70 minutes, until golden and just set, with a slight wobble in the centre. If the top rises and threatens to crack, run a knife round the edge after 15 minutes.
  6. Turn off the oven and leave the cake inside with the door ajar for 30 minutes, then cool completely and chill at least 4 hours.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of plain whole-milk Greek yogurt — a thick, strained-style one.
  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.

The dairy-free version is softer and sweeter-tasting, with less of quark's tang; add an extra tablespoon of lemon juice and chill it overnight.

Tips

  • If you can find quark, use 750 g of it, well drained, in place of the yogurt and cream cheese.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · Germany · Western Europe · medieval

Tarts of fresh curd cheese were baked in German-speaking lands by the late Middle Ages, and Käsekuchen made with quark remains one of the most common cakes in German bakeries. It is lighter and tangier than an American cream-cheese cake, and is usually baked on a shortcrust base, sometimes with raisins or as a no-crust variant.

Italian ricotta cheesecake

A lighter, slightly grainy crustless cheesecake of ricotta flavoured with lemon and orange zest.

Dairy-free option 6.0 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 20 min · cook 1 h · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • 900 g whole-milk ricotta, drained in a sieve for 1 hour
  • ⅔ cup granulated erythritol (129 g)
  • 5 large eggs
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • 2 tsp orange zest
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp unsalted butter, for the pan
  • 2 tbsp almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (12 g), for the pan

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs6.0 g7.3 g
Total carbohydrate17.1 g19.2 g
Fibre0.4 g1.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.7 g10.7 g
Sugars0.5 g1.8 g
Fat10.5 g21.7 g
Protein8.6 g6.7 g
Energy158 kcal264 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 170 °C (340 °F). Butter a 23 cm (9 in) springform pan and dust it with the almond flour.
  2. Beat the drained ricotta and erythritol until as smooth as you can get it, about 2 minutes.
  3. Beat in the eggs one at a time, then the zests and vanilla. Sift in the coconut flour and salt and mix just until combined.
  4. Pour into the pan and bake 55–60 minutes, until golden at the edges and set with a slight wobble in the middle.
  5. Turn off the oven and leave the cake inside with the door ajar for 30 minutes, then cool fully and chill at least 4 hours.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of whole-milk ricotta — add 2 tbsp almond milk to loosen it.
  • Use olive oil instead of unsalted butter.

With dairy-free cream cheese the cake is smoother and denser, closer to a New York style than to the grainy ricotta original.

Tips

  • Draining the ricotta is essential; wet ricotta gives a soggy, weeping cake.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · Rome · Southern Europe · ancient

Cakes of fresh cheese were made in ancient Rome; Cato the Elder's farming treatise of the second century BC gives recipes such as libum and placenta that combine cheese with flour and honey. Ricotta-based baked desserts remain common across Italy, especially around Easter, and the ricotta cheesecake is their modern, cake-shaped form.

Japanese cotton cheesecake

A tall, airy soufflé-style cheesecake lightened with whipped egg whites and baked gently in a water bath.

Dairy-free option 3.0 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 35 min · cook 1 h 15 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

  • 250 g full-fat cream cheese, at room temperature
  • 3 tbsp unsalted butter (43 g)
  • ⅓ cup whole milk (81 g)
  • 5 large egg yolks
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 3 tbsp almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (18 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 5 large egg whites, cold
  • ½ tsp cream of tartar
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.0 g3.5 g
Total carbohydrate9.6 g10.5 g
Fibre0.6 g0.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g6.0 g
Sugars1.9 g1.1 g
Fat15.7 g13.1 g
Protein5.6 g5.1 g
Energy178 kcal159 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 160 °C (320 °F). Line the base and sides of an 18 cm (7 in) round pan with parchment, with the side collar rising 5 cm (2 in) above the rim. If it is a springform, wrap the outside in two layers of foil.
  2. Melt the cream cheese, butter and milk together in a bowl over simmering water, whisking until smooth. Take off the heat and let it cool for 5 minutes.
  3. Whisk in the yolks one at a time, then the lemon juice and vanilla. Sift in the coconut flour, almond flour and salt and whisk until smooth; strain the mixture if it has lumps.
  4. Whisk the egg whites with the cream of tartar until foamy, then add the powdered erythritol a spoonful at a time and whisk to soft, droopy peaks, not stiff ones; over-whipped whites crack the cake.
  5. Fold a third of the whites into the cheese base with a whisk, then fold in the rest gently with a spatula.
  6. Pour into the pan, tap it twice on the counter to release large bubbles, and set it in a roasting tin. Pour in hot water to come 2.5 cm (1 in) up the side.
  7. Bake 20 minutes at 160 °C (320 °F), then lower to 140 °C (285 °F) and bake 50–55 minutes more, until risen, golden and springy.
  8. Turn the oven off and leave the cake inside with the door slightly open for 30 minutes so it does not collapse. Cool, then chill at least 3 hours.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.
  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use unsweetened almond milk instead of whole milk.

The dairy-free version rises nearly as well but shrinks more as it cools and is less tangy; it is best eaten the day it is made.

Tips

  • Low-carb flours give less support than wheat, so expect some sinking; a gentle oven and slow cooling help most.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · Japan · East Asia · 1960s

The light, soufflé-style cheesecake is a Japanese adaptation of European cheesecakes. It is often credited to a Japanese pastry chef who encountered German Käsekuchen on a trip abroad in the 1960s, though the exact story is not well documented. It became a bakery staple in Japan and later spread across East Asia, where it is known as cotton or soufflé cheesecake.

See it on the map

New York cheesecake

Dense, tall and tangy, baked low and slow on an almond-flour crust so the top stays smooth.

Dairy-free option 5.9 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 30 min · cook 1 h 15 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 5 tbsp unsalted butter (71 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Filling

  • 900 g full-fat cream cheese, at room temperature
  • ¾ cup granulated erythritol (144 g)
  • ½ cup sour cream (115 g)
  • 4 large eggs
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.9 g8.5 g
Total carbohydrate21.1 g24.6 g
Fibre1.2 g2.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose14.0 g14.0 g
Sugars3.9 g2.3 g
Fat40.4 g32.6 g
Protein9.6 g8.5 g
Energy424 kcal374 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F). Line the base of a 23 cm (9 in) springform pan with parchment.
  2. Stir the crust ingredients together, press firmly into the pan and bake 10 minutes. Let it cool while you make the filling.
  3. Beat the cream cheese and erythritol on low speed until completely smooth, scraping the bowl twice. Low speed keeps air out, which is what cracks the top.
  4. Beat in the sour cream, then the eggs one at a time, then the zest, juice and vanilla, stopping as soon as each is mixed in.
  5. Pour onto the crust. Wrap the outside of the pan in foil and set it in a roasting tin; pour in hot water to come halfway up the pan.
  6. Bake 70–75 minutes, until the edge is set and the centre still wobbles. Turn the oven off, prop the door open and leave the cake inside for an hour.
  7. Run a knife round the edge, cool to room temperature, then refrigerate at least 8 hours before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of sour cream.

Use a firm, block-style dairy-free cream cheese, not a tub spread, and chill overnight: the dairy-free version sets softer.

Tips

  • Erythritol can recrystallise in the fridge; a powdered sweetener or allulose avoids the slight crunch.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · New York City · North America · early 1900s

Cheese-and-honey cakes were baked in ancient Greece and Rome, and curd tarts spread across medieval Europe. The New York style followed the arrival of cream cheese, first sold in New York State in the 1870s; by the early twentieth century the city's delicatessens were known for a tall, dense cake made almost entirely of it.

No-bake lemon cheesecake

A cool, sharp lemon cheesecake set with gelatin on a press-in almond crust, with no oven needed for the filling.

Dairy-free option 4.8 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 30 min · cook 10 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (120 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Filling

  • ¼ cup water (59 g), cold
  • 2½ tsp unflavored powdered gelatin
  • ⅓ cup fresh lemon juice (81 g)
  • 450 g full-fat cream cheese, at room temperature
  • ⅔ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (80 g)
  • 1½ tbsp lemon zest
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g), cold

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.8 g7.3 g
Total carbohydrate16.6 g19.5 g
Fibre1.3 g1.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.4 g10.4 g
Sugars3.2 g2.5 g
Fat35.0 g30.7 g
Protein6.7 g6.2 g
Energy358 kcal331 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Line the base of a 20 cm (8 in) springform pan with parchment.
  2. Mix the crust ingredients, press firmly into the pan and bake 8–10 minutes until lightly golden. Cool completely. (Skip the baking for a fully no-bake cake; the crust will be softer.)
  3. Sprinkle the gelatin over the cold water and leave 5 minutes to bloom. Warm the lemon juice until hot but not boiling, take off the heat and stir in the bloomed gelatin until fully dissolved. Let it cool to lukewarm.
  4. Beat the cream cheese, powdered erythritol, zest and vanilla until smooth, then beat in the lemon-gelatin mixture in a slow stream.
  5. Whip the cream to soft peaks and fold it into the cheese mixture.
  6. Spread over the crust, smooth the top, cover and refrigerate at least 6 hours, until set firm.
  7. Run a hot knife round the edge before releasing the pan.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.85 × the amount) — melted.
  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese — block-style.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chilled, firm part only.

Because gelatin does the setting, the dairy-free version sets almost as firmly; it tastes faintly of coconut, which suits the lemon.

Tips

  • Make sure the gelatin mixture is lukewarm, not cold, when it goes in, or it sets in strings.
  • Use powdered sweetener here: granules do not dissolve in an unbaked filling.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · United States · North America · mid-1900s

Unbaked cheesecakes set with gelatin, sometimes called refrigerator or icebox cheesecakes, spread in the twentieth century once home refrigerators and packaged powdered gelatin became common in the United States and Britain. They became a staple of home entertaining, especially in summer; no single origin is recorded.

Where it comes from

Cheesecake · New England · North America · late 1900s

Pumpkin pie has been an American autumn dessert since colonial New England and a Thanksgiving fixture since the nineteenth century. Combining its spiced pumpkin custard with cream-cheese cheesecake is a twentieth-century American idea, and by the late 1900s pumpkin cheesecake was a common holiday alternative to the pie.

Cookies & biscuits

Almond biscotti (cantucci)

Twice-baked Tuscan almond biscotti with whole almonds and a hint of orange, made without butter as in Prato.

Dairy-free 1.2 g net carbs Serves 24 · 1 biscotto prep 20 min · cook 45 min · rest 20 min

Ingredients

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ½ tsp xanthan gum
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ½ tsp anise seed, lightly crushed; optional
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp orange zest
  • ¾ cup almonds (107 g), whole, skin on

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.2 g
Total carbohydrate6.6 g
Fibre1.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.0 g
Sugars0.6 g
Fat6.8 g
Protein3.2 g
Energy81 kcal

Contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, erythritol, xanthan gum, baking powder, salt and anise together.
  3. Beat the eggs with the vanilla and orange zest, then stir into the dry ingredients to make a stiff, sticky dough. Mix in the whole almonds.
  4. With wet hands, shape the dough into a log about 30 cm (12 in) long and 7 cm (3 in) wide, and flatten the top slightly.
  5. Bake 25 minutes, until firm and golden. Cool for 20 minutes; almond-flour logs crumble if sliced hot.
  6. Lower the oven to 150 °C (300 °F). With a serrated knife, cut the log on the diagonal into 24 slices about 1.5 cm (½ in) thick.
  7. Lay the slices flat and bake 10 minutes, turn them and bake 8–10 minutes more, until dry. They crisp further as they cool.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Saw gently with the knife rather than pressing down, and the whole almonds will cut cleanly.
  • In Tuscany they are dipped in sweet wine; coffee works too.

Where it comes from

Biscotti · Prato · Southern Europe · 1800s

Twice-baked biscuits kept well and were made in Italy for centuries; 'biscotto' itself means twice-cooked. The almond version known as cantucci or biscotti di Prato is tied to the Tuscan town of Prato, where a bakery made them famous in the nineteenth century. The traditional dough uses eggs, sugar, flour and almonds, with no butter or oil.

See it on the map

Chewy ginger molasses cookies

Soft, crackle-topped spice cookies with a chewy middle, warm with ginger, cinnamon and cloves and a true molasses flavour from a single spoonful.

Dairy-free option 2.1 g net carbs Serves 20 · 1 cookie prep 20 min · cook 12 min · rest 45 min

Ingredients

  • 2¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (216 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • ¾ tsp baking soda
  • ¼ tsp xanthan gum
  • 2 tsp ground ginger
  • 1½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ¼ tsp ground cloves
  • ¼ tsp ground allspice
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tbsp blackstrap or dark molasses (20 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g), for rolling

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.1 g2.1 g
Total carbohydrate10.3 g10.3 g
Fibre1.5 g1.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.8 g6.8 g
Sugars1.4 g1.4 g
Fat10.6 g10.5 g
Protein2.8 g2.8 g
Energy118 kcal117 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Whisk together the almond flour, coconut flour, baking soda, xanthan gum, spices and salt.
  2. Beat the butter with the allulose and the 1/4 cup erythritol until creamy, then beat in the egg, molasses and vanilla.
  3. Stir in the dry ingredients to a soft dough. Cover and chill 30 minutes so it can be rolled.
  4. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line two baking sheets with parchment.
  5. Roll the dough into 20 balls, roll each in the remaining erythritol and set 5 cm (2 in) apart. Press each down slightly.
  6. Bake 10–12 minutes, until the tops crack and the edges are set but the centres still look soft. Allulose browns fast, so watch the last few minutes.
  7. Cool 10 minutes on the sheet before moving; they firm up as they cool but stay chewy.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

A stick-style dairy-free butter works one-for-one; the cookies spread a little more, so chill the dough the full time.

Tips

  • Molasses is real sugar (about 15 g of carbohydrate per tablespoon), so the whole batch gets just one tablespoon — under 1 g per cookie. It is there for the dark, slightly bitter flavour that no sweetener copies; don't add more.
  • Allulose is what keeps the centres chewy; an all-erythritol version bakes up crisp, more like a gingersnap.

Where it comes from

Molasses cookie · New England · North America · 1800s

Molasses, shipped in from the Caribbean sugar trade, was the everyday sweetener of colonial and nineteenth-century New England, far cheaper than refined sugar. Ginger-spiced molasses cookies and gingerbreads became traditional household baking there and spread across the United States.

See it on the map

Chocolate chip cookies

Chewy-edged almond-flour cookies studded with sugar-free dark chocolate, with a short chill so they hold their shape.

Dairy-free option 1.6 g net carbs Serves 18 · 1 cookie prep 15 min · cook 11 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp allulose (24 g), helps the cookies spread and stay chewy
  • 1 large egg, at room temperature
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (216 g)
  • ¼ tsp xanthan gum
  • ½ tsp baking soda
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened) (85 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.6 g1.6 g
Total carbohydrate11.9 g11.9 g
Fibre1.8 g1.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.4 g8.4 g
Sugars0.7 g0.7 g
Fat13.2 g13.1 g
Protein3.3 g3.2 g
Energy142 kcal142 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened), xanthan gum.

⚠️ Many brands contain milk fat; choose one labelled dairy-free.

Method

  1. Beat the butter, erythritol and allulose until pale and fluffy, about 2 minutes. Beat in the egg and vanilla.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, xanthan gum, baking soda and salt together, then stir them into the butter mixture until no dry streaks remain. Fold in the chocolate chips.
  3. Cover and chill the dough for 30 minutes; almond-flour dough is soft and spreads too thin if baked warm.
  4. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line two baking sheets with parchment. Scoop 18 balls of dough, space them 5 cm (2 in) apart and flatten each slightly.
  5. Bake 10–12 minutes, until the edges are golden but the centres still look soft.
  6. Leave on the sheet for 10 minutes before moving; they are fragile until cool and firm up as they set.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

Dairy-free butter gives a slightly softer, flatter cookie with less of a browned-butter flavour; chill the dough the full 30 minutes.

Tips

  • A pinch more salt on top just out of the oven sharpens the chocolate.
  • Allulose browns quickly; check the first tray at 9 minutes.

Where it comes from

Drop cookie · Whitman, Massachusetts · North America · 1930s

The chocolate chip cookie is credited to Ruth Wakefield, who ran the Toll House Inn in Whitman, Massachusetts, and added chopped semi-sweet chocolate to a butter cookie in the late 1930s. Her recipe spread through newspapers and cookbooks, and chocolate makers soon began selling chips made for baking.

Coconut macaroons

Golden-tipped mounds of shredded coconut bound with whipped egg white, crisp outside and moist inside.

Dairy-free 1.9 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 macaroon prep 15 min · cook 20 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

  • 3 large egg whites, at room temperature
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ⅛ tsp cream of tartar
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp almond extract
  • 2½ cups unsweetened shredded coconut (200 g)
  • 40 g dark chocolate, 85% cacao, optional, for dipping

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.9 g
Total carbohydrate10.2 g
Fibre2.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g
Sugars1.6 g
Fat9.1 g
Protein1.7 g
Energy103 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, granulated erythritol.

⚠️ Check the label: some dark chocolate contains milk.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Whisk the egg whites with the salt and cream of tartar to soft peaks. Add the erythritol a spoonful at a time and whisk to firm, glossy peaks. Whisk in the vanilla and almond extract.
  3. Fold in the coconut until evenly coated.
  4. Scoop 16 mounds onto the sheet, pinching each into a peak.
  5. Bake 18–22 minutes, until the peaks and bottoms are golden. Cool on the sheet.
  6. If you like, melt the chocolate and dip the bases, then set on parchment until firm.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Unsweetened coconut is drier than the sweetened kind; let the mixture stand 5 minutes before scooping so it binds.

Where it comes from

Macaroon · United States · North America · late 1800s

Macaroons began as almond-paste cookies in Italy and France. Once dried, shredded coconut became widely sold in the late nineteenth century, American cooks made coconut versions, and because they need no flour or leavening they became a Passover favourite in Jewish American households.
