Florentines

Lacy almond caramel cookies with orange zest, their flat undersides coated in dark chocolate.

Dairy-free option 2.3 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 florentine prep 20 min · cook 12 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 3 tbsp unsalted butter (43 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 3 tbsp heavy whipping cream (45 g)
  • 1 cup almonds (143 g), sliced or slivered
  • 2 tbsp almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (12 g)
  • 1 tbsp orange zest
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 60 g dark chocolate, 85% cacao

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.3 g2.4 g
Total carbohydrate7.9 g8.0 g
Fibre1.6 g1.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.0 g4.0 g
Sugars1.4 g1.4 g
Fat9.6 g9.5 g
Protein2.5 g2.4 g
Energy110 kcal109 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

⚠️ Check the label: some dark chocolate contains milk.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F); allulose browns quickly, so this is lower than a sugar recipe. Line two baking sheets with parchment.
  2. Melt the butter with the allulose and cream in a small pan and simmer 2 minutes, stirring, until it thickens slightly.
  3. Off the heat, stir in the almonds, almond flour, orange zest, vanilla and salt.
  4. Drop level tablespoons onto the sheets, 8 per sheet and well apart, and flatten each into a thin round.
  5. Bake 9–12 minutes, watching closely, until lacy and golden-brown at the edges. Cool on the sheet until firm, then lift off.
  6. Melt the chocolate, spread it over the flat undersides and drag a fork through to make waves. Set chocolate side up.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream caramel is a little softer and faintly coconut-flavoured; bake a minute longer so the florentines crisp.

Tips

  • Erythritol won't make a caramel that binds; this one needs allulose.

Where it comes from

Florentine · Florence · Southern Europe · by the 1800s

Despite the name, the origin of florentines is disputed. Many food historians credit French or Austrian pastry kitchens rather than Florence, and the stories tying them to Florentine nobility are not documented. What is clear is that the almond-and-caramel lace biscuit with a chocolate back was a European confectioners' classic by the nineteenth century.

Gingersnaps

Crisp, deeply spiced ginger cookies with crackled tops, made dairy-free with coconut oil.

Dairy-free 1.0 g net carbs Serves 24 · 1 cookie prep 15 min · cook 14 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • ⅓ cup coconut oil (72 g), softened, not melted
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp allulose (24 g), for colour and snap
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp ground ginger
  • 1 tsp ground cinnamon
  • ¼ tsp ground cloves
  • ⅛ tsp ground black pepper
  • ¾ tsp baking soda
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g), for rolling

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.0 g
Total carbohydrate8.1 g
Fibre1.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g
Sugars0.5 g
Fat7.5 g
Protein2.1 g
Energy82 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Beat the coconut oil, erythritol and allulose together, then beat in the egg and vanilla.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, spices, baking soda and salt, and stir into the wet mixture. Chill 30 minutes.
  3. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line two baking sheets with parchment.
  4. Roll into 24 small balls, roll in erythritol and set 5 cm (2 in) apart. Flatten each to about 6 mm (¼ in).
  5. Bake 12–14 minutes, until browned at the edges. Cool on the sheet; they snap once completely cool.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • For extra crispness, return the cooled cookies to a 120 °C (250 °F) oven for 10 minutes.
  • Traditional gingersnaps use molasses; a drop of blackstrap is optional but adds sugar.

Where it comes from

Gingerbread · England · Northern Europe · by the 1800s

Ginger-spiced breads and biscuits have been baked in Europe since the Middle Ages. Crisp ginger biscuits were popular in Britain, where they are often called ginger nuts, and travelled to North America, where the name gingersnap was in common use by the nineteenth century.

Kourabiedes

Tender Greek butter-almond cookies scented with orange blossom and buried in a snowfall of powdered sweetener.

Dairy-free option 1.1 g net carbs Serves 20 · 1 cookie prep 20 min · cook 18 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • ½ cup almonds (72 g), blanched, toasted and chopped
  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • 1 large egg yolk
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp orange blossom water, or rose water
  • 1¾ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (168 g)
  • ½ tsp baking powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g), for coating

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.1 g1.1 g
Total carbohydrate6.9 g6.9 g
Fibre1.3 g1.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.5 g4.5 g
Sugars0.6 g0.6 g
Fat11.0 g10.9 g
Protein2.7 g2.7 g
Energy115 kcal115 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: orange blossom water, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Toast the blanched almonds in a dry pan until golden, cool and chop finely.
  2. Beat the butter with the ¼ cup powdered erythritol for 5 minutes, until very pale; the long beating gives kourabiedes their tender, melting texture.
  3. Beat in the egg yolk, vanilla and orange blossom water. Stir in the almond flour, baking powder, salt and chopped almonds. Chill 30 minutes.
  4. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment. Shape the dough into 20 small crescents or balls.
  5. Bake 15–18 minutes, until just golden on the bottom but pale on top.
  6. While still warm, sprinkle a little orange blossom water over them if you like, then dredge thickly in the remaining powdered erythritol. Cool completely and dredge again.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

Dairy-free butter makes a slightly softer cookie; chill the shaped cookies 15 minutes before baking so they keep their crescents.

Tips

  • They are fragile while warm; move them only once cool.

Where it comes from

Shortbread · Greece · Southern Europe · Ottoman era

Kourabiedes share their name and lineage with the kurabiye of Turkey and the qurabiya of Persia and the Arab world, rich butter cookies that spread across the Ottoman lands. In Greece they became a Christmas and celebration cookie, made with toasted almonds and buried in icing sugar.

Linzer cookies

Hazelnut-almond sandwich cookies with a window of quick raspberry chia jam, dusted with powdered sweetener.

Dairy-free option 1.7 g net carbs Serves 14 · 1 sandwich cookie prep 40 min · cook 12 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Raspberry jam

  • 125 g raspberries
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 1 tsp fresh lemon juice
  • 1 tbsp chia seeds (12 g)

Dough

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g)
  • 1 large egg yolk
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 cup hazelnut flour (ground hazelnuts) (96 g)
  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g), for dusting

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.7 g1.7 g
Total carbohydrate9.8 g9.8 g
Fibre2.4 g2.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose5.7 g5.7 g
Sugars1.0 g1.0 g
Fat15.0 g14.8 g
Protein2.9 g2.9 g
Energy157 kcal157 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. For the jam, simmer the raspberries, erythritol and lemon juice for 5 minutes, mashing the fruit. Stir in the chia seeds and cool; it thickens as it stands.
  2. Beat the butter and ⅓ cup powdered erythritol until smooth, then beat in the egg yolk, lemon zest and vanilla. Stir in the hazelnut flour, almond flour, cinnamon and salt.
  3. Divide the dough in two, flatten into discs, wrap and chill 1 hour.
  4. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line two baking sheets with parchment.
  5. Roll each disc between parchment to 3 mm (⅛ in) thick and cut 28 rounds with a 6 cm (2½ in) cutter. Cut a small window from the centre of half of them. Re-chill if the dough softens.
  6. Bake 10–12 minutes, until the edges just colour. Cool completely on the sheets.
  7. Dust the window cookies with powdered erythritol. Spread about 1 teaspoon of jam on each solid cookie and top with a window cookie.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

The dairy-free dough is softer and tears more easily; roll it straight from the fridge and chill the cut rounds for 10 minutes before baking.

Tips

  • Assemble on the day you serve them; the jam softens the cookies overnight.

Where it comes from

Linzer · Linz · Western Europe · 1600s

The Linzer torte, a nut pastry filled with jam under a lattice, is named after the Austrian city of Linz and is among the oldest named cakes in Europe; a recipe for it survives from the seventeenth century. The sandwich cookies with a jam window are a later, smaller version of the same pastry, and are now a Christmas staple in Austria and beyond.

See it on the map

Ma'amoul-style walnut and pistachio cookies

Buttery almond-flour shells filled with cinnamon walnuts or rosewater pistachios, pressed in a mould or patterned with a fork.

Dairy-free option 2.0 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 cookie prep 45 min · cook 18 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

Dough

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • ½ tsp xanthan gum
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup unsalted butter (75 g), melted
  • 1 large egg white
  • 2 tsp orange blossom water (10 g)

Walnut filling

  • ½ cup walnuts (58 g), finely chopped
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 1 tsp orange blossom water

Pistachio filling

  • ⅓ cup pistachios, shelled (41 g), finely chopped
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)
  • 1 tsp rose water

To finish

  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.0 g2.0 g
Total carbohydrate8.5 g8.5 g
Fibre2.1 g2.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.4 g4.4 g
Sugars1.0 g1.0 g
Fat13.8 g13.9 g
Protein4.1 g4.0 g
Energy148 kcal150 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, orange blossom water, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), rose water, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Mix the almond flour, coconut flour, powdered erythritol, xanthan gum and salt. Stir in the melted butter, egg white and orange blossom water and knead briefly to a smooth, pliable dough. Cover and rest 30 minutes.
  2. Stir each filling together in its own bowl; they should clump when pressed.
  3. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  4. Divide the dough into 16 balls. Flatten one in your palm to a 7 cm (3 in) round, put a heaped teaspoon of filling in the centre, and close the dough around it, pinching to seal. Use walnut filling for 8 and pistachio for 8.
  5. Press each into a ma'amoul mould and tap out, or flatten slightly and pattern the tops with a fork or tweezers.
  6. Bake 15–18 minutes, until the bottoms are just golden; the tops stay pale. Cool completely, then dust with powdered erythritol.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.85 × the amount) — melted; traditional ma'amoul often uses ghee or a neutral fat.

Coconut oil makes a slightly crisper, more crumbly shell; keep the dough covered so it doesn't dry out while you fill the cookies.

Tips

  • If the dough cracks as you close it, patch it with a pinch from the next ball; almond dough has no gluten to stretch.
  • Traditional ma'amoul also come with date filling, left out here for the carbs.

Where it comes from

Ma'amoul · The Levant · Middle East & North Africa · by the 1800s

Ma'amoul are filled shortbread cookies shared across Lebanon, Syria, Palestine and Jordan, traditionally made from semolina and pressed in carved wooden moulds whose patterns signal the filling. Families bake them in large batches for Easter and for the feasts at the end of Ramadan. This version keeps the nut fillings and the shape but replaces the semolina shell.

See it on the map

Mexican wedding cookies (polvorones)

Crumbly pecan butter balls rolled twice in powdered sweetener, so tender they dissolve on the tongue.

Dairy-free option 0.7 g net carbs Serves 24 · 1 cookie prep 20 min · cook 16 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • ¾ cup pecans (82 g), toasted and finely ground
  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp ground cinnamon, optional
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g), for coating

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs0.7 g0.7 g
Total carbohydrate5.4 g5.4 g
Fibre0.9 g0.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose3.8 g3.8 g
Sugars0.4 g0.4 g
Fat9.4 g9.3 g
Protein1.6 g1.6 g
Energy94 kcal94 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Toast the pecans in a dry pan until fragrant, cool and pulse until fine; stop before they turn into paste.
  2. Beat the butter, ¼ cup powdered erythritol, vanilla, cinnamon and salt until fluffy. Stir in the ground pecans and almond flour. Chill 30 minutes.
  3. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment. Roll the dough into 24 balls and set 3 cm (1 in) apart.
  4. Bake 14–16 minutes, until the bottoms are lightly golden.
  5. Cool 5 minutes, then roll gently in the powdered erythritol. Cool completely and roll again.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

Dairy-free butter makes them a little more fragile; let them cool longer before the first roll in sweetener.

Tips

  • Powdered erythritol can taste cooling on its own; a blend with allulose or monk fruit softens that.

Where it comes from

Polvorón · Mexico · Latin America & Caribbean · colonial era

Polvorones, crumbly shortbreads whose name comes from the Spanish word for dust, are an old Andalusian sweet that came to Mexico with Spanish colonisation. Mexican bakers made them with local pecans, and the powdered-sugar balls became fixtures at weddings and holidays. Similar cookies exist across Europe and the Middle East, which is why the same recipe also goes by names such as Russian tea cakes and snowballs.

Nuremberg-style Lebkuchen

Soft, flourless nut-and-spice Christmas cookies in the Elisen style, glazed with dark chocolate.

Dairy-free 2.7 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 cookie prep 25 min · cook 18 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Dough

  • 2 large eggs
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp allulose (24 g)
  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 1 cup hazelnut flour (ground hazelnuts) (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp orange zest
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • 2 tsp ground cinnamon
  • ¼ tsp ground cloves
  • ¼ tsp ground allspice
  • ¼ tsp ground cardamom
  • ¼ tsp ground nutmeg
  • ¼ tsp ground ginger
  • ½ tsp baking powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 32 g almonds, 16 whole blanched almonds, for the tops; optional

Chocolate glaze

  • 60 g dark chocolate, 85% cacao
  • 2 tsp coconut oil

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.7 g
Total carbohydrate12.3 g
Fibre2.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose7.5 g
Sugars1.5 g
Fat10.6 g
Protein3.6 g
Energy125 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol.

⚠️ Check the label: some dark chocolate contains milk.

Method

  1. Beat the eggs with the erythritol and allulose for 5 minutes, until thick and pale.
  2. Fold in the almond and hazelnut flours, zests, spices, baking powder and salt. The mixture will be soft. Cover and leave 1 hour so the nuts absorb the moisture and the spices bloom.
  3. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F) and line two baking sheets with parchment.
  4. With wet hands, shape 16 mounds about 6 cm (2½ in) across and 1.5 cm (½ in) high. Press an almond into any you are not glazing.
  5. Bake 15–18 minutes, until the edges are firm and the tops just coloured; the centres stay soft. Cool completely.
  6. Melt the chocolate with the coconut oil and brush or spoon it over the cooled cookies. Leave to set.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Traditional Elisenlebkuchen are baked on thin wafers called Oblaten, which contain starch; parchment does the job here.
  • Stored airtight for a few days, they soften and the spice deepens.

Where it comes from

Lebkuchen · Nuremberg · Western Europe · medieval

Honey-and-spice cakes were made in German monasteries in the Middle Ages, and Nuremberg, a hub of the spice trade, became famous for them; the city's Lebkuchen bakers are recorded from the late medieval period. The richest kind, Elisenlebkuchen, is made almost entirely of nuts with little or no flour.

Peanut butter cookies

Flourless peanut butter cookies with the classic fork crosshatch, dairy-free as written and ready in twenty minutes.

Dairy-free 2.8 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 cookie prep 10 min · cook 11 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup peanut butter, smooth, unsweetened (256 g), well stirred
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ½ tsp baking soda
  • ¼ tsp fine salt, skip if your peanut butter is salted

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.8 g
Total carbohydrate9.6 g
Fibre0.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g
Sugars1.7 g
Fat8.5 g
Protein3.9 g
Energy102 kcal

Contains: eggs, peanuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Stir all the ingredients together until thick and smooth.
  3. Roll into 16 balls and set 5 cm (2 in) apart. Press each flat with a fork, then press again at right angles to make a crosshatch.
  4. Bake 10–12 minutes, until the edges are just golden. The cookies are very soft when hot: leave them on the sheet for 15 minutes before moving.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Use a no-stir-style peanut butter with only peanuts and salt; a very oily natural one makes the cookies spread.
  • Dip the fork in erythritol between cookies so it doesn't stick.

Where it comes from

Peanut butter cookie · United States · North America · early 1900s

Peanut butter became a common American food around the turn of the twentieth century, and peanut butter cookies were printed in US cookbooks in the early 1900s. By the 1930s the fork-pressed crosshatch, which flattens the stiff dough so it bakes evenly, had become the standard way to shape them.

Scottish shortbread

A buttery almond-flour shortbread round, scored into petticoat-tail wedges and baked low until sandy and pale.

Dairy-free option 1.6 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 wedge prep 15 min · cook 30 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • 2 tsp granulated erythritol, for sprinkling

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.6 g1.6 g
Total carbohydrate7.3 g7.3 g
Fibre1.8 g1.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.0 g4.0 g
Sugars0.8 g0.8 g
Fat16.2 g16.0 g
Protein3.6 g3.5 g
Energy166 kcal166 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C (300 °F). Line the base of a 20 cm (8 in) round tin with parchment.
  2. Beat the butter, powdered erythritol, vanilla and salt until smooth. Stir in the almond flour and coconut flour to a soft dough; don't overwork it.
  3. Press evenly into the tin. Crimp the edge with your fingers, score into 12 wedges and prick all over with a fork.
  4. Chill 30 minutes so the butter firms and the shortbread keeps its sandy texture.
  5. Bake 28–32 minutes, until firm and pale golden at the edges. Sprinkle with the erythritol and re-cut along the scores while warm.
  6. Cool completely in the tin before lifting out; it is crumbly until cold.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

Shortbread is mostly butter, so the dairy-free version tastes noticeably different; choose a dairy-free butter you like on its own, and add a pinch more salt.

Tips

  • Low heat is the point: shortbread should be pale, not browned.

Where it comes from

Shortbread · Scotland · Northern Europe · 1500s

Shortbread grew out of medieval 'biscuit bread', twice-baked dough that bakers enriched with butter in place of yeast. By the sixteenth century it was a Scottish luxury for festivals such as Hogmanay. The story that Mary, Queen of Scots, favoured the thin wedges called petticoat tails is popular but unproven.

See it on the map

Snickerdoodles

Soft, crackle-topped butter cookies rolled in cinnamon sweetener, with cream of tartar for their signature tang.

Dairy-free option 1.4 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 cookie prep 15 min · cook 11 min · rest 20 min

Ingredients

Dough

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • 1 tsp cream of tartar
  • ½ tsp baking soda
  • ¼ tsp fine salt

Coating

  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 2 tsp ground cinnamon

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.4 g1.4 g
Total carbohydrate10.4 g10.4 g
Fibre1.5 g1.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose7.5 g7.5 g
Sugars0.7 g0.7 g
Fat12.4 g12.3 g
Protein3.1 g3.1 g
Energy131 kcal131 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Beat the butter and erythritol until fluffy, then beat in the egg and vanilla.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, cream of tartar, baking soda and salt, and stir into the butter mixture to a soft dough. Chill 20 minutes.
  3. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment. Mix the coating erythritol and cinnamon in a small bowl.
  4. Roll the dough into 16 balls, roll each in the cinnamon mixture and set 5 cm (2 in) apart. Press each down to about 1.5 cm (½ in) thick, since almond-flour dough does not spread much.
  5. Bake 10–12 minutes, until the tops crackle and the edges just colour. Cool on the sheet 10 minutes.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

With dairy-free butter the cookies are a touch softer and flatter; flatten them a little less before baking.

Tips

  • Cream of tartar gives the tang; don't replace it with extra baking powder.

Where it comes from

Drop cookie · New England · North America · 1800s

Snickerdoodles appear in American cookbooks in the late nineteenth century and are often associated with New England baking. The origin of the name is uncertain: it is sometimes linked to the German Schneckennudeln, a kind of cinnamon roll, but that connection is a guess rather than a documented fact.

See it on the map

Soft amaretti

Chewy almond and egg-white cookies with crackled, sweetener-dusted tops, naturally flourless and dairy-free.

Dairy-free 0.9 g net carbs Serves 20 · 1 cookie prep 15 min · cook 20 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 large egg whites, at room temperature
  • 1 tsp almond extract
  • ½ tsp lemon zest
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g), for rolling

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs0.9 g
Total carbohydrate8.1 g
Fibre1.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.3 g
Sugars0.5 g
Fat5.0 g
Protein2.4 g
Energy60 kcal

Contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Mix the almond flour, erythritol and salt in a bowl.
  3. Whisk the egg whites to soft peaks, then fold them into the almond mixture with the almond extract and lemon zest to make a sticky paste.
  4. With damp hands, roll the paste into 20 balls. Roll each in powdered erythritol and set 3 cm (1 in) apart.
  5. Bake 18–20 minutes, until the tops crack and the bottoms are lightly golden; the centres should stay soft.
  6. Cool completely on the sheet; they firm up as they cool.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Traditional amaretti get their bitter note from apricot kernels; the almond extract stands in for it.
  • Stored airtight, they stay chewy for about a week.

Where it comes from

Macaroon · Saronno · Southern Europe · by the 1800s

Almond-and-egg-white cookies have been baked across Italy for centuries, and many towns have their own amaretti. Saronno, near Milan, became the best known for the crisp kind, and by the nineteenth century they were sold commercially there. The popular story of a young couple baking them for a visiting cardinal in the 1700s is a legend.

See it on the map

Speculoos

Thin, crisp spiced biscuits from the Low Countries, rolled and cut or pressed into a wooden mould.

Dairy-free option 1.0 g net carbs Serves 24 · 1 biscuit prep 20 min · cook 14 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp allulose (12 g), for the caramel colour
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp ground cinnamon
  • ½ tsp ground nutmeg
  • ¼ tsp ground cloves
  • ½ tsp ground ginger
  • ¼ tsp ground cardamom
  • ½ tsp baking soda
  • ¼ tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.0 g1.0 g
Total carbohydrate6.6 g6.6 g
Fibre1.1 g1.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.5 g4.5 g
Sugars0.5 g0.5 g
Fat8.3 g8.2 g
Protein2.1 g2.1 g
Energy88 kcal88 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Beat the butter, erythritol and allulose until creamy, then beat in the egg.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, spices, baking soda and salt together and mix into the butter to a firm dough.
  3. Flatten into a disc, wrap and chill at least 1 hour; the spices deepen if you leave it overnight.
  4. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F) and line two baking sheets with parchment.
  5. Roll the dough between two sheets of parchment to 3 mm (⅛ in) thick. Cut into rectangles, or press into a speculoos mould dusted with almond flour and turn out. Transfer to the sheets.
  6. Bake 12–14 minutes, until deep golden at the edges. Cool on the sheet; they crisp as they cool.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

Dairy-free butter gives a softer dough; keep it cold and work quickly when rolling and cutting.

Tips

  • If the dough cracks while rolling, let it sit for 5 minutes at room temperature.

Where it comes from

Spiced biscuit · Belgium and the Netherlands · Western Europe · by the 1700s

Speculoos (speculaas in Dutch) are spiced shortcrust biscuits traditionally pressed into carved wooden moulds and baked for the feast of Saint Nicholas in early December. The warm spices reflect the spice trade that ran through the Low Countries' ports, and both Belgium and the Netherlands claim the biscuit as their own.

See it on the map

Vanillekipferl

Austrian almond crescents, tender and short, tossed in vanilla sweetener while still warm.

Dairy-free option 0.7 g net carbs Serves 24 · 1 crescent prep 30 min · cook 14 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • ½ cup hazelnut flour (ground hazelnuts) (48 g)
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), cold, diced
  • 1 large egg yolk
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g), for coating

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs0.7 g0.7 g
Total carbohydrate4.4 g4.4 g
Fibre0.8 g0.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose2.9 g2.9 g
Sugars0.4 g0.4 g
Fat8.4 g8.3 g
Protein1.7 g1.7 g
Energy86 kcal86 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Mix the almond flour, hazelnut flour, ¼ cup powdered erythritol and salt. Rub in the butter, then add the egg yolk and 1 teaspoon of the vanilla and knead quickly to a smooth dough.
  2. Wrap and chill 1 hour.
  3. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  4. Roll walnut-sized pieces into 8 cm (3 in) ropes, thicker in the middle, and curve each into a crescent on the sheet.
  5. Bake 12–14 minutes, until just golden at the tips.
  6. Rub the remaining vanilla into the coating sweetener. Cool the crescents 5 minutes, then very gently toss them in it.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

Dairy-free butter gives a softer dough that loses its shape faster; chill the shaped crescents 15 minutes before baking.

Tips

  • A scraped vanilla bean in the coating is traditional and worth it.

Where it comes from

Kipferl · Vienna · Western Europe · by the 1900s

Vanillekipferl are short nut crescents baked for Christmas in Austria, Germany and the Czech lands. The crescent shape is sometimes tied to a legend about the Ottoman siege of Vienna in 1683, but there is no evidence for that story. They became a fixture of Viennese Christmas baking once vanilla grew affordable.

Bars & brownies

Blondies

Chewy brown-butter bars with toasted pecans and sugar-free chocolate chips, the vanilla cousin of the brownie.

Dairy-free option 1.5 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 15 min · cook 25 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g)
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g), gives the butterscotch flavour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • ½ tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup pecans (36 g), toasted and chopped
  • ⅓ cup sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened) (56 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.5 g1.5 g
Total carbohydrate13.5 g13.5 g
Fibre1.7 g1.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.3 g10.3 g
Sugars0.7 g0.7 g
Fat13.9 g13.8 g
Protein3.3 g3.3 g
Energy148 kcal148 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol, sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened).

⚠️ Many brands contain milk fat; choose one labelled dairy-free.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment.
  2. Melt the butter in a light-coloured pan and keep cooking, swirling, until it smells nutty and the solids turn brown. Pour into a bowl and cool 10 minutes.
  3. Whisk in the erythritol and allulose, then the eggs and vanilla.
  4. Stir in the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder and salt, then the pecans and chocolate chips.
  5. Spread in the pan and bake 22–26 minutes, until golden and just set in the centre. Allulose browns fast; tent with foil if the top darkens early.
  6. Cool completely, then chill 30 minutes before cutting.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — melt it but don't try to brown it.

Dairy-free butter can't be browned, so the bars lose the toffee note; add an extra ½ teaspoon of vanilla and toast the pecans well.

Where it comes from

Brownie · United States · North America · 1900s

Butterscotch bars made with brown sugar and no chocolate appeared in American cookbooks in the first half of the twentieth century, sometimes as 'butterscotch brownies'. The name blondie came into wide use later in the century as the bar became a bake-sale staple alongside the brownie.

See it on the map

Fudgy brownies

Dense, crackle-topped chocolate brownies built on unsweetened chocolate and just enough almond flour to hold them together.

Dairy-free option 1.7 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 15 min · cook 25 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g)
  • 85 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), chopped
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g), keeps the centre fudgy
  • 3 large eggs, at room temperature
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ½ cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (48 g)
  • ¼ cup unsweetened cocoa powder (22 g)
  • 1 tsp instant espresso powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp baking powder
  • ¼ cup sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened) (42 g), optional

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.7 g1.7 g
Total carbohydrate13.7 g13.7 g
Fibre2.0 g2.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.0 g10.0 g
Sugars0.3 g0.3 g
Fat11.9 g12.2 g
Protein3.0 g3.0 g
Energy130 kcal132 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

⚠️ Many brands contain milk fat; choose one labelled dairy-free.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment, leaving an overhang.
  2. Melt the butter and chocolate together over low heat, stirring until smooth. Cool slightly.
  3. Whisk the erythritol, allulose and eggs hard for 2 minutes, until pale; this makes the shiny, crackled top. Whisk in the chocolate mixture and vanilla.
  4. Fold in the almond flour, cocoa, espresso powder, salt and baking powder, then the chocolate chips.
  5. Spread in the pan and bake 22–26 minutes, until the edges are set and a skewer in the centre comes out with moist crumbs.
  6. Cool completely in the pan, then chill 1 hour before cutting into 16 squares.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.85 × the amount).

Coconut oil makes a firmer, more truffle-like brownie when chilled; serve at room temperature for the fudgiest texture.

Tips

  • Cut with a hot, dry knife, wiping between cuts.

Where it comes from

Brownie · Chicago · North America · 1890s

The brownie is often credited to the kitchen of Chicago's Palmer House hotel, which is said to have created a chocolate-nut bar for the 1893 World's Columbian Exposition, though that account is hard to document. Recipes called brownies appear in American cookbooks by the early 1900s and became fudgier and richer in chocolate over the following decades.

See it on the map

Lemon bars

A sharp, silky lemon custard baked over a crisp almond shortbread base, dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 1.6 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 20 min · cook 40 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 3 tbsp granulated erythritol (36 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ cup coconut oil (54 g), melted

Filling

  • 4 large eggs
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp allulose (24 g)
  • ½ cup fresh lemon juice (122 g), from about 3 lemons
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

To finish

  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.6 g
Total carbohydrate13.1 g
Fibre1.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.2 g
Sugars0.8 g
Fat9.4 g
Protein3.7 g
Energy109 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment, leaving an overhang.
  2. Mix the crust ingredients, press evenly into the pan and bake 12–15 minutes, until golden. Lower the oven to 160 °C (325 °F).
  3. Whisk the eggs, erythritol and allulose until smooth. Whisk in the lemon juice, zest, coconut flour and salt, then leave 5 minutes for the coconut flour to hydrate and whisk again.
  4. Pour over the warm crust and bake 20–25 minutes, until the filling is set at the edges with only a slight wobble in the centre.
  5. Cool to room temperature, then chill at least 3 hours. Dust with powdered erythritol and cut into 16 squares with a clean, wet knife.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Strain the filling for the smoothest top; coconut flour can leave a few specks.

Where it comes from

Lemon bar · United States · North America · mid-1900s

Lemon bars, also called lemon squares, became common in American community cookbooks in the mid-twentieth century. They descend from older English lemon curd and lemon tarts, pressed into a sheet pan with a shortbread base so they could be cut into squares.

Millionaire's shortbread

Almond shortbread, a soft allulose caramel and a snap of dark chocolate, stacked and cut into small squares.

Dairy-free option 3.3 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 30 min · cook 35 min · rest 2 h 30 min

Ingredients

Shortbread

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ cup unsalted butter (57 g), melted

Caramel

  • ¼ cup unsalted butter (57 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ⅓ cup heavy whipping cream (79 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp fine salt

Chocolate

  • 100 g dark chocolate, 85% cacao, chopped
  • 2 tsp coconut oil

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.3 g3.5 g
Total carbohydrate10.8 g11.0 g
Fibre1.7 g1.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose5.8 g5.8 g
Sugars2.2 g2.2 g
Fat15.5 g15.3 g
Protein2.7 g2.7 g
Energy168 kcal167 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

⚠️ Check the label: some dark chocolate contains milk.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment, leaving an overhang.
  2. Mix the shortbread ingredients, press firmly into the pan and bake 15–18 minutes, until golden. Cool completely.
  3. For the caramel, melt the butter with the allulose in a heavy pan and simmer, stirring, until it turns amber, 4–6 minutes. Pour in the cream carefully (it bubbles) and simmer 5–8 minutes more, stirring, until thick enough to coat a spoon. Stir in the vanilla and salt.
  4. Pour the caramel over the cooled shortbread and chill 1 hour, until firm.
  5. Melt the chocolate with the coconut oil, spread over the caramel and chill 1 hour more.
  6. Let stand 10 minutes, then cut into 16 squares with a hot knife.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

The coconut-cream caramel is softer and tastes faintly of coconut; simmer it a few minutes longer and keep the bars in the fridge.

Tips

  • Allulose caramel stays soft and chewy; erythritol would set gritty and hard.

Where it comes from

Shortbread · Scotland · Northern Europe · 1900s

Caramel shortbread, a layer of caramel and chocolate on a shortbread base, is closely associated with Scotland, though when and where it was first made is not documented. The name millionaire's shortbread, a nod to its richness, became common in Britain in the later twentieth century.

See it on the map

Nanaimo bars

Three no-bake layers: a chocolate-coconut-nut base, a vanilla buttercream middle and a thin chocolate top.

Dairy-free option 3.1 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 35 min · cook 5 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

Base

  • ⅓ cup unsalted butter (75 g)
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • ¼ cup unsweetened cocoa powder (22 g)
  • 1 large egg, beaten
  • ¾ cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (72 g)
  • ½ cup unsweetened shredded coconut (40 g)
  • ⅓ cup walnuts (39 g), finely chopped

Filling

  • ¼ cup unsalted butter (57 g), softened
  • 2 tbsp heavy whipping cream (30 g)
  • ¾ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (90 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Topping

  • 90 g dark chocolate, 85% cacao, chopped
  • 1 tbsp unsalted butter (14 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.1 g3.2 g
Total carbohydrate13.9 g13.9 g
Fibre2.1 g2.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.6 g8.6 g
Sugars1.9 g1.9 g
Fat16.5 g16.3 g
Protein2.7 g2.7 g
Energy174 kcal174 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

⚠️ Check the label: some dark chocolate contains milk.

Method

  1. Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment.
  2. For the base, melt the butter with the erythritol and cocoa over low heat. Take it off the heat and whisk in the egg, then return to very low heat and stir 1–2 minutes until it thickens slightly. Stir in the almond flour, coconut and walnuts.
  3. Press firmly into the pan and chill 30 minutes.
  4. For the filling, beat the butter, cream, powdered erythritol and vanilla until smooth and fluffy. Spread over the base and chill 30 minutes, until firm.
  5. Melt the chocolate with the butter, cool until barely warm, and spread thinly over the filling.
  6. Chill at least 2 hours. Let stand 10 minutes, then cut with a hot knife into 16 squares.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

The dairy-free filling is softer and melts faster at room temperature; keep the bars chilled until just before serving.

Tips

  • The original middle layer uses custard powder, which is cornstarch; vanilla and a little extra cream stand in for it.

Where it comes from

Nanaimo bar · Nanaimo, British Columbia · North America · 1950s

Nanaimo bars are named after the city of Nanaimo on Vancouver Island. Recipes under that name appeared in local community cookbooks in the 1950s, though similar no-bake layered bars were printed elsewhere earlier under other names. They are now regarded as a Canadian national treat.

Pecan pie bars

A gooey, caramel-like pecan filling on a short almond crust, cut into squares instead of slices.

Dairy-free option 1.3 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 20 min · cook 40 min · rest 2 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ cup unsalted butter (57 g), melted

Filling

  • ¼ cup unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 1½ cups pecans (164 g), roughly chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.3 g1.3 g
Total carbohydrate10.7 g10.7 g
Fibre1.9 g1.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose7.5 g7.5 g
Sugars0.9 g0.9 g
Fat18.4 g18.3 g
Protein3.7 g3.6 g
Energy187 kcal187 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment.
  2. Mix the crust ingredients, press into the pan and bake 12 minutes, until lightly golden. Lower the oven to 160 °C (325 °F), since allulose browns faster than sugar.
  3. Whisk the melted butter, allulose, eggs, vanilla and salt until smooth. Stir in the pecans.
  4. Pour over the crust and spread evenly. Bake 25–30 minutes, until the edges are set and the centre jiggles only slightly. Tent with foil if the nuts darken too quickly.
  5. Cool completely, then chill 2 hours before cutting into 16 squares.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.

With dairy-free butter the filling sets a little softer and tastes less like toffee; a pinch more salt helps.

Tips

  • Allulose is essential here: it gives the syrupy texture of a corn-syrup filling.

Where it comes from

Pecan pie · Southern United States · North America · early 1900s

Pecans are native to the southern United States, and pecan pie is a Southern classic whose exact origin is unknown. It became widespread in the early twentieth century, especially after corn-syrup makers promoted a recipe in the 1930s. Bars baked in a sheet pan are a more recent way of serving it to a crowd.

Raspberry crumble bars

Fresh raspberries baked between an almond shortbread base and a cinnamon nut crumble, dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 2.6 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 20 min · cook 40 min · rest 1 h 30 min

Ingredients

Base and crumble

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • ½ tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup coconut oil (72 g), softened
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ cup almonds (36 g), sliced, for the crumble
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon

Filling

  • 250 g raspberries
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 2 tsp fresh lemon juice (10 g)
  • 1 tbsp chia seeds (12 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.6 g
Total carbohydrate11.1 g
Fibre3.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose5.5 g
Sugars1.5 g
Fat12.6 g
Protein3.9 g
Energy146 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment.
  2. Mix the almond flour, coconut flour, erythritol, baking powder and salt. Rub in the coconut oil, then mix in the egg and vanilla to a crumbly dough.
  3. Press two-thirds of the dough into the pan and bake 12 minutes.
  4. Mix the remaining dough with the sliced almonds and cinnamon, pinching it into clumps.
  5. Toss the raspberries with the erythritol, lemon juice and chia seeds and spread over the warm base. Scatter the crumble on top.
  6. Bake 25–30 minutes, until the crumble is golden and the fruit bubbles at the edges.
  7. Cool completely, then chill 1 hour so the filling sets before cutting into 16 squares.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Frozen raspberries work; don't thaw them, and add 5 minutes to the bake.

Where it comes from

Crumble · United Kingdom · Northern Europe · 1940s

The fruit crumble became popular in Britain during the Second World War, when rationing made a rubbed-in topping a thrifty alternative to pastry. Bar versions, with a pressed shortbread base and a crumble on top, are a later idea that suits a square pan and a lunchbox.

Seven-layer magic bars

Layers of almond crumb, chocolate, coconut and pecans glued together with a homemade sugar-free coconut condensed milk.

Dairy-free 2.0 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 square prep 20 min · cook 55 min · rest 2 h

Ingredients

Condensed coconut milk

  • 1 cup full-fat coconut milk (canned) (226 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Crust

  • 1¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (120 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ¼ cup coconut oil (54 g), melted

Layers

  • ⅓ cup sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened) (56 g)
  • ¾ cup unsweetened shredded coconut (60 g)
  • ½ cup pecans (54 g), chopped
  • ¼ cup walnuts (29 g), chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.0 g
Total carbohydrate11.1 g
Fibre2.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.8 g
Sugars0.9 g
Fat17.5 g
Protein3.0 g
Energy180 kcal

Contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened).

⚠️ Many brands contain milk fat; choose one labelled dairy-free.

Method

  1. Simmer the coconut milk, allulose and salt in a wide pan, stirring often, for 25–30 minutes, until reduced by about half and thick enough to coat a spoon. Stir in the vanilla and cool a little.
  2. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment.
  3. Mix the crust ingredients, press into the pan and bake 10 minutes.
  4. Scatter the chocolate chips over the crust, then the coconut, then the pecans and walnuts. Pour the condensed coconut milk evenly over everything and press down lightly with a spatula.
  5. Bake 20–25 minutes, until the coconut on top is golden and the edges bubble.
  6. Cool completely, then chill 2 hours before cutting into 16 squares.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • The classic version has butterscotch chips as a seventh layer; there's no good sugar-free one, so a second nut stands in.
  • Allulose is what keeps the condensed milk soft and sticky; erythritol would crystallise.

Where it comes from

Magic bar · United States · North America · 1950s

Layered bars of graham-cracker crumbs, chocolate, coconut and nuts bound with sweetened condensed milk spread through American kitchens in the 1950s and 1960s, helped by condensed-milk advertising. They go by many names, including magic bars, seven-layer bars and Hello Dollies.

Pies & tarts

Almond frangipane tart (galette des rois-style)

Two rounds of almond pastry sealed around a rum-scented frangipane and scored in the classic pattern; a low-carb nod to the French Epiphany cake.

Dairy-free option 3.2 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 50 min · cook 35 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Pastry

  • 2½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (240 g)
  • 1 tbsp psyllium husk powder
  • ½ tsp xanthan gum
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 6 tbsp unsalted butter (85 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg

Frangipane

  • ⅓ cup unsalted butter (75 g), softened
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • ½ tsp rum extract
  • ¼ tsp almond extract

Glaze

  • 1 large egg yolk
  • 1 tsp water

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.2 g3.2 g
Total carbohydrate16.1 g16.1 g
Fibre4.2 g4.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.7 g8.7 g
Sugars1.6 g1.6 g
Fat32.0 g31.7 g
Protein8.9 g8.7 g
Energy337 kcal336 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, granulated erythritol, psyllium husk powder, rum extract, water, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Pulse the almond flour, psyllium, xanthan gum, erythritol and salt in a food processor. Add the butter and egg and pulse until the dough forms. Divide in two, flatten each into a disc and chill 30 minutes.
  2. Beat the butter and erythritol for the frangipane until fluffy, then the egg, almond flour and both extracts. Chill while you roll the pastry.
  3. Roll each disc between parchment to a 23 cm (9 in) round. Slide one round, on its parchment, onto a baking sheet.
  4. Spread the frangipane over it, leaving a 2 cm (3/4 in) border. Hide a whole almond in it if you like. Brush the border with water, lay the second round on top and press the edges firmly to seal.
  5. Whisk the yolk with the water and brush the top. Chill 20 minutes, brush again, then score a curved sunburst pattern lightly with a knife tip, and cut a small steam hole in the centre.
  6. Heat the oven to 180 °C (355 °F) and bake 30–35 minutes, until deep golden. Cool at least 30 minutes; serve warm or at room temperature.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter gives a slightly crumblier pastry and a softer frangipane; chill the assembled tart well before baking.

Tips

  • This is a short almond pastry, not puff pastry: it will be tender and crumbly rather than flaky.
  • Warn guests if you hide a whole almond in place of the traditional fève.

Where it comes from

Galette des rois · Paris · Western Europe · medieval

Sharing a cake with a hidden bean at Epiphany, the finder becoming king or queen for the day, is a custom with medieval roots in France and elsewhere in Europe. In Paris and northern France the cake became a puff-pastry galette filled with frangipane, an almond cream; in the south a brioche ring is more usual. Porcelain charms called fèves later replaced the dried bean.

See it on the map

Apricot frangipane tart

A crisp almond-flour shell filled with soft almond cream and fresh apricot halves that slump and caramelise at the edges in the oven.

Dairy-free option 3.3 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 slice prep 35 min · cook 50 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cold, diced
  • 1 large egg yolk

Frangipane

  • 6 tbsp unsalted butter (85 g), softened
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 2 large eggs, at room temperature
  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • ½ tsp almond extract
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Topping

  • 4 fresh apricots, halved and stoned (about 140 g)
  • 2 tbsp almonds (18 g), flaked

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.3 g3.3 g
Total carbohydrate15.9 g15.9 g
Fibre2.6 g2.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.0 g10.0 g
Sugars2.2 g2.2 g
Fat22.1 g21.9 g
Protein6.2 g6.1 g
Energy239 kcal239 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Pulse or rub together the almond flour, coconut flour, erythritol, salt and cold butter until crumbly, then mix in the egg yolk to make a soft dough.
  2. Press the dough evenly into a 23 cm (9 in) tart tin with a removable base, chill 20 minutes, then prick and bake at 175 °C (350 °F) for 12 minutes until just golden at the edges.
  3. For the frangipane, beat the butter and erythritol until fluffy, then beat in the eggs one at a time. Fold in the almond flour, extracts and salt.
  4. Spread the frangipane in the warm shell. Press the apricot halves in, cut side up, in a ring, and scatter the flaked almonds around them.
  5. Bake 35–40 minutes, until the frangipane is puffed, golden and firm in the centre. Cover the edge with foil if the crust darkens.
  6. Cool at least an hour in the tin; the filling firms as it cools.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Use a firm stick-style dairy-free butter; a soft tub spread makes the crust crumbly and the frangipane greasy.

Tips

  • Choose ripe but firm apricots — very soft ones collapse and release juice into the cream.
  • Twelve thin slices keep the fruit per serving small; the apricots are the main source of net carbs.

Where it comes from

Frangipane tart · France · Western Europe · by the 1700s

Almond cream (crème d'amande) was part of French pastry by the 1700s, and its name frangipane is often linked, without firm evidence, to the Frangipani family of Italian nobles. Fruit tarts set on almond cream — with apricots, pears or plums depending on the season — are a staple of French pâtisseries, and apricot is the classic summer version.

Bakewell tart

Almond pastry spread with a quick raspberry-chia jam and baked under a moist almond frangipane and toasted flaked almonds.

Dairy-free option 3.3 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 40 min · cook 40 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Pastry

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg yolk

Raspberry jam

  • 1 cup raspberries (123 g)
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 1 tbsp chia seeds (12 g)

Frangipane

  • ½ cup unsalted butter (114 g), softened
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • ½ tsp almond extract
  • ½ tsp baking powder
  • 3 tbsp almonds (27 g), flaked

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.3 g3.3 g
Total carbohydrate21.6 g21.6 g
Fibre3.9 g3.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose14.4 g14.4 g
Sugars1.8 g1.8 g
Fat29.6 g29.2 g
Protein7.7 g7.6 g
Energy314 kcal314 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Pulse the pastry ingredients in a food processor until the dough clumps. Press it into a 23 cm (9 in) tart tin with a removable base and chill 20 minutes.
  2. Simmer the raspberries and erythritol, mashing, for 5 minutes. Stir in the chia seeds and cool; the jam thickens as it stands.
  3. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Bake the pastry 10 minutes, until just firm, and cool for 10 minutes.
  4. Beat the butter and erythritol until fluffy, then the eggs one at a time. Fold in the almond flour, almond extract and baking powder.
  5. Spread the jam over the pastry, then spoon the frangipane over it and smooth gently to the edges. Scatter the flaked almonds on top.
  6. Bake 25–30 minutes, until golden and springy in the centre; lay foil over it if it browns too fast. Cool at least an hour before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter makes a slightly denser frangipane with a softer crust; it tastes very close to the original.

Tips

  • Spread the jam thinly and keep it away from the very edge, or it bubbles up through the frangipane.

Where it comes from

Bakewell tart · Bakewell, Derbyshire · Northern Europe · 1800s

The town of Bakewell is known first for the Bakewell pudding, a jam-and-egg custard in pastry recorded in the 1800s. A popular story says it began as a cook's mistake at a local inn, but that tale is legend rather than record. The tart, with a firmer almond sponge over the jam, developed later from the pudding and became the familiar British teatime version in the 1900s.

See it on the map

Butter tarts

Small almond-pastry shells filled with a runny, caramel-like butter filling and a few toasted pecans.

Dairy-free option 1.6 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 tart prep 35 min · cook 20 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

Pastry

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)
  • ½ tsp xanthan gum
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 5 tbsp unsalted butter (71 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg

Filling

  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 3 tbsp unsalted butter (43 g), melted
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup pecans (36 g), toasted, chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.6 g1.6 g
Total carbohydrate12.6 g12.6 g
Fibre2.0 g2.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose9.0 g9.0 g
Sugars0.9 g0.9 g
Fat19.0 g18.8 g
Protein4.8 g4.7 g
Energy200 kcal199 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Pulse the almond flour, erythritol, xanthan gum and salt in a food processor. Add the butter and egg and pulse until the dough forms a ball. Chill 15 minutes.
  2. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Divide the dough into 12 pieces and press each into a cup of a standard 12-cup muffin tin, up the sides to about 2 cm (3/4 in) high.
  3. Bake the shells 8 minutes, until just set.
  4. Whisk the allulose, melted butter, egg, vanilla and salt until smooth. Sprinkle the pecans into the shells and pour the filling over them, about two-thirds full.
  5. Bake 12–15 minutes, until the filling bubbles and the pastry is golden; the centres should still be slightly runny. Cool 30 minutes in the tin before lifting out with a thin knife.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter gives a slightly less toffee-like filling, and the shells are a little more crumbly; let them cool fully in the tin.

Tips

  • Allulose darkens quickly; the lower 165 °C oven keeps the filling from scorching before the pastry is done.
  • Whether butter tarts should hold raisins is a long-running Canadian argument; pecans are the low-carb side of it.

Where it comes from

Butter tart · Barrie, Ontario · North America · 1900

Butter tarts are one of the few desserts widely thought of as Canadian. The earliest known printed recipe is usually traced to a 1900 community cookbook from Barrie, Ontario, though home cooks were likely making them before then. They are related to other syrup-and-butter pastries such as the Scottish ecclefechan tart and the American pecan pie.

French silk chocolate pie

A light, airy chocolate filling, beaten until it is smooth as silk, in a baked crust under softly whipped cream.

Dairy-free option 3.7 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 35 min · cook 12 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Filling

  • 85 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), melted and cooled
  • ¾ cup unsalted butter (170 g), softened
  • ¾ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (90 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 3 large eggs, pasteurized, at room temperature

Topping

  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g)
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g)
  • 15 g dark chocolate, 85% cacao, shaved

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.7 g4.6 g
Total carbohydrate19.8 g20.7 g
Fibre3.0 g3.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose13.0 g13.0 g
Sugars1.9 g2.0 g
Fat40.9 g40.1 g
Protein7.1 g7.0 g
Energy411 kcal408 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

⚠️ Check the label: some dark chocolate contains milk.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Press the crust into a 23 cm (9 in) pie plate and bake 10–12 minutes, until golden. Cool completely.
  2. Beat the softened butter and powdered erythritol with an electric mixer for 3 minutes, until pale and fluffy. Beat in the cooled melted chocolate, vanilla and salt.
  3. Add the eggs one at a time, beating 4–5 minutes on medium-high after each, until the filling is light and no longer grainy. This long beating is what gives the silky texture.
  4. Spread the filling in the cold crust and refrigerate at least 4 hours, until firm.
  5. Just before serving, whip the cream with the powdered erythritol to soft peaks, spread it over the pie and scatter the shaved chocolate on top.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style one.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and whip only the solid part.

With dairy-free butter the filling is slightly softer and tastes more of chocolate than of butter; keep it cold until serving.

Tips

  • The eggs are not cooked, so use pasteurized eggs.
  • If the filling looks curdled, the chocolate was too warm or the butter too cold; keep beating and it usually comes together.

Where it comes from

Chocolate cream pie · Maryland, United States · North America · 1951

Despite the name, this is an American pie rather than a French one. It is usually credited to a Maryland home baker's entry in the 1951 Pillsbury Bake-Off, which made it widely known. The original relied on raw eggs beaten into butter and chocolate, which is why modern versions call for pasteurized eggs.

See it on the map

Key lime pie

A sharp, pale lime custard set in a cinnamon-almond crust, with a homemade coconut condensed milk taking the place of the canned kind; dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 4.9 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 30 min · cook 45 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 4 tbsp coconut oil (54 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Coconut condensed milk

  • 1½ cups coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (360 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)

Filling

  • 4 large egg yolks
  • 1 tbsp lime zest, finely grated, from 2–3 limes
  • ½ cup fresh lime juice (120 g), Key limes if you can find them
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs4.9 g
Total carbohydrate18.6 g
Fibre1.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.0 g
Sugars2.1 g
Fat27.4 g
Protein5.4 g
Energy282 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Simmer the coconut cream and allulose in a wide saucepan over medium-low heat, stirring often, for 20–25 minutes, until reduced to about 1 cup and thick enough to coat a spoon. Cool to lukewarm.
  2. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Stir the crust ingredients together, press them into a 23 cm (9 in) pie plate and bake 10 minutes. Lower the oven to 160 °C (325 °F).
  3. Whisk the yolks with the lime zest until pale, then whisk in the cooled condensed coconut, followed by the lime juice and salt. It will thicken as the acid meets the yolks.
  4. Pour into the crust and bake 15 minutes, just until the surface is set but still pale.
  5. Cool for 30 minutes, then refrigerate at least 4 hours before slicing.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Do not boil the coconut cream hard; a gentle simmer keeps it from splitting into oil.
  • Persian limes are fine; the filling will just be a touch less floral than with Key limes.

Where it comes from

Key lime pie · Key West, Florida · North America · late 1800s

Key limes grew in the Florida Keys, where fresh milk was scarce before refrigeration and the arrival of a mainland road. Canned sweetened condensed milk, available from the later 1800s, made a custard that sets with lime juice alone. Who first made the pie is unknown, and the stories naming an inventor are local legend; Florida made it the official state pie in 2006.

See it on the map

Lemon meringue pie

A tart, buttery lemon curd filling with a little gelatin for clean slices, under a tall meringue browned in the oven.

Dairy-free option 2.6 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 40 min · cook 30 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 4 tbsp coconut oil (54 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Lemon filling

  • 3 tbsp water (45 g)
  • 1 tsp unflavored powdered gelatin
  • 5 large egg yolks
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • ½ cup fresh lemon juice (122 g)
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cubed

Meringue

  • 4 large egg whites, at room temperature
  • ¼ tsp cream of tartar
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.6 g2.6 g
Total carbohydrate18.5 g18.5 g
Fibre1.5 g1.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose14.4 g14.4 g
Sugars1.2 g1.2 g
Fat19.9 g19.7 g
Protein6.2 g6.2 g
Energy216 kcal216 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Press the crust into a 23 cm (9 in) pie plate and bake 10–12 minutes. Leave the oven on.
  2. Sprinkle the gelatin over the water and let it soften for 5 minutes.
  3. In a heavy saucepan whisk the yolks, powdered erythritol, lemon juice and zest. Cook over low heat, whisking constantly, until thick enough to coat a spoon, about 8 minutes; do not let it boil.
  4. Off the heat, whisk in the softened gelatin until it dissolves, then the butter a few cubes at a time. Pour into the crust.
  5. Beat the egg whites with the cream of tartar to soft peaks. Add the powdered erythritol a spoonful at a time and beat to stiff, glossy peaks; beat in the vanilla.
  6. Spread the meringue over the warm filling, sealing it right to the crust so it does not shrink. Swirl into peaks and bake 12–15 minutes, until the tips are golden.
  7. Cool for an hour at room temperature, then refrigerate at least 3 hours before slicing with a wet knife.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

The crust is already dairy-free; with dairy-free butter the curd is a little less rich but sets just as well thanks to the gelatin.

Tips

  • Erythritol meringue browns less than sugar meringue; a few seconds under the grill or a kitchen torch gives more colour.
  • Spread the meringue on warm filling: it cooks from below too and is less likely to weep.

Where it comes from

Lemon meringue pie · Philadelphia · North America · early 1800s

Lemon custards and lemon puddings in pastry were made in Britain and Europe for centuries, and meringue became common in European kitchens during the 1700s. The meringue-topped lemon pie familiar in America is often credited to the Philadelphia baker and cooking teacher Elizabeth Goodfellow in the early 1800s, though that attribution comes from later accounts.

See it on the map

Pecan pie

Toasted pecans set in a glossy, caramel-flavoured custard; allulose stands in for corn syrup because it stays liquid and browns like sugar.

Dairy-free option 2.2 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 20 min · cook 55 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Filling

  • 1½ cups pecans (164 g), halves, plus a few for the top
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • ¾ cup allulose (144 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • 1½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.2 g2.1 g
Total carbohydrate21.9 g21.9 g
Fibre3.0 g3.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.8 g16.8 g
Sugars1.5 g1.5 g
Fat30.0 g29.7 g
Protein6.6 g6.5 g
Energy309 kcal309 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Press the crust ingredients over the base and sides of a 23 cm (9 in) pie plate and bake 10 minutes. Lower the oven to 160 °C (325 °F).
  2. Toast the pecans on a tray for 6–8 minutes while the crust bakes, then chop about two-thirds of them roughly.
  3. Whisk the melted butter, allulose, eggs, vanilla and salt until smooth. Stir in the chopped pecans.
  4. Pour into the crust and arrange the whole halves on top. Bake 40–45 minutes, until the filling is puffed at the edge and only slightly loose in the centre.
  5. Allulose browns quickly: lay foil loosely over the pie after 20 minutes. Cool completely, at least 3 hours, so the filling sets.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter works in both crust and filling; the filling tastes slightly less of brown butter but sets the same.

Tips

  • Erythritol would recrystallise into a gritty layer here; allulose is the sweetener that behaves like syrup.

Where it comes from

Pecan pie · Texas and the American South · North America · late 1800s

Pecans are native to the river valleys of the southern United States and northern Mexico. Recipes for pecan pies and custards appear in print by the late 1800s, especially in Texas. The pie became a national fixture in the 1930s, when corn-syrup makers promoted a version built on their product.

See it on the map

Pumpkin pie

A smooth, warmly spiced pumpkin custard in a pressed almond crust, set firm enough to slice cleanly once chilled.

Dairy-free option 5.4 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 slice prep 25 min · cook 1 h · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Filling

  • 425 g pumpkin purée (canned, unsweetened), one 15 oz can
  • ¾ cup heavy whipping cream (178 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 1½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ½ tsp ground ginger
  • ¼ tsp ground nutmeg
  • ⅛ tsp ground cloves
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.4 g6.3 g
Total carbohydrate24.0 g24.9 g
Fibre3.6 g3.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.0 g15.0 g
Sugars3.4 g3.5 g
Fat25.2 g26.3 g
Protein7.5 g7.6 g
Energy284 kcal295 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Stir the crust ingredients together and press them evenly over the base and up the sides of a 23 cm (9 in) pie plate.
  2. Bake the crust 10–12 minutes, until just golden at the rim. Let it cool while you mix the filling.
  3. Whisk the pumpkin, cream, eggs, erythritol, spices, salt and vanilla until completely smooth.
  4. Pour the filling into the crust. Bake 45–50 minutes, until the edge is set and the centre jiggles slightly when the plate is nudged. Cover the rim with foil if it browns too fast.
  5. Cool on a rack for an hour, then refrigerate at least 3 hours before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream sets the filling just as firmly; a faint coconut note sits under the spices.

Tips

  • Blot very wet pumpkin purée with paper towel first, or the filling may weep.
  • A crack in the centre means it baked too long; take it out while the middle still wobbles.

Where it comes from

Pumpkin pie · New England · North America · 1700s

Pumpkins and other squash are native to the Americas and were grown by Indigenous peoples long before European settlement. English cooks were already baking pumpkin into pies in the 1600s, and Amelia Simmons's 'American Cookery' (1796) printed a pumpkin custard baked in a crust much like today's. Through the 1800s the pie became firmly tied to the New England Thanksgiving table.
