Strawberry-rhubarb pie

Mostly rhubarb with a smaller share of strawberries, lightly set with xanthan gum under an almond-pastry lattice.

Dairy-free option 4.1 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 45 min · cook 50 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

Pastry

  • 2½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (240 g)
  • 1 tbsp psyllium husk powder
  • ½ tsp xanthan gum
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 6 tbsp unsalted butter (85 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg

Filling

  • 300 g rhubarb, cut in 1 cm (1/2 in) pieces
  • 150 g strawberries, hulled and quartered
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ¾ tsp xanthan gum
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Glaze

  • 1 large egg, beaten

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.1 g4.1 g
Total carbohydrate20.3 g20.3 g
Fibre4.2 g4.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.0 g12.0 g
Sugars2.3 g2.3 g
Fat20.6 g20.4 g
Protein6.9 g6.8 g
Energy235 kcal235 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, psyllium husk powder, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Pulse the almond flour, psyllium, xanthan gum, erythritol and salt in a food processor. Add the butter and egg and pulse until the dough comes together. Divide into two-thirds and one-third, flatten each into a disc and chill 30 minutes.
  2. Roll the larger piece between two sheets of parchment to a 28 cm (11 in) round and flip it into a 23 cm (9 in) pie plate. Patch any cracks with your fingers. Roll the smaller piece to a rectangle and cut 2 cm (3/4 in) strips.
  3. Heat the oven to 190 °C (375 °F). Toss the rhubarb and strawberries with the lemon juice and vanilla. Whisk the erythritol and xanthan gum together and sprinkle over the fruit, tossing so no clumps remain.
  4. Pile the fruit into the crust. Lay the strips over it in a lattice, pressing the ends onto the rim, and brush with beaten egg.
  5. Bake 15 minutes, then lower to 175 °C (350 °F) and bake 30–35 minutes more, until the filling bubbles in the centre. Cover the edge with foil if it darkens.
  6. Cool at least 3 hours so the juices set before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter makes a slightly softer pastry; keep the lattice strips well chilled so they hold their shape.

Tips

  • Whisking the xanthan gum into the sweetener first is the only way to keep it from forming slimy lumps.
  • Rhubarb is the low-carb backbone here; adding more strawberries raises the carbs quickly.

Where it comes from

Fruit pie · American Midwest · North America · 1800s

Rhubarb was grown for centuries as a medicinal root before its stalks became a common food in Britain and North America in the 1800s. Americans baked it so often that it was nicknamed 'pie plant'. Strawberries ripen in the same late-spring weeks, and the two became a classic pairing of American home baking in the 1900s.

See it on the map

Tarte au citron

A crisp, sweet almond pastry shell filled with a bright, smooth lemon cream, finished with butter blended in after cooking.

Dairy-free option 2.5 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 40 min · cook 30 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Sweet pastry

  • 1¾ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (168 g)
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg yolk

Lemon cream

  • 3 large eggs
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • ½ cup fresh lemon juice (122 g)
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • 6 tbsp unsalted butter (85 g), soft, cubed

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.5 g2.5 g
Total carbohydrate12.7 g12.6 g
Fibre1.8 g1.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.4 g8.4 g
Sugars1.2 g1.2 g
Fat22.2 g22.0 g
Protein5.9 g5.8 g
Energy233 kcal232 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Pulse the almond flour, powdered erythritol and salt in a food processor. Add the cold butter and yolk and pulse until the dough clumps. Press it evenly into a 23 cm (9 in) tart tin with a removable base and chill 20 minutes.
  2. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Prick the base, bake 12–15 minutes until evenly golden, and cool. Cover the edge with foil if it darkens.
  3. Whisk the eggs, powdered erythritol, lemon juice and zest in a heatproof bowl set over simmering water. Whisk constantly until the cream thickens and reaches about 80 °C (176 °F), 8–10 minutes.
  4. Let it cool to about 60 °C (140 °F), then blend in the soft butter with a stick blender until glossy. Strain out the zest if you want it perfectly smooth.
  5. Pour into the cooled shell, smooth the top and refrigerate at least 4 hours.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter gives a slightly lighter, more lemony cream; the pastry is a little more fragile, so chill it well before baking.

Tips

  • Blending the butter in at 60 °C, rather than cooking it in, is what makes the cream dense and silky.
  • Almond pastry tears easily: press it into the tin rather than rolling it.

Where it comes from

Lemon tart · France · Western Europe · by the 1900s

Lemons have been grown along the Mediterranean since the Middle Ages, and lemon creams in pastry were made in both French and English kitchens long before anyone recorded who first made them. The tarte au citron, a crisp sweet pastry with a tart lemon cream, is now a fixture of French pâtisseries; no single town or baker can be credited with it.

See it on the map

Puddings & custards

Cauliflower kheer

Finely riced cauliflower stands in for rice in a slow-simmered milk pudding perfumed with cardamom and saffron and topped with pistachios.

Dairy-free option 3.5 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 bowl prep 15 min · cook 40 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups cauliflower (160 g), riced very finely
  • 3 cups unsweetened almond milk (720 g)
  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g)
  • ⅛ tsp saffron threads
  • ½ tsp ground cardamom
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp xanthan gum
  • ½ tsp rose water, optional

To serve

  • 2 tbsp pistachios, shelled (15 g), chopped
  • 2 tbsp almonds (18 g), slivered

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.5 g4.2 g
Total carbohydrate13.0 g13.8 g
Fibre1.6 g1.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.0 g8.0 g
Sugars2.4 g2.5 g
Fat11.0 g10.8 g
Protein2.7 g2.8 g
Energy126 kcal124 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, rose water, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Steam or microwave the riced cauliflower for 3 minutes, then squeeze it dry in a clean towel. This removes most of the cabbagey flavour.
  2. Bring the almond milk, cream and saffron to a simmer in a heavy pan. Add the cauliflower and simmer gently, stirring often, for 30–35 minutes, until reduced by about a third and the cauliflower is very soft.
  3. Stir in the cardamom, erythritol and salt. Sprinkle in the xanthan gum while whisking and simmer 2 minutes more to thicken.
  4. Take off the heat and stir in the rose water if using. Serve warm, or chill for an hour or more; it thickens as it cools.
  5. Top with the pistachios and almonds.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream makes a richer, slightly coconut-scented kheer, close to South Indian coconut-milk payasam.

Tips

  • Rice the cauliflower as fine as couscous; larger pieces stay crunchy and never feel like rice.

Where it comes from

Rice pudding (kheer) · India · South Asia · ancient

Rice simmered in milk and sweetened is one of the oldest sweets of the Indian subcontinent, known in the north as kheer and in the south as payasam, where it is a classic temple offering. Cardamom, saffron and nuts are traditional flavourings. Using cauliflower in place of rice is a modern low-carb adaptation, not a traditional dish.

See it on the map

Chocolate pots de crème

Small, dense chocolate custards baked in a water bath; richer and smoother than a mousse.

Dairy-free option 4.1 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 pot prep 20 min · cook 35 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups heavy whipping cream (357 g)
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • 70 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), finely chopped
  • 5 large egg yolks
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp instant espresso powder

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.1 g6.4 g
Total carbohydrate16.7 g19.0 g
Fibre2.0 g2.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.6 g10.6 g
Sugars2.2 g2.4 g
Fat31.3 g30.6 g
Protein5.5 g5.8 g
Energy322 kcal316 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C (300 °F). Set six small ramekins or pots in a roasting tin.
  2. Heat the cream and almond milk until steaming, pour over the chocolate and leave 2 minutes, then stir until smooth.
  3. Whisk the yolks, erythritol, vanilla, salt and espresso powder. Slowly whisk in the chocolate cream and strain into a jug.
  4. Pour into the pots and add hot water to the tin to come halfway up. Cover the tin loosely with foil.
  5. Bake 30–35 minutes, until set at the edge with a slight wobble in the centre. Cool, then refrigerate at least 4 hours.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream makes an equally dense custard; the chocolate covers most of the coconut flavour.

Tips

  • Unsweetened chocolate keeps the carbs low; taste the warm chocolate cream and add a few drops of stevia if you like it sweeter.

Where it comes from

Baked custard · France · Western Europe · 1700s

Pots de crème are named for the small lidded cups they were baked and served in. French porcelain makers were producing sets of these custard cups by the 1700s, a sign of how fashionable the little custards were at table. Vanilla, coffee and chocolate are the classic flavours.

Crema catalana

A stovetop custard infused with lemon zest and cinnamon, chilled in shallow dishes and burnt on top to a thin crackling crust.

Dairy-free option 2.5 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 dish prep 20 min · cook 15 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups unsweetened almond milk (360 g)
  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g)
  • 2 tsp lemon zest, pared in strips if you can
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 6 large egg yolks
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • ¼ tsp xanthan gum
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Topping

  • 3 tbsp allulose (36 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.5 g4.0 g
Total carbohydrate19.5 g21.0 g
Fibre0.4 g0.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.6 g16.6 g
Sugars1.8 g1.9 g
Fat19.4 g19.0 g
Protein4.1 g4.3 g
Energy204 kcal200 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Heat the almond milk, cream, lemon zest and cinnamon until steaming. Cover and leave 15 minutes to infuse, then strain.
  2. Whisk the yolks, erythritol, xanthan gum and salt in a saucepan until smooth. Gradually whisk in the warm milk.
  3. Cook over low heat, stirring constantly with a spatula, until the custard thickens and coats the spatula, about 80 °C (176 °F). Do not let it boil.
  4. Pour into six shallow dishes, cool, then refrigerate at least 4 hours.
  5. Just before serving, sprinkle each evenly with allulose and burn the top with a torch until amber.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream sets a little firmer and adds a coconut note that sits alongside the lemon and cinnamon.

Tips

  • Unlike crème brûlée, this custard is cooked on the stove, not baked, and is traditionally flavoured with lemon and cinnamon instead of vanilla.

Where it comes from

Stirred custard · Catalonia, Spain · Southern Europe · medieval (claimed)

Crema catalana is a stirred custard of Catalonia, flavoured with lemon and cinnamon and caramelised with a hot iron. Catalans claim it as one of Europe's oldest custard desserts and a forerunner of crème brûlée, though its exact age is debated. It is traditionally eaten on St Joseph's Day, 19 March, and is also called crema de Sant Josep.

See it on the map

Crème brûlée

A rich vanilla cream custard baked gently in a water bath and finished with a thin, crackly layer of torched allulose.

Dairy-free option 2.9 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 ramekin prep 20 min · cook 40 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • 2 cups heavy whipping cream (476 g)
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 5 large egg yolks
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)

Topping

  • 3 tbsp allulose (36 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.9 g6.0 g
Total carbohydrate19.5 g22.6 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.6 g16.6 g
Sugars2.6 g2.9 g
Fat32.4 g31.5 g
Protein4.5 g4.9 g
Energy324 kcal316 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C (300 °F). Set six 120 ml (4 oz) ramekins in a roasting tin.
  2. Heat the cream with the vanilla and salt until steaming, but not boiling.
  3. Whisk the yolks and erythritol until smooth. Pour in the hot cream in a thin stream, whisking gently so you do not beat in air. Strain into a jug.
  4. Divide among the ramekins. Pour hot water into the tin to come halfway up the ramekins.
  5. Bake 30–40 minutes, until the edges are set and the centres wobble like jelly. Lift out of the water, cool, then refrigerate at least 4 hours.
  6. Just before serving, blot any moisture off the tops, sprinkle each evenly with allulose and torch until amber. Let it harden for a minute.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream sets a little softer and tastes of coconut; add a pinch more salt and an extra yolk if you want a firmer set.

Tips

  • Allulose caramelises fast and burns fast; keep the torch moving and stop at amber.
  • Erythritol will not melt into a proper crust, which is why the topping uses allulose.

Where it comes from

Baked custard · France (disputed with England) · Western Europe · 1600s

Where crème brûlée began is disputed. A burnt-cream recipe under the French name appears in a cookbook by the French chef François Massialot at the end of the 1600s, while England claims a 'burnt cream' long associated with Trinity College, Cambridge, and Catalonia has its own crema catalana. The dessert became a restaurant standard worldwide in the 1980s.

See it on the map

Haupia

Firm squares of lightly sweet coconut pudding, set with agar instead of starch; dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 1.5 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 square prep 10 min · cook 10 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

  • 2½ cups full-fat coconut milk (canned) (565 g)
  • ½ cup water (118 g)
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 1½ tsp agar powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.5 g
Total carbohydrate6.8 g
Fibre0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose5.3 g
Sugars0.0 g
Fat10.0 g
Protein1.0 g
Energy95 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, water.

Method

  1. Lightly oil a 20 cm (8 in) square pan.
  2. Whisk the agar into the cold water in a saucepan until there are no lumps. Whisk in the coconut milk, erythritol and salt.
  3. Bring to a boil over medium heat, whisking, and simmer 2 minutes so the agar fully dissolves.
  4. Off the heat, stir in the vanilla and pour into the pan.
  5. Cool to room temperature; it sets as it cools. Refrigerate at least 2 hours, then cut into 12 squares.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Agar must boil to work; simply warming it leaves the pudding liquid.
  • For a softer, spoonable version reduce the agar to 1 teaspoon.

Where it comes from

Coconut pudding · Hawaii · Oceania · traditional (pre-1800s)

Haupia is a traditional Hawaiian dessert of coconut cream thickened with pia, the starch of Polynesian arrowroot. As pia became scarce in the 1900s, cooks switched to cornstarch, which is how most haupia is made today. It is a fixture of the lūʻau table and is often served alongside kūlolo, a taro pudding.

See it on the map

Leche flan

The Philippine yolk-rich, extra-dense caramel custard, steamed in an oval mould and finished with lemon zest in place of calamansi.

Dairy-free option 1.8 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 slice prep 20 min · cook 40 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

Caramel

  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)

Custard

  • 10 large egg yolks
  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g)
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ½ tsp lemon zest

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.8 g3.0 g
Total carbohydrate17.7 g18.9 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.8 g15.8 g
Sugars1.2 g1.3 g
Fat16.5 g16.2 g
Protein4.3 g4.4 g
Energy178 kcal175 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, water.

Method

  1. Cook the allulose and water in a small pan, swirling, until amber. Pour into an oval llanera or an 18–20 cm (7–8 in) round pan and tilt to coat.
  2. Stir the yolks gently with a spoon, without whisking in air. Stir in the cream, almond milk, erythritol, vanilla and lemon zest until the erythritol dissolves.
  3. Strain twice onto the caramel and cover the mould tightly with foil.
  4. Steam over gently simmering water, lid on, for 30–40 minutes, until a knife comes out clean and the centre is just set. Keep the heat low: rapid boiling makes holes.
  5. Cool, then refrigerate at least 6 hours. Run a knife round the edge and invert onto a plate.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream makes a very dense, slightly coconut-flavoured flan, which is close in spirit to the coconut flans of the region.

Tips

  • Save the 10 whites for a meringue or an angel-food-style cake.
  • Straining twice is what gives the smooth, bubble-free texture leche flan is known for.

Where it comes from

Flan · Manila, Philippines · Southeast Asia · Spanish colonial era

Leche flan descends from the Spanish flan brought to the Philippines during more than three centuries of Spanish rule. Filipino cooks made it denser and richer, with many yolks and, later, canned condensed and evaporated milk. A widely repeated story links its yolk-heavy recipe to egg whites used in building mortar, but that remains unproven.

See it on the map

Malva pudding

A light, spongy baked pudding that soaks up a hot buttery cream sauce as it comes out of the oven, served warm.

Dairy-free option 3.0 g net carbs Serves 9 · 1 square prep 20 min · cook 45 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

Apricot jam

  • 2 fresh apricots, stoned and finely chopped
  • 1 tbsp allulose (12 g)
  • 1 tbsp water (15 g)

Pudding

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp baking soda
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), melted
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • 1 tsp fresh lemon juice
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Sauce

  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ¼ cup water (59 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.0 g3.5 g
Total carbohydrate20.2 g20.7 g
Fibre1.8 g1.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.4 g15.4 g
Sugars2.1 g2.1 g
Fat22.1 g21.7 g
Protein5.4 g5.4 g
Energy235 kcal234 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, water.

Method

  1. Cook the apricots, allulose and water in a small pan over low heat, stirring and mashing, for 6–8 minutes until thick and jammy. Cool.
  2. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Grease a 20 cm (8 in) square baking dish.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, baking powder, baking soda and salt.
  4. Beat the eggs and erythritol until pale and thick, about 3 minutes. Beat in the apricot jam, melted butter, almond milk, lemon juice and vanilla, then fold in the dry ingredients.
  5. Pour into the dish and bake 25–30 minutes, until well risen, golden and a skewer comes out clean.
  6. In the last 5 minutes, heat the cream, butter, allulose and water in a saucepan until the butter melts and it just simmers.
  7. Prick the hot pudding all over with a skewer and pour the hot sauce slowly over it. Leave 15 minutes to soak before serving warm.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With coconut cream and dairy-free butter the sauce soaks in just as well; the flavour is a little less buttery.

Tips

  • Two fresh apricots cooked down stand in for the spoonful of apricot jam in a traditional batter; allulose browns fast, so keep the heat low.

Where it comes from

Baked sponge pudding · Cape Town, South Africa · Sub-Saharan Africa · 1900s

Malva pudding is an Afrikaans dessert associated with the Cape and its Dutch-descended cooking. Its exact origin and the meaning of the name are not firmly recorded. It became one of South Africa's best-known desserts in the later 1900s and is now served across the country.

Panna cotta

Vanilla cream set with just enough gelatin to hold a soft wobble, served with a spoonful of crushed raspberries.

Dairy-free option 3.5 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 glass prep 15 min · cook 5 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • 2¼ tsp unflavored powdered gelatin
  • 2 cups heavy whipping cream (476 g)
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

To serve

  • 90 g raspberries
  • 2 tsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.5 g6.5 g
Total carbohydrate13.3 g16.4 g
Fibre1.0 g1.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.8 g8.8 g
Sugars3.3 g3.6 g
Fat28.9 g28.1 g
Protein3.4 g3.8 g
Energy290 kcal282 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Pour the almond milk into a small bowl, sprinkle the gelatin over it and leave 5 minutes to soften.
  2. Heat the cream, erythritol and salt until steaming and the erythritol dissolves; do not boil.
  3. Off the heat, stir in the softened gelatin until it melts completely, then the vanilla.
  4. Cool to room temperature, stirring now and then so the vanilla does not settle, then pour into six small glasses or lightly oiled moulds.
  5. Refrigerate at least 6 hours, until set.
  6. Crush the raspberries with the powdered erythritol and spoon over the top, or turn the moulds out onto plates first.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream sets slightly firmer and tastes of coconut; cut the gelatin to 2 teaspoons for a softer wobble.

Tips

  • Erythritol can recrystallise in cold cream; powdered erythritol or allulose dissolves more completely.

Where it comes from

Panna cotta · Piedmont, Italy · Southern Europe · 1900s

Panna cotta, 'cooked cream', is usually described as a traditional dessert of Piedmont in northern Italy. Despite that reputation, it is hard to find in Italian cookbooks before the second half of the 1900s, and its earlier history is not well documented. It became an international restaurant favourite from the 1990s.

Spanish flan

A silky egg custard flavoured with lemon zest and baked over an allulose caramel that turns to sauce when it is unmoulded.

Dairy-free option 1.6 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 slice prep 25 min · cook 1 h · rest 6 h

Ingredients

Caramel

  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)

Custard

  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g)
  • 1 cup unsweetened almond milk (240 g)
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • 4 large eggs
  • 2 large egg yolks
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.6 g2.7 g
Total carbohydrate17.5 g18.7 g
Fibre0.1 g0.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.8 g15.8 g
Sugars1.3 g1.4 g
Fat14.5 g14.2 g
Protein4.8 g4.9 g
Energy161 kcal158 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F). Have ready a 20 cm (8 in) round cake pan or flan mould and a larger roasting tin.
  2. Cook the allulose and water in a small pan over medium heat, swirling, until it turns amber, 5–7 minutes. Pour at once into the pan and tilt to coat the base.
  3. Warm the cream, almond milk and lemon zest until steaming. Leave 10 minutes to infuse.
  4. Whisk the eggs, yolks, erythritol, vanilla and salt without beating in air. Slowly whisk in the warm milk, then strain onto the caramel.
  5. Set the pan in the roasting tin and add hot water to come halfway up. Bake 50–60 minutes, until set at the edge and just wobbly in the centre.
  6. Cool, then refrigerate at least 6 hours. Run a thin knife around the edge, invert onto a plate with a rim, and let the caramel run down.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream gives a flan just as firm and a touch denser, with a mild coconut flavour that suits the caramel.

Tips

  • Allulose caramel goes from amber to burnt quickly and never gets as hard as sugar caramel; pour it as soon as it colours.

Where it comes from

Flan · Spain · Southern Europe · ancient (Roman roots)

The Romans made sweet and savoury custards of eggs and milk. In Spain the custard came to be baked over caramel and turned out of its mould, becoming the flan served across the country. Spanish colonists carried it to Latin America and the Philippines, where local versions developed.

Sticky toffee pudding

A soft, coffee-dark almond sponge soaked in a buttery allulose toffee sauce; no dates, so the toffee carries the flavour.

Dairy-free option 2.9 g net carbs Serves 9 · 1 square prep 25 min · cook 35 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

Sponge

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp instant espresso powder
  • ¼ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), softened
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • ¼ cup unsweetened almond milk (60 g)
  • 1½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Toffee sauce

  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.9 g3.4 g
Total carbohydrate19.1 g19.6 g
Fibre2.2 g2.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose14.1 g14.1 g
Sugars1.5 g1.6 g
Fat25.3 g24.9 g
Protein6.4 g6.3 g
Energy270 kcal268 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Grease a 20 cm (8 in) square pan and line the base with parchment.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder, salt, espresso powder and cinnamon.
  3. Beat the butter and allulose until creamy, then the eggs one at a time, then the almond milk and vanilla. Fold in the dry ingredients.
  4. Spread in the pan and bake 25–30 minutes, until springy and a skewer comes out clean. Allulose browns fast, so cover loosely with foil after 15 minutes.
  5. Meanwhile, melt the butter and allulose for the sauce, then simmer gently for 3–4 minutes, until deep golden. Whisk in the cream and salt and simmer 2 minutes more.
  6. Poke holes all over the warm sponge with a skewer and pour over half the sauce. Let it soak for 10 minutes, cut into 9 squares and serve with the rest of the sauce.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With dairy-free butter and coconut cream the sauce is a little thinner and tastes faintly of coconut, but it still caramelises well.

Tips

  • The sponge reheats well: warm it with the sauce poured over, covered, at 150 °C (300 °F) for 10 minutes.

Where it comes from

Steamed and baked sponge pudding · Cumbria, England · Northern Europe · 1970s

Sticky toffee pudding, a date sponge drenched in toffee sauce, is usually credited to the Sharrow Bay hotel on Ullswater in the Lake District, which made it famous in the 1970s. The hotel's own account was that the recipe came to it from elsewhere, so the true origin is uncertain. It belongs to the long British tradition of sponge puddings with sauce.

Vanilla-cinnamon chia pudding

Chia seeds soaked overnight in coconut and almond milk until they set into a thick, spoonable pudding; dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 5.1 g net carbs Serves 4 · 1 jar prep 10 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • ½ cup chia seeds (96 g)
  • 1 cup full-fat coconut milk (canned) (226 g)
  • 1 cup unsweetened almond milk (240 g)
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

To serve

  • 60 g raspberries

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs5.1 g
Total carbohydrate20.7 g
Fibre9.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g
Sugars1.3 g
Fat20.1 g
Protein5.5 g
Energy250 kcal

Contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Whisk the coconut milk, almond milk, erythritol, vanilla, cinnamon and salt in a bowl.
  2. Whisk in the chia seeds, wait 5 minutes, then whisk again to break up the clumps that form.
  3. Divide among four jars, cover and refrigerate at least 4 hours or overnight.
  4. Stir before serving and top each jar with a few raspberries.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • For a smooth, custard-like texture, blend the pudding after it has set.
  • It keeps 4 days in the fridge, so it is easy to make ahead for breakfasts.

Where it comes from

Chia drink and pudding · Central Mexico · Latin America & Caribbean · ancient (modern pudding form, 2000s)

Chia is native to Mexico and Central America and was a major crop of Mesoamerica; Aztec records list it among the goods paid as tribute. It was eaten as a grain and stirred into drinks, as in the chia fresca still made today. The soaked, pudding-style dish is a modern invention that spread with health-food cooking in the 2000s.

Zabaglione

Egg yolks and Marsala whisked over simmering water into a warm, airy foam, spooned over a few berries.

Dairy-free 4.4 g net carbs Serves 4 · 1 glass prep 10 min · cook 10 min

Ingredients

  • 4 large egg yolks
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • 4 tbsp dry Marsala or dry sherry (59 g)

To serve

  • 60 g raspberries
  • 40 g blueberries

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs4.4 g
Total carbohydrate11.6 g
Fibre1.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g
Sugars1.9 g
Fat4.6 g
Protein3.0 g
Energy92 kcal

Contains: eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Divide the berries among four glasses.
  2. Bring a few centimetres of water to a bare simmer in a saucepan. In a heatproof bowl that sits above, not in, the water, whisk the yolks and powdered erythritol until pale.
  3. Add the Marsala. Set the bowl over the pan and whisk constantly for 6–8 minutes, until the mixture triples in volume and holds a ribbon; it should reach about 70 °C (160 °F).
  4. Spoon the warm foam over the berries and serve at once.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • If the bowl gets too hot the yolks scramble at the edges; lift it off the heat for a few seconds while you keep whisking.
  • Not all the alcohol cooks off at this temperature. For an alcohol-free version use 4 tbsp water, 1 tsp lemon juice and ½ tsp rum extract instead of the Marsala.

Where it comes from

Zabaglione · Turin, Italy · Southern Europe · by the 1800s

Zabaglione is a foam of egg yolks, sugar and sweet wine, usually Marsala, whisked over heat. Several Italian cities claim it, and the best-known stories, which credit Turin or a Renaissance court, are legends rather than documented history. It is related to the French sabayon, which is the same technique.

Mousses & creams

Bavarian cream (bavarois)

A vanilla custard set with gelatin and lightened with whipped cream, turned out of a mould and served cold.

Dairy-free option 3.0 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 slice or small mould prep 30 min · cook 10 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

  • 3 tbsp water (45 g), cold
  • 1 tbsp unflavored powdered gelatin
  • 1 cup whole milk (244 g)
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 4 large egg yolks
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 1¼ cups heavy whipping cream (298 g), cold

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.0 g3.3 g
Total carbohydrate15.0 g15.3 g
Fibre0.0 g0.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.0 g12.0 g
Sugars2.8 g1.7 g
Fat16.7 g15.6 g
Protein4.3 g3.6 g
Energy184 kcal166 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, water.

Method

  1. Pour the cold water into a small bowl, sprinkle the gelatin over it and leave it to swell for 5 minutes. Lightly oil a 1-litre (1 quart) mould or eight small ramekins.
  2. Heat the milk until steaming. Whisk the yolks, allulose and salt in a bowl, then slowly whisk in the hot milk.
  3. Return to the pan and cook over low heat, stirring constantly, until the custard coats the back of a spoon and reads 80 °C (176 °F). Do not let it boil.
  4. Take it off the heat, stir in the softened gelatin until dissolved, then the vanilla. Strain into a bowl.
  5. Set the bowl in a larger bowl of iced water and stir now and then until the custard is cool and just beginning to thicken, like raw egg white.
  6. Whip the cream to soft peaks and fold it into the thickening custard. Pour into the mould and refrigerate at least 6 hours.
  7. To unmould, dip the mould in hot water for 5–10 seconds, run a knife round the edge and turn out onto a plate.

Dairy-free version

  • Use unsweetened almond milk instead of whole milk.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and whip only the solid part.

With almond milk and coconut cream the set is just as firm, since the gelatin does the work, but the flavour leans towards coconut and the texture is slightly denser.

Tips

  • Fold in the cream at the moment the custard starts to set: too early and it separates into layers, too late and it sets in lumps.
  • Serve with a few raspberries crushed with a pinch of sweetener.

Where it comes from

Bavarian cream · Paris · Western Europe · early 1800s

Despite the name, the bavarois belongs to French classical cookery, and how it came to be named after Bavaria is not known. Versions appear in French cookbooks by the early nineteenth century, and the great Parisian chefs of that period, Antonin Carême among them, made it a standard of the cold dessert repertoire.

See it on the map

Brazilian lime mousse (mousse de limão)

A sharp, airy lime mousse that keeps the tang of the Brazilian classic, made with cream, cream cheese and allulose in place of condensed milk.

Dairy-free option 3.6 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1/2 cup prep 20 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

  • 1 tsp unflavored powdered gelatin
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g), cold
  • 115 g full-fat cream cheese, softened
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g)
  • ⅓ cup fresh lime juice (79 g), from 3–4 limes
  • 1 tbsp lime zest, plus extra to serve
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 1¼ cups heavy whipping cream (298 g), cold

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.6 g6.2 g
Total carbohydrate16.7 g19.5 g
Fibre0.2 g0.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose13.0 g13.0 g
Sugars2.4 g2.3 g
Fat24.5 g22.2 g
Protein3.1 g3.1 g
Energy245 kcal229 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), water.

Method

  1. Sprinkle the gelatin over the cold water and leave 5 minutes to swell, then melt it by warming 10–15 seconds in the microwave or over hot water. Do not let it boil.
  2. Beat the cream cheese with the powdered erythritol and allulose until completely smooth. Beat in the lime juice a little at a time, then the zest, salt and melted gelatin.
  3. In a separate cold bowl whip the cream to soft peaks.
  4. Stir a third of the cream into the lime mixture to loosen it, then gently fold in the rest until no streaks remain.
  5. Spoon into six glasses and chill at least 3 hours, until set. Finish with a little grated lime zest.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and whip only the thick part.

Whipped coconut cream holds less air than dairy cream, so the mousse is denser and the coconut flavour sits alongside the lime; the gelatin is what keeps it set.

Tips

  • In the original, the acid in the lime juice thickens the condensed milk on its own; without condensed milk the gelatin does that job.
  • Zest the limes before juicing them, and avoid the bitter white pith.

Where it comes from

Mousse · Brazil · Latin America & Caribbean · 1900s

Mousse de limão is one of Brazil's most familiar home desserts, built on sweetened condensed milk, cream and the juice and zest of limes. Condensed milk became a mainstay of Brazilian sweets during the twentieth century, and this quick, no-bake mousse is a product of that kitchen; it has no single recorded inventor.

See it on the map

Chocolate mousse

The bistro mousse made the old way: melted chocolate, egg yolks and beaten whites, nothing else to hide behind.

Dairy-free 3.3 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 small glass (about 1/2 cup) prep 25 min · cook 5 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • 115 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), chopped
  • 2 tbsp coconut oil (27 g)
  • 3 tbsp strong brewed coffee or espresso (45 g), hot
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • 4 large egg yolks, at room temperature
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 4 large egg whites, at room temperature
  • ¼ tsp cream of tartar
  • 10 drops liquid stevia, optional, to taste

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.3 g
Total carbohydrate16.6 g
Fibre3.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.0 g
Sugars0.5 g
Fat17.1 g
Protein6.7 g
Energy203 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: liquid stevia, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Melt the chocolate and coconut oil together in a heatproof bowl set over barely simmering water, stirring until smooth. Take the bowl off the heat.
  2. Whisk in the hot coffee and all but 2 tbsp of the powdered erythritol. The mixture will tighten, then loosen again as you keep whisking.
  3. Let it cool for 5 minutes, then whisk in the yolks one at a time, followed by the vanilla, salt and stevia. Taste: unsweetened chocolate needs the sweetener adjusted to your liking now, before the whites go in.
  4. In a clean bowl, whip the whites with the cream of tartar to soft peaks, then add the remaining 2 tbsp erythritol and whip to firm, glossy peaks.
  5. Stir a quarter of the whites into the chocolate to lighten it, then fold in the rest in two additions with a large spatula, stopping as soon as no white streaks remain.
  6. Spoon into six small glasses and refrigerate at least 4 hours, until set.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • The eggs are not cooked; use very fresh eggs, or pasteurised eggs if serving anyone vulnerable.
  • The chocolate must be warm but not hot when the yolks go in, or they will scramble; just warm to the lip is right.

Where it comes from

Chocolate mousse · Paris · Western Europe · 1700s

French cookery books of the 1700s already describe light, whipped chocolate preparations under the name mousse. The plain version of chocolate folded with beaten eggs became a fixture of French home and bistro cooking in the twentieth century, and from there spread worldwide.

See it on the map

Eton mess

Broken crisp meringue, softly whipped cream and lightly sweetened strawberries, folded together just before serving.

Dairy-free option 4.0 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 glass prep 20 min · cook 1 h 30 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Meringue

  • 2 large egg whites, at room temperature
  • ¼ tsp cream of tartar
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Strawberries

  • 250 g strawberries, hulled and quartered
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)
  • 1 tsp fresh lemon juice

Cream

  • 1¼ cups heavy whipping cream (298 g), cold
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.0 g5.9 g
Total carbohydrate14.8 g16.7 g
Fibre0.8 g0.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose9.9 g9.9 g
Sugars3.6 g3.8 g
Fat18.0 g17.5 g
Protein2.9 g3.1 g
Energy191 kcal186 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 110 °C (225 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Whip the whites with the cream of tartar to soft peaks. Add the powdered erythritol a spoonful at a time, whipping until the meringue is stiff and glossy, then beat in the vanilla.
  3. Spread in rough mounds on the sheet and bake 90 minutes. Turn off the oven and leave the meringue inside to dry for an hour.
  4. Meanwhile, toss the strawberries with the erythritol and lemon juice and leave for 20 minutes to draw out their juice. Crush about a quarter of them with a fork.
  5. Whip the cream with the powdered erythritol to soft peaks.
  6. Just before serving, break the meringue into bite-sized pieces and fold it loosely through the cream with the berries and their juice. Spoon into glasses and serve at once.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and whip only the solid part.

Whipped coconut cream is heavier than dairy cream and tastes of coconut, which suits the strawberries; fold more gently, as it deflates if overworked.

Tips

  • Erythritol meringue stays crisp only for a day or two and softens fast in humid weather; store it airtight and assemble at the last minute.

Where it comes from

Eton mess · Eton · Northern Europe · 1800s

Eton mess takes its name from Eton College, where it has long been served at the school's summer events, including the annual cricket match against Harrow. A dish under that name was recorded by the late nineteenth century, though early versions were simply strawberries or bananas with cream; meringue appears to have become standard later.

See it on the map

Lemon posset

Three ingredients: cream boiled with sweetener, then set by nothing more than lemon juice into something silky and sharp.

Dairy-free option 3.2 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 small ramekin prep 10 min · cook 10 min · rest 5 h

Ingredients

  • 2 cups heavy whipping cream (476 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • ⅓ cup fresh lemon juice (81 g), from about 2 lemons

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.2 g6.3 g
Total carbohydrate19.3 g22.4 g
Fibre0.1 g0.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.0 g16.0 g
Sugars2.7 g3.0 g
Fat28.7 g27.8 g
Protein2.3 g2.7 g
Energy280 kcal272 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Put the cream, allulose and zest in a wide saucepan and bring slowly to the boil, stirring until the allulose dissolves.
  2. Let it boil gently for 3 minutes, watching that it does not rise over the rim. This reduction is what gives the posset its body.
  3. Take the pan off the heat and stir in the lemon juice. Leave it to stand for 10 minutes.
  4. Strain through a fine sieve into a jug to remove the zest, then pour into six small ramekins or glasses.
  5. Cool, then refrigerate uncovered for at least 5 hours, until set.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream does not thicken with acid the way dairy cream does, so it relies on chilling to set and will be softer and spoonable rather than firm; simmer it a little longer and chill overnight.

Tips

  • Allulose rather than erythritol keeps the posset smooth: erythritol tends to recrystallise in a cold cream.

Where it comes from

Posset · England · Northern Europe · medieval

In medieval and Tudor England a posset was a hot drink of milk or cream curdled with wine or ale, often spiced and taken as a remedy. The modern lemon posset, a set cream thickened by citrus juice, is a much later descendant that became a popular British dessert in the twentieth century.

See it on the map

Sugar alcohols & allulose5.0 g5.0 g
Sugars2.0 g2.3 g
Fat21.5 g20.9 g
Protein1.7 g2.0 g
Energy214 kcal208 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Stir the lemon zest, lemon juice, white wine and powdered erythritol together in a large bowl and leave for 15 minutes so the zest flavours the liquid.
  2. Pour in the cold cream and add the nutmeg.
  3. Whisk slowly, by hand or on low speed, until the cream thickens enough to hold soft folds. Stop before it is stiff: the acid makes it thicken suddenly, and it goes grainy if pushed further.
  4. Spoon into small glasses, grate a little more nutmeg over, and chill for 1–2 hours before serving.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and use only the solid part.

Coconut cream gives a thicker, denser syllabub with a coconut note and does not separate on standing the way dairy syllabub can.

Tips

  • Older recipes used sweet wine or sherry; a dry white keeps the carbs down, and dry sherry works the same way.

Where it comes from

Syllabub · England · Northern Europe · 1500s

Syllabub was popular in England from the sixteenth century onward. Early versions were frothy drinks of cream or milk mixed with wine or cider; over the following two centuries cooks began whipping it into a thicker cream that could be eaten with a spoon, and by the 1700s this 'everlasting' syllabub was a fashionable dessert.

See it on the map

Tiramisù

Layers of coffee-soaked almond sponge and a cooked mascarpone cream, finished with a heavy dusting of cocoa.

Dairy-free option 4.8 g net carbs Serves 9 · 1 square prep 45 min · cook 20 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

Almond sponge

  • 4 large eggs, separated
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Coffee soak

  • ¾ cup strong brewed coffee or espresso (178 g), cooled
  • 1 tsp rum extract
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)

Mascarpone cream

  • 3 large egg yolks
  • ½ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (60 g)
  • 450 g mascarpone, cold
  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g), cold

To finish

  • 2 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (11 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.8 g8.3 g
Total carbohydrate21.4 g25.4 g
Fibre1.5 g2.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.0 g15.0 g
Sugars3.0 g2.6 g
Fat40.5 g31.2 g
Protein9.5 g9.6 g
Energy427 kcal354 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, mascarpone, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), rum extract.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a 23 x 33 cm (9 x 13 in) baking pan with parchment.
  2. Beat the 4 yolks with half the erythritol and the vanilla until pale and thick. In a clean bowl, whip the whites to soft peaks, add the remaining erythritol and whip until firm.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, baking powder and salt together, fold into the yolks, then fold in the whites in three additions. Spread evenly in the pan and bake 15–18 minutes, until golden and springy. Cool completely, then cut into two pieces that each fit a 20 cm (8 in) square dish.
  4. Stir the coffee, rum extract and 1 tbsp erythritol together.
  5. For the cream, whisk the 3 yolks and powdered erythritol in a heatproof bowl over simmering water until the mixture is thick, pale and reads 71 °C (160 °F), about 5 minutes. Cool to room temperature.
  6. Beat the mascarpone just until smooth, then fold in the cooled yolk mixture. In another bowl whip the cream to soft peaks and fold it in. Do not overbeat mascarpone, which turns grainy.
  7. Lay one sponge in the dish, brush it generously with half the coffee, and spread over half the cream. Repeat with the second sponge, the rest of the coffee and the rest of the cream.
  8. Cover and refrigerate at least 8 hours. Sift the cocoa over just before serving.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of mascarpone — use a firm block style.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and whip only the solid part.

The dairy-free cream is tangier and a little denser than mascarpone; it sets well after an overnight chill but cuts less cleanly on the first day.

Tips

  • The almond sponge drinks coffee faster than ladyfingers, so brush rather than dip it.

Where it comes from

Tiramisù · Treviso · Southern Europe · 1960s–1970s

Tiramisù is a recent dessert, and where it was invented is disputed. Treviso in the Veneto, and a restaurant there in particular, is the claim most often repeated, dating it to the late 1960s or early 1970s; Friuli, to the north-east, points to earlier layered coffee-and-cream desserts in its mountain towns. Neither claim has been settled, but the dish spread rapidly through Italy in the 1980s and then abroad.

See it on the map

Frozen desserts

Affogato

A scoop of quick vanilla ice cream 'drowned' in a shot of hot espresso at the table.

Dairy-free option 2.7 g net carbs Serves 4 · 1 scoop with 1 espresso prep 15 min · cook 10 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

Quick vanilla ice cream

  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g)
  • 2 large egg yolks
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 1½ tsp pure vanilla extract

To serve

  • ½ cup strong brewed coffee or espresso (118 g), 4 freshly made espresso shots

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.7 g5.0 g
Total carbohydrate18.5 g20.8 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose15.8 g15.8 g
Sugars2.0 g2.2 g
Fat23.8 g23.1 g
Protein3.1 g3.3 g
Energy243 kcal237 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Heat the cream until steaming. Whisk the yolks, allulose and salt, then slowly whisk in the hot cream.
  2. Return to the pan and stir over low heat until it coats a spatula and reaches 80 °C (176 °F). Strain, stir in the vanilla and chill until cold.
  3. Churn in an ice-cream maker, or pour into a shallow container and freeze, stirring hard every 30 minutes for 3 hours. Freeze until firm.
  4. To serve, put a firm scoop into each of four small, chilled cups and pour a hot espresso over it at the table.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut ice cream melts faster under hot coffee and gives a creamier, coconut-flavoured pool; scoop it straight from a very cold freezer.

Where it comes from

Affogato · Italy · Southern Europe · 1900s

Affogato, Italian for 'drowned', is a scoop of gelato with espresso poured over it. It belongs to Italy's twentieth-century espresso-bar culture; no single inventor or date is documented, and it became widely known outside Italy only from the late twentieth century.

See it on the map

Almond semifreddo

A no-churn frozen mousse of cooked yolks and whipped cream with toasted almonds, sliced like a cake.

Dairy-free option 2.0 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 35 min · cook 10 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

  • ½ cup almonds (72 g), chopped
  • 4 large egg yolks
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ½ tsp almond extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 1½ cups heavy whipping cream (357 g), cold

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.0 g3.3 g
Total carbohydrate12.5 g13.8 g
Fibre0.9 g0.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose9.6 g9.6 g
Sugars1.4 g1.6 g
Fat18.3 g17.9 g
Protein3.6 g3.8 g
Energy190 kcal186 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, almond extract, water.

Method

  1. Line a 23 x 13 cm (9 x 5 in) loaf pan with plastic film, leaving an overhang.
  2. Toast the almonds in a dry pan until fragrant and lightly golden. Cool.
  3. Whisk the yolks, allulose and water in a heatproof bowl over simmering water until thick, pale and 71 °C (160 °F), about 6 minutes. Take off the heat and beat until cool and doubled in volume, then beat in the vanilla, almond extract and salt.
  4. Whip the cream to soft peaks. Fold it into the yolk mixture with three quarters of the almonds.
  5. Pour into the pan, scatter the remaining almonds over, cover with the overhanging film and freeze at least 8 hours.
  6. Turn out and slice with a knife dipped in hot water. Let slices soften 5 minutes before serving.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and whip only the solid part.

Whipped coconut cream gives a firmer, slightly icier semifreddo; take it out of the freezer 10–15 minutes before slicing.

Where it comes from

Semifreddo · Italy · Southern Europe · 1900s

Semifreddo, Italian for 'half-cold', is a family of still-frozen desserts made from a cooked egg foam folded with whipped cream, which freezes soft without churning. It has relatives in the French parfait and frozen mousses, and was well established in Italian cookery by the twentieth century.

Coconut kulfi

A dairy-free kulfi: coconut milk slowly reduced with cardamom and saffron, frozen dense and unchurned with pistachios.

Dairy-free 4.7 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 small mould prep 10 min · cook 40 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

  • 800 g full-fat coconut milk (canned), two cans, shaken
  • ½ cup coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (120 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • ¼ tsp saffron threads
  • ¾ tsp ground cardamom
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup pistachios, shelled (41 g), chopped
  • ½ tsp rose water, optional

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs4.7 g
Total carbohydrate17.3 g
Fibre0.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.0 g
Sugars0.9 g
Fat28.9 g
Protein3.6 g
Energy280 kcal

Contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), rose water.

Method

  1. Pour the coconut milk and coconut cream into a wide, heavy pan and bring to a simmer.
  2. Simmer over low heat, stirring often and scraping the sides and base, until reduced by about a third, 30–40 minutes.
  3. Stir in the allulose, saffron, cardamom and salt and simmer 5 minutes more. Take off the heat and stir in most of the pistachios and the rose water, if using.
  4. Cool to room temperature, then pour into kulfi moulds, small paper cups or ice-lolly moulds. Sprinkle the remaining pistachios on top.
  5. Freeze at least 6 hours. To unmould, dip briefly in warm water.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Kulfi is not churned, so it is meant to be dense; scraping the pan as it reduces gives the faint caramel note of the traditional milk version.

Where it comes from

Kulfi · Delhi · South Asia · 1500s

Kulfi is usually traced to the Mughal era in northern India, when milk was boiled down until thick, flavoured with nuts and saffron, and frozen in sealed metal cones packed in ice and salt. It remains a street and festival sweet across the Indian subcontinent. Traditional kulfi is made from dairy milk; this version uses coconut milk instead.

Coconut-strawberry paletas

Mexican-style ice pops of creamy coconut milk with chunks of strawberry and a squeeze of lime.

Dairy-free 3.0 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 ice pop prep 15 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

  • 400 g full-fat coconut milk (canned), one can, shaken
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lime juice (15 g)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 200 g strawberries, hulled and finely diced

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.0 g
Total carbohydrate11.4 g
Fibre0.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose7.9 g
Sugars1.3 g
Fat10.7 g
Protein1.2 g
Energy111 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Warm a quarter of the coconut milk with the allulose just until dissolved. Stir it back into the rest with the lime juice, vanilla and salt.
  2. Divide the strawberries among eight ice-pop moulds, then pour in the coconut mixture, leaving a little room at the top.
  3. Freeze 1 hour, insert the sticks, and freeze at least 5 hours more.
  4. Run the moulds briefly under warm water to release.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Mash half the berries and keep half in pieces for streaks of colour as well as bites of fruit.

Where it comes from

Paletas · Tocumbo, Michoacán · Latin America & Caribbean · 1940s

Paletas, Mexican ice pops made from fresh fruit or cream, spread across Mexico from the mid-twentieth century through small shops called paleterías. The small town of Tocumbo in Michoacán is closely associated with them, as many of the country's paleterías were founded by families from there.

Gelato al cioccolato

Dense, deeply chocolatey Italian-style gelato with more milk than cream, so the chocolate flavour comes through clean.

Dairy-free option 5.4 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/2 cup prep 15 min · cook 10 min · rest 7 h

Ingredients

  • 2 cups whole milk (488 g)
  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g)
  • ⅓ cup Dutch-process cocoa powder (28 g)
  • ⅔ cup allulose (129 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp xanthan gum
  • 60 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), finely chopped
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.4 g4.8 g
Total carbohydrate23.9 g23.2 g
Fibre2.4 g2.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.1 g16.1 g
Sugars3.7 g0.7 g
Fat11.6 g22.4 g
Protein4.0 g3.3 g
Energy147 kcal228 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Whisk the cocoa, allulose, salt and xanthan gum together in a dry saucepan so the xanthan cannot clump.
  2. Whisk in the milk and cream a little at a time to make a smooth paste and then a liquid. Heat, whisking, until it just reaches a simmer and has thickened slightly, about 5 minutes.
  3. Take it off the heat, add the chopped chocolate and vanilla, and whisk until the chocolate has melted completely.
  4. Cool over iced water, then refrigerate at least 4 hours until very cold.
  5. Churn in an ice-cream maker, then freeze for 2–3 hours. Gelato is best served slightly softer than ice cream, so let it stand 5 minutes before scooping.

Dairy-free version

  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of whole milk.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With coconut milk the gelato is richer and a little firmer; the chocolate largely covers the coconut flavour.

Where it comes from

Gelato · Naples · Southern Europe · 1600s

Iced and frozen desserts were developed in Italy during the seventeenth century, and recipes for sorbetti were printed in Naples in the 1690s. Chocolate, which reached Italy in the same century, soon became a flavour for them. Modern gelato, with its higher proportion of milk and slower churning, took shape in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries.

Lemon granita

Sicilian-style lemon ice, scraped into fine crystals as it freezes: no machine, sharp and very refreshing.

Dairy-free 1.9 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1/2 cup prep 10 min · cook 5 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

  • 2 cups water (474 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • ⅔ cup fresh lemon juice (163 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.9 g
Total carbohydrate18.0 g
Fibre0.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.0 g
Sugars0.7 g
Fat0.1 g
Protein0.1 g
Energy13 kcal

Contains: none of the common allergen groups.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, water.

Method

  1. Warm 1 cup of the water with the allulose and zest, stirring until dissolved. Take off the heat and leave 10 minutes.
  2. Strain, then stir in the remaining water and the lemon juice. Pour into a metal baking dish about 20 x 30 cm (8 x 12 in).
  3. Freeze for 45 minutes, then rake the icy edges into the middle with a fork.
  4. Repeat every 30 minutes for about 3 hours, until the whole dish is fine, fluffy crystals.
  5. Serve straight away in chilled glasses, or cover and keep frozen and rake again before serving.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Allulose gives softer crystals than erythritol, which freezes into hard shards.

Where it comes from

Granita · Catania · Southern Europe · 1800s

Sicilians long harvested snow from Mount Etna and other peaks, stored it in pits, and served it shaved with fruit syrups; a link to the sherbets of the island's Arab period is often suggested but is traditional rather than documented. The granita made by freezing and stirring a flavoured syrup, now eaten across Sicily, was established by the nineteenth century.

See it on the map

Mint chocolate chip ice cream

A dairy-free coconut base steeped with fresh mint, sharpened with peppermint and streaked with fine chocolate flecks.

Dairy-free 5.0 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/2 cup prep 20 min · cook 15 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (360 g)
  • 1 cup full-fat coconut milk (canned) (226 g)
  • 1 cup fresh mint leaves (16 g), loosely packed leaves
  • 4 large egg yolks
  • ⅔ cup allulose (129 g)
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • ½ tsp peppermint extract
  • 30 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao)
  • 30 g sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened)
  • 2 tsp coconut oil

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs5.0 g
Total carbohydrate23.7 g
Fibre1.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose17.5 g
Sugars1.6 g
Fat28.3 g
Protein4.2 g
Energy285 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), peppermint extract, sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

⚠️ Many brands contain milk fat; choose one labelled dairy-free.

Method

  1. Bring the coconut cream and coconut milk to a simmer, add the mint, take off the heat, cover and steep 30 minutes. Strain, pressing the leaves, and return the liquid to the pan.
  2. Reheat until steaming. Whisk the yolks, allulose and salt, slowly whisk in the hot coconut, then return to the pan and stir over low heat until it reaches 80 °C (176 °F) and coats a spatula.
  3. Strain, stir in the peppermint extract, and chill at least 4 hours.
  4. Melt both chocolates with the coconut oil and let it cool until barely warm but still fluid.
  5. Churn the base. In the last minute of churning, drizzle the chocolate in a thin stream into the machine; it freezes into crisp flecks. Freeze 3–4 hours before serving.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Steeping fresh mint gives a grassier, rounder flavour than extract alone; the extract adds the cool finish.
  • Fresh-mint ice cream is pale cream, not green; that is the honest colour.

Where it comes from

Mint chocolate chip ice cream · England · Northern Europe · 1970s

Mint-flavoured ices are old, but mint chocolate chip as a named flavour is often credited to a British culinary student's entry in a 1973 competition to create a dish for the wedding of Princess Anne. It became one of the most popular ice-cream flavours in Britain and North America.

See it on the map

Raspberry sorbet

Bright, seedless raspberry sorbet with a little lemon; nothing in it but fruit, water and sweetener.

Dairy-free 4.2 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1/2 cup prep 15 min · cook 5 min · rest 6 h

Ingredients

  • 450 g raspberries, fresh or frozen
  • ¾ cup water (178 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs4.2 g
Total carbohydrate25.1 g
Fibre4.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.0 g
Sugars3.4 g
Fat0.5 g
Protein0.9 g
Energy46 kcal

Contains: none of the common allergen groups.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, water.

Method

  1. Warm the water and allulose until dissolved, then cool.
  2. Blend the raspberries with the syrup, lemon juice and salt until smooth.
  3. Press through a fine sieve with the back of a spoon to remove the seeds.
  4. Refrigerate until very cold, about 2 hours, then churn in an ice-cream maker. Freeze 3–4 hours before serving.

Dairy-free as written

  • With no machine, freeze in a shallow dish and blend the frozen chunks in a food processor until smooth, then refreeze briefly.

Where it comes from

Sorbet · Naples · Southern Europe · 1600s

The word sorbet comes, through Italian sorbetto, from the Turkish şerbet and Arabic sharbat, sweet cooling drinks of the Islamic world. Frozen versions made with ice and salt were developed in Italy in the seventeenth century and quickly taken up in France, where fruit sorbets became a fixture of fine dining.

Vanilla ice cream

A rich French-style custard ice cream; allulose keeps it soft enough to scoop straight from the freezer.

Dairy-free option 3.7 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/2 cup prep 20 min · cook 15 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

  • 2 cups heavy whipping cream (476 g)
  • 1 cup whole milk (244 g)
  • 5 large egg yolks
  • ⅔ cup allulose (129 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 3 tsp pure vanilla extract (13 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.7 g5.4 g
Total carbohydrate19.8 g21.5 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose16.1 g16.1 g
Sugars3.5 g2.2 g
Fat25.3 g30.2 g
Protein4.3 g4.3 g
Energy266 kcal302 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Heat the cream and milk in a saucepan until steaming, with small bubbles at the edge.
  2. Whisk the yolks, allulose and salt in a bowl until smooth. Pour in the hot cream slowly, whisking all the time.
  3. Return to the pan and cook over low heat, stirring constantly with a spatula, until the custard coats the spatula and reads 80 °C (176 °F). Do not let it boil.
  4. Strain into a clean bowl, stir in the vanilla, and cool over iced water. Cover and refrigerate at least 4 hours, or overnight, until very cold.
  5. Churn in an ice-cream maker according to its instructions, then pack into a container and freeze for 3–4 hours before serving.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.
  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of whole milk.

A coconut base churns well and scoops easily but tastes of coconut and freezes a little harder; let it stand 10 minutes before scooping.

Tips

  • Allulose lowers the freezing point much as sugar does; erythritol freezes rock hard and recrystallises, so do not substitute it here.

Where it comes from

Ice cream · Paris · Western Europe · 1700s

Frozen sweetened creams were served at European courts from the seventeenth century, made by stirring a pot set in ice and salt. Custard bases thickened with egg yolk, the style now called French, were common in French and English recipe books by the 1700s and remain the classic method for vanilla ice cream.

See it on the map

Candy & confections

Brigadeiros

Brazil's party sweet: a fudgy chocolate caramel rolled into balls, made here from cream reduced with allulose instead of condensed milk.

Dairy-free option 1.8 g net carbs Serves 10 · 2 brigadeiros prep 25 min · cook 30 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups heavy whipping cream (357 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g)
  • 3 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (16 g)
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 30 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), finely grated, for rolling
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), for rolling

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.8 g3.1 g
Total carbohydrate13.3 g14.7 g
Fibre1.1 g1.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.4 g10.4 g
Sugars1.1 g1.3 g
Fat16.9 g17.0 g
Protein1.7 g1.9 g
Energy167 kcal169 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Simmer the cream and allulose in a heavy saucepan, stirring often, until reduced to about 3/4 cup and the colour of light caramel, 20–25 minutes. This stands in for sweetened condensed milk.
  2. Whisk in the butter, cocoa and salt and keep cooking over low heat, stirring constantly, until the mixture is thick enough that a spatula drawn across the base leaves a trail that closes slowly, 5–8 minutes.
  3. Stir in the vanilla, scrape into a buttered dish, cover and refrigerate 1 hour.
  4. Mix the grated chocolate with the powdered erythritol. With buttered hands, roll the mixture into 20 small balls and roll each in the chocolate. Set in small paper cases. Keep chilled.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.
  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter.

Reduced coconut cream makes a convincing condensed-milk substitute but gives softer brigadeiros with a coconut note; chill them longer before rolling.

Where it comes from

Brigadeiro · Rio de Janeiro · Latin America & Caribbean · 1940s

The brigadeiro appeared in Brazil in the 1940s, when sweetened condensed milk had become a staple. Its name is commonly linked to Brigadeiro (Brigadier) Eduardo Gomes, a presidential candidate in 1945, whose supporters are said to have sold the sweets to raise funds; the story is widely repeated but not firmly documented.

See it on the map

Chocolate fudge

Soft, dense chocolate fudge made by reducing cream and allulose, then beating in unsweetened chocolate.

Dairy-free option 1.1 g net carbs Serves 25 · 1 square prep 15 min · cook 20 min · rest 4 h

  • ½ cup walnuts (58 g), chopped, optional

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Line a 20 cm (8 in) square pan with parchment, leaving an overhang.
  2. Bring the cream, butter, allulose and salt to the boil in a heavy saucepan, then simmer, stirring often, until reduced by about half and thick enough to coat a spoon, 12–15 minutes. Watch it closely: allulose browns faster than sugar.
  3. Take off the heat and add the chocolate. Leave 1 minute, then stir until smooth.
  4. Beat in the powdered erythritol and vanilla with a wooden spoon for a minute or two, until the mixture thickens and loses some of its shine. Stir in the walnuts.
  5. Spread in the pan, cool, then refrigerate at least 4 hours. Lift out and cut into 25 squares.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.
  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Coconut cream reduces to a slightly softer fudge with a faint coconut flavour; keep it chilled and serve straight from the fridge.

Tips

  • Allulose keeps the fudge soft; this is a creamy refrigerator fudge, not the crumbly crystallised kind.

Where it comes from

Fudge · Poughkeepsie, New York · North America · 1880s

Fudge is an American sweet of the late nineteenth century. One of the earliest accounts links it to students at Vassar College in Poughkeepsie, who made it in the 1880s, and fudge-making soon spread through other women's colleges and seaside resorts. How it got its name is uncertain.

See it on the map

Chocolate truffles

A soft coconut-cream ganache rolled into rough balls and tossed in cocoa, dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 2.7 g net carbs Serves 12 · 2 truffles prep 30 min · cook 5 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

  • 115 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), finely chopped
  • 60 g sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened)
  • ½ cup coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (120 g)
  • ⅓ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (40 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 3 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (16 g), for rolling

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.7 g
Total carbohydrate10.7 g
Fibre2.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose5.1 g
Sugars0.5 g
Fat10.1 g
Protein2.2 g
Energy112 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

⚠️ Many brands contain milk fat; choose one labelled dairy-free.

Method

  1. Put both chocolates in a heatproof bowl.
  2. Heat the coconut cream with the powdered erythritol until it just simmers, whisking to dissolve. Pour it over the chocolate and leave for 2 minutes.
  3. Stir slowly from the centre outwards until smooth and glossy, then stir in the vanilla and salt.
  4. Cover and refrigerate about 2 hours, until firm enough to scoop.
  5. Scoop heaped teaspoons, roll quickly between cool palms, and drop into the cocoa to coat. Refrigerate in a covered box; bring to cool room temperature for 10 minutes before eating.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • If the ganache splits and looks oily, whisk in a teaspoon of warm water.
  • The rough, cocoa-dusted shape is the point: it is what gives truffles their name.

Where it comes from

Chocolate truffle · Chambéry · Western Europe · late 1800s

Chocolate truffles are named for their resemblance to the truffle fungus: knobbly, irregular and dusted in cocoa. They are often said to have been created by a confectioner in Chambéry in the 1890s, though the story is traditional rather than documented; by the twentieth century ganache truffles were a standard of French confectionery.

Marzipan

A smooth almond paste scented with rose water and bitter almond, for shaping into little fruits or dipping in chocolate.

Dairy-free 1.1 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 piece (about 20 g) prep 20 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 200 g almond flour (blanched, finely ground)
  • 100 g powdered erythritol (confectioners' style)
  • 1 large egg white, pasteurised
  • ½ tsp almond extract
  • 1 tsp rose water
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.1 g
Total carbohydrate8.6 g
Fibre1.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.2 g
Sugars0.6 g
Fat6.6 g
Protein2.9 g
Energy76 kcal

Contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), rose water.

Method

  1. Pulse the almond flour and powdered erythritol in a food processor until very fine, about 30 seconds; stop before it turns oily.
  2. Add the egg white, almond extract, rose water and salt and pulse until the mixture gathers into a ball.
  3. Turn out and knead briefly until smooth. If it is sticky, knead in a little more almond flour; if crumbly, a few drops of water.
  4. Wrap and chill 1 hour, then shape into small balls, fruits or logs. Store airtight in the fridge.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Use a pasteurised egg white, since the marzipan is not cooked.
  • Commercial marzipan is half sugar, which holds its shape; this version is softer, so keep shapes small and chill them.

Where it comes from

Marzipan · Toledo · Southern Europe · medieval

Sweet pastes of almonds and sugar probably reached Europe from the Middle East in the Middle Ages. Toledo in Spain, whose convents are long famous for mazapán, and the German port of Lübeck, which became a great marzipan centre, both claim it as their own; neither claim can be settled, and the sweet was made widely around the Mediterranean early on.

Peanut butter cups

Dark chocolate shells around a salty, sweetened peanut butter centre, made in a muffin tin.

Dairy-free 3.5 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 cup prep 30 min · cook 5 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Chocolate

  • 60 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao)
  • 1½ tbsp coconut oil (20 g)
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)

Filling

  • ½ cup peanut butter, smooth, unsweetened (128 g)
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • 2 tsp coconut oil, melted
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Peppermint patties

Cool, firm peppermint fondant discs dipped in dark chocolate; dairy-free as written.

Dairy-free 1.2 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 patty prep 40 min · cook 5 min · rest 1 h 30 min

Ingredients

Filling

  • ¼ cup coconut oil (54 g), softened, not melted
  • 2 tbsp coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (30 g)
  • 1 cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (120 g)
  • 1 tsp peppermint extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Coating

  • 120 g sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened)
  • 45 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao)
  • 1 tbsp coconut oil (14 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.2 g
Total carbohydrate13.0 g
Fibre1.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.3 g
Sugars0.2 g
Fat8.8 g
Protein1.0 g
Energy92 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), peppermint extract, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened), unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).
