The chocolate-covered peppermint patty is best known from the York Peppermint Pattie, first made in York, Pennsylvania, in 1940. Peppermint creams covered in chocolate were made by confectioners before then, but the firm, flat patty became the American standard.

Sesame brittle

Toasted sesame seeds bound in a thin, glassy allulose caramel and snapped into shards.

Dairy-free 1.4 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 piece prep 10 min · cook 15 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup sesame seeds (144 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)
  • 2 tsp coconut oil
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp baking soda

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.4 g
Total carbohydrate10.8 g
Fibre1.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.0 g
Sugars0.1 g
Fat6.7 g
Protein2.1 g
Energy79 kcal

Contains: coconut, sesame.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, water.

Method

  1. Line a baking sheet with parchment and lightly oil a second sheet of parchment.
  2. Toast the sesame seeds in a dry pan over medium heat, stirring, until golden and fragrant, 4–5 minutes. Tip onto a plate.
  3. In a heavy pan, heat the allulose, water, coconut oil and salt, stirring until dissolved, then cook without stirring until it reaches 150 °C (300 °F) and is light amber. Allulose browns quickly near the end, so stay with it.
  4. Off the heat, stir in the vanilla and baking soda, then the sesame seeds, quickly and thoroughly.
  5. Turn onto the lined sheet, cover with the oiled parchment and roll as thin as you can. Peel off the top sheet and leave to cool for 30 minutes, then break into pieces.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Allulose brittle is not as hard as sugar brittle and softens in humid air; store it airtight, with parchment between layers, and eat within a few days.

Where it comes from

Sesame brittle · Levant (eastern Mediterranean) · Middle East & North Africa · ancient

Sesame seeds bound with honey are among the oldest confections of the eastern Mediterranean and the Middle East, and the Greek pasteli is mentioned in ancient sources. Related sesame sweets are still made from Greece and Turkey through the Levant to Iran and India.

Soft almond turrón (Jijona style)

The soft, crumbly kind of Spanish turrón: roasted almonds ground to a paste with sweetener, lemon and cinnamon, pressed into a bar.

Dairy-free 1.5 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 slice prep 25 min · cook 15 min · rest 12 h

Ingredients

  • 250 g almonds, blanched
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)
  • 1 large egg white
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.5 g
Total carbohydrate9.5 g
Fibre2.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g
Sugars0.7 g
Fat7.8 g
Protein3.5 g
Energy94 kcal

Contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 165 °C (325 °F). Roast the almonds on a tray for 10–12 minutes, until golden through. Cool.
  2. Grind the almonds in a food processor until they pass the crumb stage and become a thick, oily paste, 3–5 minutes, scraping down often.
  3. Warm the allulose and water in a small pan until it bubbles and reaches 115 °C (240 °F). Do not let it darken.
  4. With the processor running, add the egg white, then pour in the hot syrup, then the zest, cinnamon and salt. Process until evenly combined.
  5. Line a 20 x 10 cm (8 x 4 in) loaf tin with parchment, press the paste in firmly and level it. Cover and leave at room temperature for 12 hours to firm, then slice.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Toast the almonds well: their roasted flavour is the whole character of turrón de Jijona.

Where it comes from

Turrón · Jijona (Xixona) · Southern Europe · 1500s

Turrón, an almond and honey confection, has been made around Jijona and Alicante in eastern Spain for centuries and is documented there from the sixteenth century; its roots are often traced to the sweets of Moorish Spain. Jijona gives its name to the soft, ground-almond kind, while the hard kind with whole almonds is associated with Alicante. This version replaces the honey with allulose.

See it on the map

Tahini halva

A dense sesame confection of tahini beaten with hot syrup, studded with pistachios and set in a loaf tin.

Dairy-free 3.0 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 slice prep 15 min · cook 10 min · rest 12 h

Ingredients

  • 1 cup tahini (240 g), well stirred
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g)
  • ¼ cup water (59 g)
  • ⅓ cup pistachios, shelled (41 g), roughly chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.0 g
Total carbohydrate11.6 g
Fibre1.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose7.0 g
Sugars0.2 g
Fat8.3 g
Protein3.2 g
Energy102 kcal

Contains: sesame, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol, water.

Method

  1. Line a 20 x 10 cm (8 x 4 in) loaf tin with parchment. Put the tahini, vanilla and salt in a bowl.
  2. Heat the erythritol, allulose and water in a small pan, stirring until dissolved, then boil without stirring until it reaches 120 °C (250 °F).
  3. Pour the hot syrup into the tahini and stir hard with a wooden spoon. Within a minute it will seize and thicken; keep stirring until it pulls away from the bowl, then fold in the pistachios.
  4. Press firmly into the tin and smooth the top. Cool, then cover and leave 12 hours at room temperature or in the fridge before slicing.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Real halva's flaky strands come from sugar crystallising as it is beaten; erythritol crystallises too and gives some of that texture, but expect something closer to a firm fudge.
  • Stop stirring as soon as it pulls together; overworking makes it oily.

Where it comes from

Halva · Istanbul · Middle East & North Africa · Ottoman era

Halva is an old Arabic word for a sweet, applied across the Middle East, South Asia and the Balkans to many different confections. The tahini-based kind, made by beating sesame paste with a hot sugar syrup, became widespread across the Ottoman lands and remains the classic halva of Turkey, the Levant, Greece and the Balkans.

See it on the map

Fat bombs

Chocolate-almond fat bombs

Small frozen bites of almond butter, coconut oil and cocoa with a little chopped almond for crunch.

Dairy-free 1.1 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 fat bomb prep 15 min · cook 5 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • ½ cup coconut oil (109 g)
  • ½ cup almond butter, unsalted (128 g), well stirred
  • 3 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (16 g)
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 3 tbsp almonds (27 g), toasted and finely chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.1 g
Total carbohydrate4.0 g
Fibre1.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose1.5 g
Sugars0.5 g
Fat12.2 g
Protein2.2 g
Energy123 kcal

Contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Set 16 silicone mini-muffin cups or candy moulds on a small tray that fits in your freezer.
  2. Melt the coconut oil and almond butter together in a small pan over low heat, stirring, just until fluid. Do not let it get hot.
  3. Take the pan off the heat and whisk in the cocoa, powdered erythritol, vanilla and salt until no lumps of cocoa remain.
  4. Divide among the moulds, about 1 tablespoon each, and scatter the chopped almonds over the tops.
  5. Freeze for at least 1 hour, until hard, then pop them out and store in a sealed container.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Coconut oil melts at about 24 °C (76 °F), so keep these in the freezer or fridge and eat them cold.
  • Stir the mixture once more just before pouring; the cocoa settles as it stands.

Where it comes from

Fat bomb · United States · North America · 2010s

Fat bombs are a modern invention, not a traditional sweet. They grew out of the ketogenic and low-carb diet communities in the United States in the 2010s, where home cooks shared recipes for small, sugar-free, high-fat snacks built on coconut oil, nut butters and cocoa. This flavour borrows from the much older pairing of chocolate and almond.

Coconut-lime fat bombs

Bright, chewy little rounds of shredded coconut, coconut cream and fresh lime, set firm in the fridge.

Dairy-free 0.9 g net carbs Serves 14 · 1 fat bomb prep 15 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 1 cup unsweetened shredded coconut (80 g)
  • ⅓ cup coconut oil (72 g), softened, not melted
  • 3 tbsp coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (45 g), the thick part from the top of the can
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • 2 tbsp fresh lime juice (30 g)
  • 2 tsp lime zest, finely grated, from about 1 lime
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp unsweetened shredded coconut (10 g), for rolling

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs0.9 g
Total carbohydrate3.7 g
Fibre1.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose1.7 g
Sugars0.6 g
Fat10.4 g
Protein0.6 g
Energy100 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Put the cup of shredded coconut in a food processor and run it for 1–2 minutes, scraping down once, until it turns fine and slightly oily.
  2. Add the coconut oil, coconut cream, powdered erythritol, lime juice and zest, vanilla and salt and pulse until the mixture holds together when pinched.
  3. Taste and add a little more lime if you like it sharper.
  4. Chill the bowl for 15 minutes if the mixture is too soft to shape, then roll into 14 balls, about 1 tablespoon each.
  5. Roll the balls in the remaining shredded coconut, set them on a lined plate and refrigerate for at least 45 minutes until firm.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • These keep a week in the fridge and soften quickly at room temperature, so serve them straight from the cold.

Where it comes from

Fat bomb · United States · North America · 2010s

This is a modern low-carb recipe rather than a traditional dish. Fat bombs took shape in the 2010s among American ketogenic-diet home cooks, who leaned heavily on coconut because it is high in fat and low in sugar. The coconut-and-lime pairing echoes tropical sweets and drinks, but this form of it is new.

Mocha fat bombs

Dark, coffee-strong chocolate squares made with cocoa butter so they hold their shape a little longer out of the cold.

Dairy-free 1.2 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 fat bomb prep 15 min · cook 5 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 40 g cocoa butter, chopped
  • ⅓ cup coconut oil (72 g)
  • ¼ cup almond butter, unsalted (64 g)
  • 3 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder (16 g)
  • 2 tsp instant espresso powder
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) (30 g)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.2 g
Total carbohydrate4.2 g
Fibre1.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose2.0 g
Sugars0.4 g
Fat13.3 g
Protein1.5 g
Energy129 kcal

Contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Melt the cocoa butter in a heatproof bowl over a pan of barely simmering water, then add the coconut oil and almond butter and stir until smooth.
  2. Take the bowl off the heat. Whisk in the cocoa, espresso powder and powdered erythritol until the espresso has dissolved.
  3. Whisk in the coconut cream, vanilla and salt. The mixture will thicken slightly.
  4. Pour into 12 silicone moulds or a small loaf tin lined with parchment, about 10 × 15 cm (4 × 6 in).
  5. Freeze for 1 hour until hard. If you used a tin, lift out and cut into 12 squares with a warm knife.

Where it comes from

Fat bomb · United States · North America · 2010s

Like other fat bombs, this is a modern low-carb creation from American ketogenic-diet home cooks of the 2010s. The mocha flavour, coffee with chocolate, takes its name from the Yemeni port of Mocha, once a major coffee-trading centre, but the sweet itself has no traditional ancestor.

Peanut butter fat bombs

Salty-sweet peanut butter cups set in the freezer, with a thin cap of sugar-free dark chocolate.

Dairy-free 2.2 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 fat bomb prep 15 min · cook 5 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Peanut layer

  • ½ cup peanut butter, smooth, unsweetened (128 g)
  • ¼ cup coconut oil (54 g)
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt

Chocolate top

  • 40 g sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened)
  • 1 tsp coconut oil

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.2 g
Total carbohydrate5.7 g
Fibre1.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose2.6 g
Sugars1.2 g
Fat11.5 g
Protein2.6 g
Energy122 kcal

Contains: coconut, peanuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), sugar-free dark chocolate chips (stevia or erythritol sweetened).

Method

  1. Line a 12-cup mini-muffin tin with paper cases, or use silicone moulds.

Fat bombs are a modern invention of the low-carb and ketogenic diet community in the United States, spread largely through blogs and forums in the 2010s. This version imitates the American peanut butter cup, a candy that dates to the early twentieth century, but replaces the sugar with erythritol and much of the bulk with coconut oil.

Raspberry cheesecake fat bombs

Creamy cream-cheese bites rippled with a quick raspberry mash, frozen until just firm.

Dairy-free option 1.0 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 fat bomb prep 20 min · rest 1 h 30 min

Ingredients

  • 60 g raspberries, fresh or thawed frozen
  • 4 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (32 g), divided
  • 225 g full-fat cream cheese, softened
  • ¼ cup coconut oil (54 g), softened
  • 1 tsp fresh lemon juice
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.0 g1.5 g
Total carbohydrate3.3 g3.9 g
Fibre0.2 g0.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose2.0 g2.0 g
Sugars0.7 g0.5 g
Fat8.2 g6.9 g
Protein0.9 g0.7 g
Energy83 kcal74 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Mash the raspberries with 1 tablespoon of the powdered erythritol in a small bowl until saucy. Push through a sieve if you dislike the seeds.
  2. Beat the cream cheese and coconut oil with an electric mixer until smooth and fluffy, then beat in the remaining powdered erythritol, the lemon juice, vanilla and salt.
  3. Spoon the cream-cheese mixture into 16 silicone mini-muffin cups, filling each about three-quarters full.
  4. Drop half a teaspoon of the raspberry mash on each and swirl it in with a toothpick; do not overmix or the colour turns muddy.
  5. Freeze for 1½ hours, until firm. Unmould and keep in a sealed container in the freezer; let them stand 5 minutes before eating.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese — use a firm, block-style product.

A cashew- or almond-based cream cheese works but freezes harder and tastes nuttier; give the bites 10 minutes out of the freezer before eating.

Tips

  • Room-temperature cream cheese and coconut oil are the key to a smooth mixture; cold coconut oil leaves hard flecks.

Where it comes from

Fat bomb · United States · North America · 2010s

Fat bombs are modern, not traditional: they were popularised by American ketogenic and low-carb home cooks in the 2010s. This flavour borrows from cheesecake, whose own lineage runs back through European curd tarts to ancient Greek and Roman cheese cakes, but the frozen, sugar-free bite is a recent idea.

Fruit desserts

Baked cinnamon apple slices

Thin apple slices baked soft with butter, cinnamon and a few toasted pecans; a small, warm serving.

Dairy-free option 5.6 g net carbs Serves 8 · a quarter of an apple (about 5 slices) prep 15 min · cook 25 min · rest 5 min

Ingredients

  • 2 apples, a firm, tart variety, cored and cut into 20 slices each
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 1½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp ground nutmeg
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • ⅓ cup pecans (36 g), chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.6 g5.6 g
Total carbohydrate10.4 g10.4 g
Fibre1.8 g1.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose3.0 g3.0 g
Sugars4.9 g4.9 g
Fat6.2 g6.9 g
Protein0.6 g0.6 g

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 190 °C (375 °F). Line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Toss the apple slices with the lemon juice, then with the melted butter, erythritol, cinnamon, nutmeg and salt.
  3. Spread the slices in a single layer, overlapping slightly, and scatter the pecans over them.
  4. Bake 20–25 minutes, turning the tray once, until the apples are tender and lightly caramelised at the edges and the pecans are toasted.
  5. Rest 5 minutes, then divide among 8 small plates.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter — melted.

Coconut oil works well here and adds a faint sweetness; the slices brown about the same.

Tips

  • Apples are high in sugar, so the portion is deliberately small; serve with a spoonful of unsweetened coconut yogurt to make it go further.

Where it comes from

Baked apples · England · Northern Europe · medieval

Apples baked or roasted with spices are among the oldest desserts of northern Europe and appear in English cookery from the Middle Ages onward, when they were often cooked in the embers or in a cooling bread oven. Cinnamon, imported at great cost, was a mark of a wealthy table. Baked apples later became an everyday family pudding in Britain and North America.

Berry cobbler

Juicy baked berries topped with spoonfuls of soft almond-flour biscuit dough that bake into a cobbled crust.

Dairy-free option 5.9 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 bowl prep 20 min · cook 35 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

Fruit

  • 300 g blackberries
  • 200 g raspberries
  • 50 g blueberries
  • 3 tbsp granulated erythritol (36 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • ¼ tsp xanthan gum

Biscuit topping

  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 3 tbsp granulated erythritol (36 g)
  • 1½ tsp baking powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • ¼ cup heavy whipping cream (60 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp ground cinnamon, mixed with 1 tsp erythritol for the top (optional)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.9 g6.2 g
Total carbohydrate20.6 g20.9 g
Fibre5.7 g5.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose9.0 g9.0 g
Sugars4.7 g4.7 g
Fat15.9 g17.1 g
Protein4.9 g4.8 g
Energy200 kcal212 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 180 °C (350 °F). Use a 20 cm (8 in) square or 23 cm (9 in) round baking dish.
  2. Toss the berries with the erythritol and lemon juice in the dish, then sprinkle over the xanthan gum and toss again.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, erythritol, baking powder and salt in a bowl. In another bowl whisk the egg, melted butter, cream and vanilla.
  4. Stir the wet ingredients into the dry until just combined. Let the dough stand 3 minutes; the coconut flour will thicken it to a soft, scoopable batter.
  5. Drop the dough over the fruit in 8 rough spoonfuls, leaving gaps for the juice to bubble through, and sprinkle with the cinnamon mixture.
  6. Bake 30–35 minutes, until the topping is golden and firm in the centre and the fruit is bubbling. Rest 10 minutes before serving.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter — melted.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With coconut oil and coconut cream the topping is a touch more tender and tastes faintly of coconut; it browns about the same.

Tips

  • Blueberries carry the most sugar of the three berries, so they are kept to a handful for colour.

Where it comes from

Cobbler · Eastern United States · North America · 1800s

Cobblers are generally traced to British settlers in the American colonies, who adapted steamed puddings and pies by covering stewed fruit with a rough biscuit or scone dough. The name is usually explained by the topping's cobblestone look. By the nineteenth century cobblers, and cousins such as grunts, slumps and buckles, were common across the United States.

Berry crumble

Mixed raspberries, blackberries and strawberries baked under a buttery almond-and-pecan crumb.

Dairy-free option 5.4 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 bowl prep 20 min · cook 35 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

Fruit

  • 250 g raspberries
  • 200 g blackberries
  • 150 g strawberries, hulled and halved
  • 3 tbsp granulated erythritol (36 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • ¼ tsp xanthan gum

Crumble

  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • ½ cup pecans (54 g), finely chopped
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • 1 tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cold, cut into small cubes

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.4 g5.4 g
Total carbohydrate21.7 g21.7 g
Fibre5.8 g5.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.5 g10.5 g
Sugars4.4 g4.4 g
Fat17.4 g17.2 g
Protein4.1 g4.1 g
Energy205 kcal205 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Have ready a 20 cm (8 in) square baking dish or a 1.5-litre (1½-quart) gratin dish.
  2. Toss the berries with the erythritol and lemon juice in the dish. Sprinkle the xanthan gum evenly over the fruit and toss again so it does not clump.
  3. For the crumble, mix the almond flour, pecans, erythritol, cinnamon and salt in a bowl. Rub in the cold butter with your fingertips until the mixture forms damp clumps.
  4. Scatter the crumble loosely over the fruit; do not press it down.
  5. Bake 30–35 minutes, until the fruit bubbles at the edges and the top is golden. Almond flour browns quickly, so cover loosely with foil if it darkens before the fruit is bubbling.
  6. Rest 10 minutes before serving so the juices thicken.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use cold stick-style; coconut oil also works but makes a sandier crumb.

Dairy-free stick butter gives almost the same crumb; it browns a little less, so bake until the fruit bubbles rather than by colour.

Tips

  • Frozen berries work; do not thaw them, and add 5–10 minutes to the baking time.

Where it comes from

Fruit crumble · Britain · Northern Europe · 1900s

The crumble is a twentieth-century British pudding: stewed or raw fruit baked under a rubbed-in mixture of flour, fat and sugar. It is often linked to the Second World War, when rationing made a crumble topping a thriftier choice than pastry, though exactly when it first appeared is not well documented. It has remained a staple of British home cooking.

Cherry clafoutis

Sweet cherries baked in a puffed, custardy almond batter, served warm with a dusting of powdered sweetener.

Dairy-free option 4.8 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 wedge prep 15 min · cook 40 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

  • 1 tbsp unsalted butter (14 g), for the dish
  • 200 g sweet cherries, pitted, pitted
  • 3 large eggs
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • ½ cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (48 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g)
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp almond extract
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), for dusting

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.8 g5.4 g
Total carbohydrate14.9 g15.4 g
Fibre1.1 g1.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.9 g8.9 g
Sugars4.2 g4.2 g
Fat11.9 g12.1 g
Protein4.4 g4.5 g
Energy147 kcal148 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: almond extract, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 180 °C (350 °F). Butter a 23 cm (9 in) pie dish or shallow baking dish and spread the cherries over the base.
  2. Whisk the eggs and erythritol until frothy, then whisk in the almond flour and salt until smooth.
  3. Whisk in the cream, almond milk, vanilla and almond extract. The batter will be thin, like a crêpe batter.
  4. Pour the batter gently over the cherries.
  5. Bake 35–40 minutes, until puffed, golden at the edges and just set in the centre; a knife inserted midway should come out clean.
  6. Cool 15 minutes; it sinks as it cools, which is normal. Dust with powdered erythritol and serve warm.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With coconut cream the custard is slightly denser and has a mild coconut note; the almond extract helps cover it.

Tips

  • In Limousin the cherries are traditionally left unpitted, which adds a faint almond flavour from the stones; warn your guests if you do this.
  • Cherries are high in sugar for a fruit, so keep to the weight given.

Where it comes from

Clafoutis · Limousin, France · Western Europe · 1800s

Clafoutis is a country dessert from the Limousin region of central France, made by pouring a thick pancake-like batter over black cherries and baking it. It spread to the rest of France in the nineteenth century. Strictly, only the cherry version is called clafoutis; made with other fruit, the French usually call it a flaugnarde.

Gajar halwa (carrot pudding)

Grated carrot slowly simmered in milk and cream until thick and jammy, then fried in ghee-rich butter with cardamom and finished with pistachios — served in small, rich spoonfuls.

Dairy-free option 6.1 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1/3 cup prep 15 min · cook 50 min

Ingredients

  • 300 g carrot, finely grated
  • ½ cup whole milk (122 g)
  • ¾ cup heavy whipping cream (178 g)
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), or ghee
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cardamom
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 3 tbsp pistachios, shelled (23 g), chopped
  • 1 tbsp almonds, slivered or chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs6.1 g5.6 g
Total carbohydrate16.1 g15.7 g
Fibre2.0 g2.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.0 g8.0 g
Sugars4.6 g2.7 g
Fat17.9 g18.0 g
Protein3.1 g2.6 g
Energy202 kcal195 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Put the grated carrot, milk and cream in a heavy wide pan and bring to a simmer over medium heat.
  2. Cook, stirring often and scraping the bottom, for 30–35 minutes, until the liquid has almost completely evaporated and the carrot looks thick and glossy.
  3. Add the butter (or ghee) and fry, stirring constantly, for 8–10 minutes until the carrot deepens in colour and the fat starts to separate at the edges.
  4. Stir in the allulose, cardamom and salt and cook 3–4 minutes more; allulose catches easily, so keep the heat at medium-low and keep stirring.
  5. Stir in most of the nuts and serve warm, scattered with the rest.

Dairy-free version

  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of whole milk.
  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of heavy whipping cream.
  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter.

Coconut milk reduces faster and leaves a clear coconut flavour instead of the caramelised-milk taste of the original; stop reducing a little earlier so it does not turn oily.

Tips

  • Portions are small on purpose: carrot is the main ingredient, and this is where most of the carbohydrate comes from.
  • Traditional halwa uses far more carrot and reduced milk solids (khoa); the cream here gives the richness with less milk sugar.

Where it comes from

Halwa · Punjab · South Asia · 1900s

Halwa is a broad family of sweet, dense puddings found from the Middle East to South Asia. Gajar ka halwa, made by slowly cooking grated carrots in milk and finishing them in ghee, is associated with Punjab and northern India, where it is a winter dessert when red carrots are in season and a fixture of festivals such as Diwali.

See it on the map

Pavlova with berries

A crisp-shelled, soft-centred sugar-free meringue piled with whipped cream and fresh berries.

Dairy-free option 3.7 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 wedge prep 30 min · cook 1 h 30 min · rest 2 h

Ingredients

Meringue

  • 4 large egg whites, at room temperature
  • ½ tsp cream of tartar
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • ¾ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (90 g)
  • 1 tsp fresh lemon juice
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Topping

  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g), cold
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g)
  • 150 g strawberries, hulled and sliced
  • 100 g raspberries
  • 50 g blueberries
  • 1 tbsp fresh mint leaves, small leaves, to garnish

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.7 g4.8 g
Total carbohydrate18.3 g19.4 g
Fibre1.3 g1.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose13.2 g13.2 g
Sugars3.2 g3.3 g
Fat10.9 g10.6 g
Protein3.0 g3.1 g
Energy131 kcal128 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 120 °C (250 °F). Draw a 20 cm (8 in) circle on a sheet of parchment, turn it over and lay it on a baking sheet.
  2. In a very clean, grease-free bowl, whisk the egg whites with the cream of tartar and salt on medium speed until soft peaks form.
  3. Raise the speed to high and add the powdered erythritol a tablespoon at a time, beating about 15 seconds after each, until the meringue is thick, glossy and holds stiff peaks. Beat in the lemon juice and vanilla.
  4. Spread the meringue within the circle, building the sides higher than the middle to make a shallow nest.
  5. Bake 75–90 minutes, until the outside is dry and crisp and lifts cleanly off the paper. Turn the oven off and leave the meringue inside with the door ajar for at least 2 hours to cool slowly.
  6. Just before serving, whip the cream with the powdered erythritol to soft peaks, spoon it into the nest and pile the berries on top. Scatter with mint and serve at once.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — use the solid part of a can chilled overnight.

Chilled coconut cream whips softer than dairy cream and melts faster, so top the pavlova at the very last minute and serve it straight away.

Tips

  • Erythritol meringue is more fragile than sugar meringue and weeps in humid weather; make the shell on a dry day and fill it only when serving.
  • The meringue shell keeps 2 days in an airtight tin at room temperature.

Where it comes from

Pavlova · New Zealand or Australia (disputed) · Oceania · 1920s–1930s

The pavlova is named after the Russian ballerina Anna Pavlova, who toured Australia and New Zealand in the 1920s. Both countries claim to have invented it, and the question remains disputed; recipe research has generally found earlier printed versions in New Zealand, while Australia has its own well-known origin story. Large baked meringues with cream were also made in Europe before then. The map marker sits in Wellington only as a placeholder for this shared, contested origin.

See it on the map

Rhubarb fool

Sharp, softly stewed rhubarb folded through lightly whipped cream, in pink ripples.

Dairy-free option 3.1 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 glass prep 15 min · cook 15 min · rest 1 h 30 min

Ingredients

Rhubarb

  • 400 g rhubarb, trimmed and cut into 2 cm (¾ in) pieces
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 1 tsp orange zest
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)

Cream

  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g), cold
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.1 g4.6 g
Total carbohydrate16.2 g17.7 g
Fibre1.2 g1.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose11.9 g11.9 g
Sugars2.0 g2.1 g
Fat14.4 g14.0 g
Protein1.7 g1.9 g
Energy154 kcal150 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), water.

Method

  1. Put the rhubarb, erythritol, orange zest and water in a pan, cover and cook over low heat for 8–10 minutes, stirring once or twice, until the rhubarb collapses.
  2. Uncover and simmer 3–5 minutes more to drive off some liquid; you want a thick purée, not a sauce. Taste and add more sweetener if it is too sharp.
  3. Cool, then chill until cold, at least 1 hour.
  4. Whip the cream with the powdered erythritol and vanilla to soft peaks.
  5. Fold in three-quarters of the rhubarb with just a few strokes so it stays rippled. Spoon into glasses, top with the remaining rhubarb and chill 30 minutes before serving.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — use the solid part of a can chilled overnight.

Coconut cream gives a softer, slightly looser fool with a coconut note that sits well with rhubarb; serve it soon after making.

Tips

  • Rhubarb leaves are poisonous; use only the stalks.

Where it comes from

Fool · England · Northern Europe · 1600s

Fools, puréed fruit folded into cream or custard, have been made in England since at least the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, when gooseberry fool was the classic. The origin of the name is uncertain. Rhubarb came into wide use as a dessert ingredient in Britain in the nineteenth century, and rhubarb fool became one of the best-loved versions.

See it on the map

Rote Grütze (red berry pudding)

Tart redcurrants and raspberries cooked just until they burst, softly set and served cold with a pour of cream.

Dairy-free option 6.0 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1/2 cup prep 15 min · cook 10 min · rest 4 h

Ingredients

Berries

  • ½ cup water (118 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • 180 g redcurrants, stripped from their stems
  • 150 g raspberries
  • 50 g sweet cherries, pitted, pitted and halved
  • 1 tsp fresh lemon juice
  • 1½ tsp unflavored powdered gelatin
  • 3 tbsp water (45 g), cold, for the gelatin

To serve

  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g), lightly whipped or poured

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs6.0 g6.8 g
Total carbohydrate19.6 g20.4 g
Fibre3.1 g3.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.6 g10.6 g
Sugars5.0 g5.1 g
Fat7.4 g7.2 g
Protein2.0 g2.1 g
Energy109 kcal107 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, water.

Method

  1. Sprinkle the gelatin over the 3 tablespoons of cold water and leave it to swell for 5 minutes.
  2. Bring the half cup of water and the allulose to a simmer in a saucepan. Add the redcurrants and cherries and cook 3–4 minutes until the currants start to burst.
  3. Add the raspberries and lemon juice and simmer 1 minute more, just until they soften; keep some of the fruit whole.
  4. Take the pan off the heat and stir in the swollen gelatin until it has completely dissolved.
  5. Divide among six small glasses or bowls and chill at least 4 hours, until softly set.
  6. Serve cold with a spoonful of cream on top.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

The pudding itself is dairy-free; chilled coconut cream, stirred smooth, replaces the cream and adds a light coconut note.

Tips

  • The traditional thickener is potato or corn starch; gelatin gives a softer, wobbly set instead of a starchy one. For a looser, sauce-like texture use 1/4 tsp xanthan gum whisked in off the heat.
  • Frozen redcurrants and raspberries work well; cook them a minute longer.

Where it comes from

Fruit pudding · Hamburg, northern Germany · Northern Europe · by the 1800s

Grütze means groats, and the dish began as a grain porridge coloured and flavoured with berry juice before starch-thickened fruit versions took over. Rote Grütze is a regional classic of northern Germany, especially Hamburg and Schleswig-Holstein, and Denmark has a close twin, rødgrød med fløde, served the same way with cream.

See it on the map

Strawberries Romanoff

Strawberries steeped in orange juice and zest, folded through a tangy sweetened cream.

Dairy-free option 5.9 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 glass prep 15 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

Strawberries

  • 450 g strawberries, hulled and halved or quartered
  • 2 tbsp fresh orange juice (30 g)
  • 1 tsp orange zest
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)

Cream

  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g), cold
  • ¼ cup sour cream (58 g)
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.9 g6.6 g
Total carbohydrate14.1 g15.0 g
Fibre1.5 g1.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.7 g6.7 g
Sugars5.1 g4.9 g
Fat9.2 g8.4 g
Protein1.3 g1.3 g
Energy116 kcal110 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Toss the strawberries with the orange juice, zest and erythritol. Cover and refrigerate for 1 hour, stirring once, so they release their juice.
  2. Whip the cream with the powdered erythritol and vanilla to soft peaks, then fold in the sour cream.
  3. Set aside a few berries for the top. Fold the rest, with a spoonful of their juice, gently through the cream so it streaks pink.
  4. Spoon into 6 glasses, top with the reserved berries and serve within an hour.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — the solid part of a well-chilled can.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of sour cream.

Coconut cream and coconut yogurt make a looser, softer cream with a clear coconut flavour; fold it together just before serving.

Tips

  • The classic recipe adds an orange liqueur to the berries; a teaspoon per serving is traditional but adds sugar.

Where it comes from

Strawberries Romanoff · Paris, France · Western Europe · 1800s

The dish is named for the Romanov dynasty of Russia and is often credited to French chefs of the nineteenth century who cooked for Russian aristocrats, though no single inventor is firmly documented. It became widely known in the United States in the mid-twentieth century, when it was associated with fashionable restaurants. The map marker reflects its usual attribution to French cookery.

Summer pudding

A dome of juice-soaked low-carb sponge packed with lightly cooked summer berries, pressed overnight.

Dairy-free 5.3 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 wedge prep 40 min · cook 25 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

Low-carb sponge (in place of bread)

  • 4 large eggs
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp psyllium husk powder
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp coconut oil (27 g), melted, plus a little for the tin

Fruit

  • 250 g raspberries
  • 125 g redcurrants, stripped from their stalks
  • 125 g blackberries
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 3 tbsp water (45 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs5.3 g
Total carbohydrate24.9 g
Fibre5.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose13.9 g
Sugars3.9 g
Fat12.4 g
Protein6.5 g
Energy174 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Oil a 23 × 33 cm (9 × 13 in) baking tin and line it with parchment.
  2. Whisk the eggs and erythritol until pale and thick, about 3 minutes. Whisk the almond flour, psyllium, baking powder and salt together, then fold them in with the melted coconut oil.
  3. Spread thinly and evenly in the tin and bake 15–18 minutes, until springy and lightly golden. Cool completely; this thin sheet stands in for the white bread of the classic pudding.
  4. Put the berries, erythritol and water in a pan and warm over medium-low heat for 3–4 minutes, just until the juices run and the sweetener dissolves. The fruit should keep its shape. Cool slightly.
  5. Line a 1-litre (1-quart) pudding basin with cling film. Cut a round of sponge for the base and strips for the sides, overlapping them slightly. Brush the sponge generously with the berry juice.
  6. Spoon in the fruit with most of the remaining juice, keeping 3–4 tablespoons back. Cover with a lid of sponge.
  7. Fold the film over, set a saucer on top and weigh it down with a couple of cans. Refrigerate at least 8 hours.
  8. Turn out onto a plate and spoon the reserved juice over any pale patches. Cut into wedges.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Almond sponge is softer than stale white bread; pressing it overnight with a weight is what makes it hold its shape, so do not skip it.

Where it comes from

Summer pudding · England · Northern Europe · late 1800s

Summer pudding is an English dessert of stale white bread soaked in the juice of lightly stewed soft fruit and pressed in a basin. It is often said to have begun as a lighter alternative to pastry puddings for health-conscious diners in the late nineteenth century, and it became a classic of the English summer by the early twentieth. The low-carb sponge here replaces the bread.

See it on the map

Drinks

Almond horchata

A cold, creamy, cinnamon-scented drink made by blending soaked almonds with water and straining, in the spirit of horchata.

Dairy-free 2.7 g net carbs Serves 4 · 1 glass prep 15 min · rest 8 h

Ingredients

  • ¾ cup almonds (107 g), blanched
  • 4 cups water (948 g), divided
  • ¾ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 3 tbsp granulated erythritol (36 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.7 g
Total carbohydrate15.3 g
Fibre3.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose9.0 g
Sugars1.3 g
Fat13.4 g
Protein5.7 g
Energy161 kcal

Contains: tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, water.

Method

  1. Put the almonds in a bowl with 2 cups of the water and the cinnamon. Cover and soak in the fridge overnight, or at least 8 hours.
  2. Tip the almonds and soaking water into a blender, add the remaining water, the erythritol, vanilla and salt, and blend on high for 2 minutes, until very smooth.
  3. Strain through a nut-milk bag or a sieve lined with a double layer of muslin, squeezing out as much liquid as you can.
  4. Taste for sweetness, then chill. Stir and serve over ice with a dusting of cinnamon.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Most of the almond solids stay in the bag, so the drink carries far fewer carbs than the whole nuts would; the figure shown counts the whole almonds and so overstates it.
  • The leftover almond pulp can be dried in a low oven and used like almond meal.

Where it comes from

Horchata · Valencia, Spain · Southern Europe · medieval

This is an almond version, not a traditional horchata. The classic Spanish horchata de chufa comes from Valencia and is made from tiger nuts, the small tubers of a sedge grown there since Moorish times; Mexican horchata is made from rice, usually with cinnamon. The name is generally traced to the Latin for barley, an older base for such drinks. Almonds give a similar milky, nutty drink without the starch.

See it on the map

Eggnog

Thick, nutmeg-scented cooked egg custard drink, gently heated for safety and served cold.

Dairy-free option 5.6 g net carbs Serves 6 · ⅔ cup prep 10 min · cook 10 min · rest 3 h

Ingredients

  • 4 large egg yolks
  • ⅓ cup granulated erythritol (63 g)
  • 2 cups whole milk (488 g)
  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g)
  • ½ tsp ground nutmeg, freshly grated if possible, plus extra to serve
  • ⅛ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 0.0625 tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp rum extract, or 60–90 ml (¼–⅓ cup) dark rum or brandy

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs5.6 g4.1 g
Total carbohydrate16.2 g14.9 g
Fibre0.1 g0.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.6 g10.6 g
Sugars5.4 g2.1 g
Fat20.0 g17.7 g
Protein5.5 g3.4 g
Energy226 kcal185 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, rum extract.

Method

  1. Whisk the egg yolks and erythritol in a bowl until pale and thick.
  2. Heat the milk, cream, nutmeg, cinnamon and salt in a pan until steaming.
  3. Pour the hot milk into the yolks in a thin stream, whisking all the time, then return everything to the pan.
  4. Cook over low heat, stirring constantly with a spatula, until the mixture thickens slightly and reaches 71 °C (160 °F) on a thermometer. Do not let it boil or it will curdle.
  5. Strain into a jug, stir in the vanilla and rum extract (or spirit), cool, then refrigerate at least 3 hours.
  6. Whisk before serving and grate a little nutmeg over each glass.

Dairy-free version

  • Use unsweetened almond milk instead of whole milk.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With almond milk and coconut cream the eggnog is a little thinner and tastes of coconut; nutmeg and rum carry it well.

Tips

  • Erythritol can crystallise in the cold drink; allulose or a powdered sweetener stays dissolved.

Where it comes from

Eggnog · Britain and colonial America · Northern Europe · 1700s

Eggnog descends from British posset, a medieval drink of hot milk curdled with wine or ale and sometimes thickened with eggs. In the American colonies, where milk, eggs and rum were plentiful, a cold egg-and-milk punch made with rum became a holiday favourite, and the name eggnog was in use by the eighteenth century. The origin of the word nog itself is uncertain.

Golden milk (haldi doodh)

Warm almond and coconut milk whisked with turmeric, ginger, cinnamon and black pepper; soothing and mildly sweet.

Dairy-free 4.5 g net carbs Serves 2 · 1 mug prep 3 min · cook 7 min

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups unsweetened almond milk (360 g)
  • 1 tsp ground turmeric
  • ½ cup full-fat coconut milk (canned) (113 g)
  • ¼ tsp ground ginger, or a few slices of fresh ginger
  • ¼ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp ground cardamom
  • 0.0625 tsp ground black pepper
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs4.5 g
Total carbohydrate11.5 g
Fibre1.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g
Sugars1.5 g
Fat13.9 g
Protein2.1 g
Energy146 kcal

Contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Whisk the almond milk, turmeric and coconut milk in a small pan with the ginger, cinnamon, cardamom and black pepper.
  2. Warm over medium-low heat, whisking often, until it steams and small bubbles appear at the edge. Simmer gently 3–4 minutes; do not let it boil over.
  3. Whisk in the erythritol until dissolved.
  4. Strain if you used fresh ginger, then pour into mugs and serve hot.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Traditionally it is made with cow's milk; this version is dairy-free as written, and the coconut milk gives it body.
  • Turmeric stains; rinse the pan and whisk straight away.

Where it comes from

Haldi doodh · India · South Asia · traditional

Haldi doodh, milk boiled with turmeric, is a long-standing home remedy across the Indian subcontinent, given especially at bedtime or for colds. Turmeric has been used in South Asian cooking and traditional medicine for a very long time. The English name golden milk and the drink's popularity in Western cafés date only from the 2010s.

Hong Kong milk tea

Black tea brewed hard and strong, then softened with rich cream for the silky, slightly bitter cup of Hong Kong's cafés.

Dairy-free option 1.6 g net carbs Serves 2 · 1 cup prep 2 min · cook 10 min

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups strong brewed black tea (356 g), 4 tea bags or 4 tsp loose black tea (a Ceylon blend is traditional) simmered in 1¾ cups water
  • ⅓ cup heavy whipping cream (79 g)
  • 2 tbsp allulose (24 g), or to taste

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.6 g1.6 g
Total carbohydrate13.6 g13.6 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.0 g12.0 g
Sugars1.1 g0.0 g
Fat14.2 g8.4 g
Protein1.1 g0.8 g
Energy140 kcal84 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Bring the water to a boil, add the tea, lower the heat and simmer 4–5 minutes. It should be very dark and slightly bitter, much stronger than tea you would drink black.
  2. Strain the tea through a fine sieve or cloth; for a smoother cup, pour it back and forth between the pan and a jug a few times, as Hong Kong cafés do with a tea 'sock'.
  3. Divide the cream and allulose between two cups, pour in the hot tea and stir.

Dairy-free version

  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of heavy whipping cream — full-fat canned coconut milk.

Coconut milk gives a similar body but a gentler, slightly nutty flavour; it can separate if boiled, so add it off the heat.

Tips

  • Traditionally the tea is mixed with evaporated milk, or sweetened condensed milk, which is mostly sugar; cream plus allulose gives a similar richness without it.
  • Serve over ice for the cold version; make the tea a little stronger so the ice does not water it down.

Where it comes from

Milk tea · Hong Kong · East Asia · 1950s

Milk tea grew out of the British habit of tea with milk during colonial rule, adapted for Hong Kong's working population with strong tea and evaporated milk. It became the signature drink of the cha chaan teng, the inexpensive diner-cafés that spread after the Second World War. Hong Kong-style milk tea is now listed among the city's items of intangible cultural heritage.

London fog

An Earl Grey tea latte: bergamot-scented tea with vanilla and a cap of frothed milk.

Dairy-free option 4.7 g net carbs Serves 1 · 1 mug prep 2 min · cook 5 min

Ingredients

  • ¾ cup strong brewed black tea (178 g), Earl Grey, brewed strong (1 bag or 1 tsp loose tea, steeped 4–5 minutes)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tbsp allulose (12 g), or to taste
  • ⅓ cup whole milk (81 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.7 g1.7 g
Total carbohydrate16.7 g13.9 g
Fibre0.0 g0.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.0 g12.0 g
Sugars4.3 g0.9 g
Fat2.6 g0.8 g
Protein2.5 g0.3 g
Energy62 kcal25 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Brew the Earl Grey strong, then remove the bag or strain. Stir in the vanilla and allulose.
  2. Warm the milk until steaming but not boiling, then froth it with a frother, a whisk, or by shaking it in a lidded jar.
  3. Pour the milk into the tea, holding back the foam with a spoon, then spoon the foam on top.

Dairy-free version

  • Use unsweetened almond milk instead of whole milk — a 'barista' style almond milk froths best.

Plain unsweetened almond milk makes thinner, shorter-lived foam than dairy milk; it still tastes right, but the cap is lighter.

Tips

  • Steep no longer than 5 minutes; over-steeped Earl Grey turns bitter and the bergamot gets harsh.

Where it comes from

Tea latte · Vancouver · North America · 1990s

Despite its name, the London fog is generally said to have been created in Vancouver, Canada, in the 1990s. The usual story credits a café customer who asked for Earl Grey with steamed milk and vanilla, but it is anecdotal and the details vary between tellings. The drink then spread through Canadian and American coffee shops.

Masala chai

Strong black tea simmered with ginger, cardamom, cinnamon, cloves and pepper, then finished with milk.

Dairy-free option 6.8 g net carbs Serves 2 · 1 cup prep 5 min · cook 10 min

Ingredients

  • 1¼ cups strong brewed black tea (296 g), freshly brewed and strong; Assam is the usual choice
  • ½ tsp ground ginger, or a 2 cm (¾ in) piece of fresh ginger, sliced
  • ¼ tsp ground cardamom
  • ¼ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 0.0625 tsp ground cloves
  • 0.0625 tsp ground black pepper
  • 1 cup whole milk (244 g)
  • 1½ tbsp granulated erythritol (18 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs6.8 g2.3 g
Total carbohydrate16.1 g11.9 g
Fibre0.3 g0.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose9.0 g9.0 g
Sugars6.2 g1.0 g
Fat4.0 g1.2 g
Protein3.9 g0.6 g
Energy81 kcal25 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Pour the hot tea into a small pan with the ginger, cardamom, cinnamon, cloves and pepper and simmer 4–5 minutes, until dark and fragrant.
  2. Pour in the milk and erythritol and bring back just to a boil, watching closely because it rises fast. Lower the heat and simmer 1–2 minutes.
  3. Strain into cups and serve hot.

Dairy-free version

  • Use unsweetened almond milk instead of whole milk — or coconut milk thinned half-and-half with water for a richer cup.

Almond milk makes a thinner, less creamy chai and can separate if boiled hard; add it last and only bring it to a gentle simmer.

Tips

  • Whole spices (cardamom pods, a cinnamon stick, cloves) crushed lightly give a clearer flavour than ground; strain them out the same way.
  • Street-stall chai boils the loose leaves with the spices and milk instead of starting from brewed tea; that works too: use 1¼ cups water and 2 tsp loose Assam, and strain at the end.

Where it comes from

Chai · India · South Asia · 1900s

Spiced drinks and herbal brews have a long history in India, but tea drinking spread widely there only in the twentieth century, after the British established plantations in Assam and the tea industry promoted the drink at home. Indian vendors made it their own by boiling tea with milk, sugar and spices, creating the masala chai now sold at stalls across the country. The marker sits at Kolkata, close to the Assam and Darjeeling tea districts.

Mesoamerican-style spiced hot chocolate

Dark, frothy, barely sweet chocolate made with water and almond milk and warmed with chilli, allspice, vanilla and cinnamon.

Dairy-free 4.2 g net carbs Serves 2 · 1 mug prep 5 min · cook 8 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup water (237 g)
  • 1 cup unsweetened almond milk (240 g)
  • 30 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), finely chopped
  • 1 tbsp unsweetened cocoa powder
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ⅛ tsp ground allspice
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 0.0625 tsp cayenne pepper or chili flakes, a pinch; more if you like it hot
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 0.03125 tsp fine salt

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs4.2 g
Total carbohydrate20.4 g
Fibre4.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.0 g
Sugars1.3 g
Fat9.1 g
Protein3.0 g
Energy117 kcal

Contains: tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), water.

Method

  1. Heat the water and almond milk in a small pan until steaming but not boiling.
  2. Add the chocolate, cocoa, erythritol, allspice, cinnamon and cayenne and whisk over low heat until the chocolate has melted and the mixture is smooth, about 3 minutes.
  3. Take off the heat and stir in the vanilla and salt. Taste, and add a little more cayenne for the heat of the older drinks.
  4. Whisk hard, or blend for 20 seconds, until a thick foam forms on top, then pour into mugs with plenty of foam.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • The Maya and Aztecs raised foam by pouring the drink from a height between two vessels; a stick blender does the same job.

Where it comes from

Cacao drink · Maya lowlands, Mesoamerica · Latin America & Caribbean · ancient

Cacao was drunk in Mesoamerica for well over two thousand years, and chemical traces of it have been found in ancient Maya vessels. The Maya and later the Aztecs prepared it as a frothy, often bitter drink, flavoured with chilli, vanilla, flowers and other spices rather than sugar, and it carried ritual and social weight. Cinnamon and sugar were added only after the Spanish brought the drink to Europe; this version keeps it barely sweet.

Thai iced tea

Very strong spiced black tea, sweetened while hot, poured over ice and crowned with a float of cream.

Dairy-free option 1.8 g net carbs Serves 2 · 1 tall glass prep 5 min · cook 5 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 2 cups strong brewed black tea (474 g), brewed double strength (4 tea bags or 4 tsp loose tea per 2 cups water, steeped 5 minutes)
  • ¼ tsp ground cardamom
  • ⅛ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 3 tbsp allulose (36 g)
  • 4 tbsp heavy whipping cream (60 g), plus ice to fill the glasses

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.8 g3.0 g
Total carbohydrate20.0 g21.2 g
Fibre0.2 g0.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose18.0 g18.0 g
Sugars1.0 g1.1 g
Fat10.8 g10.5 g
Protein0.9 g1.0 g
Energy116 kcal113 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Brew the tea with the cardamom and cinnamon in the hot water, steeping 5 minutes so it is darker than you would drink it plain.
  2. Strain, then stir in the vanilla and allulose until dissolved. Let cool, then chill at least 30 minutes; hot tea melts the ice and dilutes the drink.
  3. Fill two tall glasses with ice and pour in the tea, leaving room at the top.
  4. Spoon 2 tbsp of cream over each glass so it floats and swirls down, and stir just before drinking.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — or full-fat coconut milk for a lighter float.

Coconut cream floats and swirls the same way and adds a faint coconut flavour that suits the spices.

Tips

  • Commercial Thai tea mixes are usually spiced (often with star anise) and coloured orange; this version approximates the spice with cardamom and cinnamon and will look brown, not orange.
  • Allulose dissolves fully in cold drinks, where erythritol can leave grit.

Where it comes from

Thai tea · Bangkok · Southeast Asia · 1900s

Cha yen, iced milk tea, is sold by street vendors and cafés across Thailand. It is made from a strong black tea, typically a spiced, coloured blend, sweetened with sugar or condensed milk and finished with evaporated milk over ice. When and how the drink took its present form is not well documented.

Vietnamese iced coffee

Very strong coffee poured over a quick sugar-free coconut 'condensed milk' and plenty of ice.

Dairy-free 3.6 g net carbs Serves 2 · 1 glass prep 5 min · cook 20 min

Ingredients

Sugar-free coconut condensed milk

  • ½ cup full-fat coconut milk (canned) (113 g)
  • 3 tbsp allulose (36 g)
  • 0.03125 tsp fine salt

Coffee

  • 1 cup strong brewed coffee or espresso (237 g), very strong, ideally dark-roast brewed in a phin filter

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.6 g
Total carbohydrate21.6 g
Fibre0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose18.0 g
Sugars0.0 g
Fat12.3 g
Protein1.3 g
Energy129 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Simmer the coconut milk, allulose and salt in a small pan over low heat, stirring often, for 15–20 minutes, until reduced by about half and thick enough to coat a spoon. Allulose browns easily, so keep the heat low. Cool.
  2. Brew the coffee strong; a traditional phin filter set over the glass takes about 5 minutes.
  3. Put 2 tablespoons of the condensed coconut milk in each glass, pour in the hot coffee and stir well.
  4. Fill the glasses with ice, stir again and serve.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • The condensed coconut milk keeps a week in the fridge; warm it slightly to loosen before using.

Where it comes from

Cà phê sữa đá · Vietnam · Southeast Asia · 1900s

Coffee was introduced to Vietnam by the French in the nineteenth century, and Vietnam is now one of the world's largest coffee producers. Because fresh milk was scarce in the tropical heat, sweetened condensed milk became the usual partner, and the slow-drip phin filter the usual method. Served over ice, it became cà phê sữa đá, a street-café staple. This version swaps the sugary condensed milk for a reduced coconut milk.

"Noatmeal" hot cereal

A warm, porridge-like bowl of hemp hearts, chia and flax simmered in almond milk, with cinnamon and vanilla.

Dairy-free 4.5 g net carbs Serves 2 · 1 bowl prep 5 min · cook 5 min · rest 3 min

Ingredients

  • ⅓ cup hemp hearts (53 g)
  • 1 tbsp chia seeds (12 g)
  • 2 tbsp golden flaxseed meal (14 g)
  • 2 tbsp unsweetened shredded coconut (10 g)
  • 1 cup unsweetened almond milk (240 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tsp granulated erythritol
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tbsp almond butter, unsalted (16 g), to serve

Sugars2.3 g

Where it comes from

Porridge · Scotland · Northern Europe · medieval

Grain porridges are eaten almost everywhere, but oat porridge is especially associated with Scotland, where oats grew well in the cool, wet climate and were a staple for centuries. This grain-free version imitates the texture with seeds that thicken as they absorb liquid.

Almond-flour pancakes

Small, tender griddle pancakes from almond flour and eggs, best cooked over gentle heat so they set before they brown.

Dairy-free option 3.0 g net carbs Serves 4 · 3 pancakes prep 10 min · cook 15 min · rest 5 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • ¼ cup unsweetened almond milk (60 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), melted, plus a little for the pan

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.0 g2.9 g
Total carbohydrate8.4 g8.4 g
Fibre2.4 g2.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose3.0 g3.0 g
Sugars1.5 g1.5 g
Fat22.1 g21.9 g
Protein10.0 g9.9 g
Energy253 kcal252 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Whisk the almond flour, baking powder, salt and erythritol in a bowl.
  2. Whisk the eggs, almond milk, vanilla and melted butter in a second bowl, then stir into the dry ingredients. Let the batter stand 5 minutes; it thickens slightly.
  3. Heat a non-stick or cast-iron pan over medium-low heat and grease it lightly.
  4. Drop 2 tablespoons of batter per pancake, spreading each to about 8 cm (3 in). Keep them small: almond-flour pancakes have no gluten and break if made large.
  5. Cook 2–3 minutes, until the edges look dry and bubbles appear, then flip gently and cook 1–2 minutes more. Keep warm while you cook the rest.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter works the same here; use a neutral oil for greasing the pan if your spread spits.

Tips

  • If they brown before the middle sets, turn the heat down — almond flour scorches sooner than wheat.
  • A few raspberries or blueberries pressed into each pancake before flipping add little carb.

Where it comes from

Pancake · Athens · Southern Europe · ancient

Batters cooked on a hot pan or griddle are among the oldest prepared foods; ancient Greek and Roman writers describe flat cakes of flour and liquid fried and served with honey. Nearly every cuisine has its own version, from the thin crêpe to the thick American griddle cake.

Belgian waffles

Crisp-edged, deep-pocketed waffles made light with whipped egg whites folded into an almond-flour batter.

Dairy-free option 3.7 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 waffle prep 15 min · cook 25 min

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 4 large eggs, separated
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ¼ tsp cream of tartar

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.7 g3.7 g
Total carbohydrate10.9 g10.9 g
Fibre3.2 g3.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.0 g4.0 g
Sugars1.8 g1.8 g
Fat24.0 g23.8 g
Protein10.0 g9.9 g
Energy273 kcal273 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat a Belgian (deep-grid) waffle iron and grease it well.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder, salt and erythritol together.
  3. Whisk the egg yolks, almond milk, melted butter and vanilla, then stir into the dry ingredients to make a thick batter. Let it stand 5 minutes so the coconut flour absorbs the liquid.
  4. Whip the egg whites with the cream of tartar to firm peaks. Stir a third into the batter to loosen it, then fold in the rest gently.
  5. Spoon about ½ cup of batter into the iron, close and cook 4–5 minutes, until steam stops and the waffle releases easily. Grain-free waffles stick if opened early.
  6. Cool on a rack for a minute, not on a plate, so the outside stays crisp.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

With dairy-free butter the waffles are a little less browned; cook them 30 seconds longer.

Tips

  • They freeze well; re-crisp straight from frozen in a toaster.

Where it comes from

Waffle · Brussels · Western Europe · medieval

Waffles descend from the thin wafers baked between hinged iron plates in medieval Europe, especially in the Low Countries. The light, deep-grid Brussels waffle became a Belgian speciality in the 1800s, and the name "Belgian waffle" was popularised in the United States after world's fairs in the 1960s.

Blueberry muffins

Moist almond-flour muffins with a lemony crumb and a modest scatter of blueberries.

Dairy-free option 3.6 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 muffin prep 15 min · cook 25 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 2½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (240 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • ⅓ cup unsalted butter (75 g), melted
  • ⅓ cup unsweetened almond milk (79 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • 110 g blueberries

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.6 g3.6 g
Total carbohydrate14.2 g14.2 g
Fibre2.6 g2.6 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.0 g8.0 g
Sugars2.1 g2.1 g
Fat17.0 g16.9 g
Protein6.2 g6.2 g
Energy195 kcal194 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a 12-cup muffin tin with paper cases.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder, salt and erythritol in a large bowl.
  3. Whisk the eggs, melted butter, almond milk, vanilla and lemon zest together, then stir into the dry ingredients until just combined. The batter is thick.
  4. Fold in the blueberries and divide between the cases.
  5. Bake 22–25 minutes, until golden and a skewer comes out clean. Cool in the tin 10 minutes before lifting out; they are fragile while hot.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter gives a very similar muffin, slightly less rich.

Tips

  • Tossing the berries in a teaspoon of the dry mix helps stop them sinking.
  • Frozen berries work; add them still frozen and bake a few minutes longer.

Where it comes from

Muffin · Boston · North America · 1800s

The American muffin is a small quick bread, made possible by the chemical leaveners — pearlash, then baking soda and baking powder — that came into use in the 1800s. Blueberry muffins are especially associated with New England, where wild blueberries grow, and Massachusetts made the blueberry muffin its official state muffin in 1986.

Cinnamon rolls

Soft spirals of psyllium-bound almond dough rolled around butter, cinnamon and sweetener, finished with a cream-cheese glaze.

Dairy-free option 3.4 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 roll prep 30 min · cook 25 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

Dough

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 3 tbsp psyllium husk powder (27 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), melted
  • ½ cup water (118 g), warm

Filling

  • 3 tbsp unsalted butter (43 g), softened
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g)
  • 1 tbsp ground cinnamon
  • ¼ tsp ground cardamom

Glaze

  • 60 g full-fat cream cheese, softened
  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 1 tbsp unsweetened almond milk (15 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.4 g3.7 g
Total carbohydrate21.8 g22.1 g
Fibre5.6 g5.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose12.8 g12.8 g
Sugars1.5 g1.3 g
Fat20.7 g19.8 g
Protein6.4 g6.3 g
Energy236 kcal231 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and grease a 20 cm (8 in) round cake pan.
  2. For the dough, whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, psyllium, baking powder, salt and erythritol. Add the eggs, melted butter and warm water and mix to a soft dough. Let it rest 10 minutes; the psyllium firms it so it can be rolled.
  3. Roll the dough between two sheets of parchment into a rectangle about 25 × 35 cm (10 × 14 in).
  4. Spread with the softened butter and sprinkle with the allulose, cinnamon and cardamom.
  5. Using the parchment to help, roll up from a long side into a firm log. Chill 5 minutes, then cut into 8 slices with a sharp knife and set them cut side up in the pan.
  6. Bake 22–25 minutes, until golden. Allulose browns quickly, so cover loosely with foil if the tops darken before 20 minutes.
  7. Beat the glaze ingredients until smooth and spread over the warm rolls.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.

Dairy-free butter and cream cheese work well; the glaze is a little thinner, so add the almond milk a few drops at a time.

Tips

  • The dough cracks if rolled too thin — about 6 mm (¼ in) is right.
  • There is no yeast: grain-free dough has no gluten to trap gas, so baking powder does the lifting.

Where it comes from

Cinnamon roll · Stockholm · Northern Europe · 1920s

The Swedish kanelbulle, a cardamom-scented yeast bun rolled with cinnamon and butter, became a staple of Swedish coffee breaks in the decades after the First World War, as butter and sugar became more affordable. Sweden marks a Cinnamon Bun Day each 4 October. The American cinnamon roll belongs to the same northern European family.

Cream scones

Tender, lightly sweet scones from almond flour, cold butter and cream, cut in wedges and baked until golden.

Dairy-free option 3.2 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 scone prep 15 min · cook 20 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg
  • ⅓ cup heavy whipping cream (79 g), plus 1 tbsp for brushing
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.2 g3.6 g
Total carbohydrate12.2 g12.6 g
Fibre3.0 g3.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g6.0 g
Sugars1.7 g1.8 g
Fat22.7 g22.5 g
Protein6.6 g6.6 g
Energy245 kcal244 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Mix the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder, erythritol and salt. Rub in the cold butter with your fingertips until it looks like coarse crumbs.
  3. Whisk the egg, cream and vanilla, add to the bowl and stir with a fork until it comes together into a soft dough.
  4. Pat into a 18 cm (7 in) round about 2.5 cm (1 in) thick and cut into 8 wedges. Chill 10 minutes so they hold their shape.
  5. Separate the wedges on the sheet, brush with a little cream and bake 18–20 minutes, until golden.
  6. Cool on the sheet 5 minutes before moving; they firm up as they cool.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream gives a slightly denser scone with a faint coconut note; dairy-free butter should be very cold, as it softens faster than butter.

Tips

  • Serve with whipped cream and a spoonful of crushed berries.

Where it comes from

Scone · Edinburgh · Northern Europe · 1500s

The scone is Scottish: the word appears in Scottish writing in the early 1500s. Early scones were flat rounds of oats or barley cooked on a griddle and cut into wedges; the oven-baked, risen scone came with baking powder in the 1800s and became part of the British afternoon tea.

Crêpes

Thin, flexible egg-based crêpes held together with a little psyllium, for folding around sweet or savoury fillings.

Dairy-free option 1.8 g net carbs Serves 4 · 2 crêpes prep 10 min · cook 20 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 4 large eggs
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • ¼ cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (24 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • 1 tsp psyllium husk powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), melted, plus extra for the pan

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.8 g1.7 g
Total carbohydrate3.6 g3.6 g
Fibre1.8 g1.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g0.0 g
Sugars0.9 g0.9 g
Fat14.2 g14.0 g
Protein8.1 g8.0 g
Energy171 kcal171 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, psyllium husk powder.

Method

  1. Blend all the ingredients until completely smooth, about 30 seconds.
  2. Let the batter rest 10 minutes so the psyllium and coconut flour hydrate; it should be like thin cream. Add a spoonful of water if it is thicker.
  3. Heat a 20 cm (8 in) non-stick pan over medium heat and brush with butter.
  4. Pour in 3 tablespoons of batter and swirl at once to coat the base thinly.
  5. Cook about 1 minute, until the edge lifts and the underside is lightly golden. Loosen with a thin spatula, flip and cook 20–30 seconds more.
  6. Stack on a plate and repeat, stirring the batter each time.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter gives slightly paler crêpes but the texture is the same.

Tips

  • Fill with whipped cream and a few berries, or with ham and spinach for a savoury version.

Where it comes from

Crêpe · Quimper, Brittany · Western Europe · medieval

Brittany is the home of the crêpe. Buckwheat, which grows on poor soil, spread there in the late Middle Ages, and thin buckwheat galettes became a staple; sweeter wheat crêpes followed as white flour became affordable. Crêperies carried the dish across France and beyond.

Dutch baby

A big oven pancake that puffs in a hot skillet and settles into a custardy centre, served with lemon and powdered sweetener.

Dairy-free option 3.4 g net carbs Serves 4 · 1 wedge prep 10 min · cook 20 min

Ingredients

  • 4 large eggs
  • ½ cup whole milk (122 g)
  • ⅓ cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (32 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • 1 tbsp granulated erythritol (12 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 3 tbsp unsalted butter (43 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g), to serve
  • 1 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style), to serve

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.4 g2.2 g
Total carbohydrate9.8 g8.7 g
Fibre1.4 g1.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose5.0 g5.0 g
Sugars2.6 g1.3 g
Fat18.8 g17.9 g
Protein9.4 g8.5 g
Energy225 kcal211 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Put a 25 cm (10 in) cast-iron or ovenproof skillet in the oven and heat to 220 °C (425 °F).
  2. Blend the eggs, milk, almond flour, coconut flour, erythritol, vanilla and salt until smooth and frothy, about 1 minute.
  3. Take the hot skillet out, add the butter and swirl until melted and foaming. Pour in the batter at once.
  4. Bake 15–18 minutes without opening the door, until puffed and well browned at the edge.
  5. Squeeze lemon juice over, dust with powdered erythritol and cut into wedges straight away; it sinks as it cools.

Dairy-free version

  • Use unsweetened almond milk instead of whole milk.
  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Without gluten it rises less than a wheat Dutch baby — expect puffed edges and a flatter, custardy middle. The dairy-free version browns a little less.

Tips

  • A very hot pan matters more than anything; do not skip preheating it.

Where it comes from

Dutch baby · Seattle · North America · early 1900s

The Dutch baby is an American dish adapted from German oven pancakes; "Dutch" is generally taken as a corruption of Deutsch. It is often credited to a Seattle family café in the early 1900s, where the name is said to have been coined.
