French toast

Slices of a quick almond-flour loaf soaked in cinnamon custard and fried in butter until crisp outside and soft inside.

Dairy-free option 3.7 g net carbs Serves 4 · 2 slices prep 15 min · cook 40 min · rest 20 min

Ingredients

Quick loaf

  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp psyllium husk powder
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 3 large eggs
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), melted
  • 2 tbsp unsweetened almond milk (30 g)

Custard

  • 2 large eggs
  • ¼ cup heavy whipping cream (60 g)
  • ½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • 2 tsp granulated erythritol

To cook

  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.7 g3.7 g
Total carbohydrate10.1 g10.1 g
Fibre4.4 g4.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose2.0 g2.0 g
Sugars2.0 g1.5 g
Fat35.5 g33.0 g
Protein13.6 g13.4 g
Energy394 kcal372 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol, psyllium husk powder.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a 20 × 20 cm (8 × 8 in) pan with parchment.
  2. Whisk the loaf's dry ingredients, then beat in the eggs, melted butter and almond milk. Spread in the pan and bake 20–25 minutes, until firm and golden.
  3. Cool completely — at least 20 minutes — then cut into 8 slices. Warm bread falls apart in the custard.
  4. Whisk the custard ingredients in a shallow dish.
  5. Melt half the butter in a large frying pan over medium-low heat. Dip each slice briefly, about 5 seconds a side; it absorbs fast.
  6. Fry 2–3 minutes per side until golden, adding the rest of the butter for the second batch. Serve hot.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of heavy whipping cream.

With dairy-free butter and coconut milk the slices brown a little less; fry them over slightly lower heat for longer.

Tips

  • Day-old slices of the almond-flour sandwich loaf work just as well and skip the first step.
  • Top with a few berries and powdered sweetener.

Where it comes from

French toast · Rome · Southern Europe · ancient

Bread soaked in milk and egg and fried is very old: a Roman recipe collection attributed to Apicius describes it. Medieval Europe knew it by names such as pain perdu, "lost bread", because it rescued stale loaves. "French toast" is an English-language name; the dish itself is common to much of Europe.

Grain-free granola

Clusters of toasted nuts, seeds and coconut bound with egg white and baked low so they crisp without burning.

Dairy-free 3.0 g net carbs Serves 12 · ⅓ cup prep 10 min · cook 30 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup pecans (109 g), roughly chopped
  • 1 cup almonds (143 g), roughly chopped
  • 1 cup unsweetened shredded coconut (80 g)
  • ½ cup pumpkin seeds (pepitas) (64 g)
  • ½ cup sunflower seeds, hulled (70 g)
  • 2 tbsp golden flaxseed meal (14 g)
  • 3 tbsp granulated erythritol (36 g)
  • 1½ tsp ground cinnamon
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 1 large egg white
  • 3 tbsp coconut oil (41 g), melted
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.0 g
Total carbohydrate10.8 g
Fibre4.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose3.0 g
Sugars1.7 g
Fat26.3 g
Protein7.2 g
Energy280 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C (300 °F) and line a large baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Mix the nuts, coconut, seeds, flaxseed meal, erythritol, cinnamon and salt in a large bowl.
  3. Whisk the egg white until frothy, then whisk in the coconut oil and vanilla. Pour over the dry mix and stir until everything is coated.
  4. Spread in an even layer about 1 cm (½ in) thick and press down firmly with a spatula.
  5. Bake 25–30 minutes, rotating the pan halfway, until golden. Coconut darkens fast at the end, so check from 20 minutes.
  6. Let it cool completely on the pan without stirring — it crisps as it cools — then break into clusters. Store airtight up to 2 weeks.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Serve with almond milk or coconut yogurt and a few berries.

Where it comes from

Granola · Dansville, New York · North America · 1860s

Granola began in American health reform. In 1863 James Caleb Jackson, who ran a sanatorium in Dansville, New York, made "granula" from baked whole-grain flour broken into pieces. John Harvey Kellogg later sold a similar product under the name granola, and the toasted oat-and-nut mix familiar today became popular in the 1960s.

Green shakshuka

Eggs poached in a skillet of softened onion, zucchini and spinach — a green take on the North African tomato dish.

Dairy-free 5.9 g net carbs Serves 3 · 2 eggs prep 10 min · cook 25 min

Ingredients

  • 2 tbsp olive oil (27 g)
  • ⅓ onion, finely chopped
  • 2 garlic cloves, sliced
  • 1 tsp ground cumin
  • ¼ tsp cayenne pepper or chili flakes, or chili flakes, to taste
  • 1 zucchini, diced
  • 200 g fresh spinach
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp ground black pepper
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 6 large eggs
  • 1 tbsp fresh mint leaves, chopped
  • 2 tbsp fresh basil leaves, torn
  • 3 tbsp unsweetened coconut yogurt (43 g), to serve

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs5.9 g
Total carbohydrate8.8 g
Fibre2.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars3.2 g
Fat21.1 g
Protein15.9 g
Energy283 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: unsweetened coconut yogurt.

Method

  1. Heat the olive oil in a 25 cm (10 in) skillet over medium heat. Cook the onion 5–6 minutes until soft.
  2. Add the garlic, cumin and cayenne and stir 30 seconds until fragrant, then add the zucchini and cook 5 minutes, stirring, until tender.
  3. Add the spinach a handful at a time, letting each wilt. Season with the salt and pepper and stir in the lemon juice.
  4. Make 6 hollows in the greens and crack an egg into each. Cover and cook over low heat 6–8 minutes, until the whites are set and the yolks still soft.
  5. Scatter with mint and basil and serve with spoonfuls of coconut yogurt.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Squeeze excess liquid from the spinach if using frozen, or the pan will be watery.

Where it comes from

Shakshuka · Tunis · Middle East & North Africa · by the 1900s

Shakshuka — eggs cooked in a spiced sauce, usually of tomatoes and peppers — is a dish of the Maghreb, most often credited to Tunisia. Tunisian and Libyan Jewish communities carried it to Israel, where it became a breakfast staple. Green versions built on leafy vegetables are a modern variation.

Lemon poppy-seed muffins

Bright, tangy almond-flour muffins flecked with poppy seeds and brushed with a lemon glaze.

Dairy-free option 3.1 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 muffin prep 15 min · cook 22 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

  • 2½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (240 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • 1½ tbsp poppy seeds (14 g)
  • 3 large eggs
  • ¼ cup unsalted butter (57 g), melted
  • ⅓ cup sour cream (76 g)
  • 1 tbsp lemon zest
  • 2 tbsp fresh lemon juice (30 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Glaze

  • ¼ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (30 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.1 g3.0 g
Total carbohydrate16.2 g16.3 g
Fibre2.7 g2.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose10.5 g10.5 g
Sugars1.5 g1.4 g
Fat17.4 g16.9 g
Protein6.5 g6.4 g
Energy197 kcal194 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol, powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a 12-cup muffin tin with paper cases.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder, salt, erythritol and poppy seeds.
  3. Whisk the eggs, melted butter, sour cream, lemon zest, lemon juice and vanilla, then stir into the dry ingredients until just combined.
  4. Divide between the cases and bake 20–22 minutes, until golden and springy.
  5. Stir the powdered erythritol and lemon juice into a thin glaze and brush over the muffins while they are still warm. Cool 15 minutes in the tin.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of sour cream.

Coconut yogurt keeps them moist but slightly less tangy; add an extra teaspoon of lemon zest.

Tips

  • Rub the zest into the erythritol first to release more lemon oil.

Where it comes from

Muffin · United States · North America · late 1900s

Poppy seed is an old ingredient in Central and Eastern European baking. Paired with lemon in an American-style quick-bread muffin, it became a familiar bakery and coffee-shop flavour in the United States by the late 1900s.

Menemen

Turkish soft-scrambled eggs folded through tomatoes and peppers that have been softened slowly in olive oil.

Dairy-free 5.4 g net carbs Serves 2 · about 1 cup prep 10 min · cook 15 min

Ingredients

  • 2 tbsp olive oil (27 g)
  • ½ red bell pepper, seeded and thinly sliced
  • 250 g ripe tomato, about 2 medium, peeled and chopped
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp cayenne pepper or chili flakes, chili flakes are traditional
  • 4 large eggs
  • ⅛ tsp ground black pepper
  • 1 tbsp fresh flat-leaf parsley, chopped

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs5.4 g
Total carbohydrate7.7 g
Fibre2.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars4.9 g
Fat23.4 g
Protein14.1 g
Energy294 kcal

Contains: eggs.

Method

  1. Warm the olive oil in a 20–23 cm (8–9 in) pan over medium heat. Cook the pepper 4–5 minutes until soft.
  2. Add the tomatoes and salt and cook 6–8 minutes, stirring, until most of the liquid has cooked off and the mixture looks jammy. Stir in the chili.
  3. Crack in the eggs. Leave them 30 seconds, then stir gently and slowly, folding the tomato through, until the eggs are just set in soft, glossy curds. Take the pan off the heat while they still look slightly underdone.
  4. Season with black pepper, scatter with parsley and serve at once.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Turkish cooks usually use long, mild green peppers (sivri biber); a red bell pepper is sweeter but works.
  • Weigh the tomatoes: they are the main source of carbs in the dish.

Where it comes from

Menemen · Menemen, İzmir · Middle East & North Africa · 1900s

Menemen is a staple Turkish breakfast of eggs scrambled with tomatoes, peppers and olive oil. Its name is shared with a town near İzmir on the Aegean coast, and the dish is often said to come from there, though that link is popular tradition rather than documented history. Whether onion belongs in it is a long-running, good-humoured argument in Turkey.

See it on the map

Quiche Lorraine

Smoky bacon set in a nutmeg-scented egg custard in a crisp almond-flour crust — made, as in Lorraine, without cheese.

Dairy-free option 4.3 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 slice prep 25 min · cook 50 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

Crust

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cold, cubed
  • 1 large egg

Filling

  • 6 slices of bacon
  • 4 large eggs
  • 1 large egg yolk
  • 1¼ cups heavy whipping cream (298 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp ground black pepper
  • ⅛ tsp ground nutmeg

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs4.3 g4.3 g
Total carbohydrate7.1 g7.1 g
Fibre2.8 g2.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g0.0 g
Sugars3.0 g1.5 g
Fat53.5 g45.9 g
Protein16.4 g15.9 g
Energy562 kcal491 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F).
  2. Pulse the almond flour, coconut flour, salt and butter in a food processor until crumbly, add the egg and pulse to a dough. Press evenly into a 23 cm (9 in) tart or pie pan, prick all over and bake 12–15 minutes until lightly golden.
  3. While the crust bakes, cut the bacon into 1 cm (½ in) strips and fry until crisp. Drain on paper towels.
  4. Whisk the eggs, yolk, cream, salt, pepper and nutmeg until smooth but not frothy.
  5. Scatter the bacon over the warm crust and pour in the custard.
  6. Bake 30–35 minutes, until the edge is set and the centre wobbles slightly. Rest 15 minutes before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut milk sets a softer custard with a gentle coconut note that the bacon mostly covers; rest it 30 minutes before slicing.

Tips

  • Cover the crust edge with foil if it browns before the filling sets.

Where it comes from

Quiche · Nancy, Lorraine · Western Europe · by the 1500s

Quiche Lorraine comes from the Lorraine region of north-eastern France, where an open tart of eggs, cream and smoked bacon was originally baked in a bread-dough base; its history is often traced to the 1500s. Cheese, now commonly added, is not part of the traditional recipe. The dish became widely fashionable in Britain and the United States after the Second World War.

Shakshuka

Eggs poached right in a skillet of tomato and red pepper sauce warmed with cumin, paprika and a little heat.

Dairy-free 5.2 g net carbs Serves 4 · 2 eggs with sauce prep 10 min · cook 30 min

Ingredients

  • 3 tbsp olive oil (40 g)
  • ¼ onion, finely chopped
  • 1 red bell pepper, seeded and diced
  • 3 garlic cloves, thinly sliced
  • 1 tsp ground cumin
  • 1½ tsp sweet or smoked paprika
  • ¼ tsp cayenne pepper or chili flakes, more or less to taste
  • 400 g canned diced tomatoes in juice, one standard can, with its juice
  • ¾ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp ground black pepper
  • 8 large eggs
  • 3 tbsp fresh flat-leaf parsley, chopped (11 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs5.2 g
Total carbohydrate8.4 g
Fibre3.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars4.6 g
Fat20.3 g
Protein14.2 g
Energy268 kcal

Contains: eggs.

Method

  1. Warm the olive oil in a 25–28 cm (10–11 in) skillet over medium heat. Add the onion and pepper and cook 8–10 minutes, stirring now and then, until the pepper is soft and starting to colour.
  2. Stir in the garlic, cumin, paprika and cayenne and cook 1 minute, just until fragrant; do not let the spices scorch.
  3. Add the tomatoes with their juice, the salt and the pepper. Simmer uncovered 10–12 minutes, stirring occasionally, until thick enough that a spoon dragged through leaves a trail. Taste and adjust the seasoning.
  4. Make 8 hollows in the sauce with the back of a spoon and crack an egg into each. Lower the heat, cover and cook 6–8 minutes, until the whites are set but the yolks still run.
  5. Scatter with parsley and serve straight from the pan.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Simmering the sauce until thick matters: a watery sauce lets the whites spread and turn rubbery before the yolks are done.
  • Canned tomatoes carry most of the carbs here; if you swap in fresh tomatoes, weigh them rather than counting by the piece.
  • Crumbled feta is a common topping, but it is not part of this dairy-free version.

Where it comes from

Shakshuka · Tunis · Middle East & North Africa · by the 1900s

Shakshuka is a dish of the Maghreb, eggs cooked in a spiced sauce of tomatoes and peppers, and it is most commonly credited to Tunisia; its exact origin is not documented. Tomatoes and peppers reached North Africa from the Americas only after the 1500s, so the dish in its present form is relatively recent. Jewish immigrants from Tunisia and Libya brought it to Israel, where it was widely adopted and is now a breakfast staple.

See it on the map

Spinach and zucchini frittata

Energy151 kcal

Contains: eggs.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 190 °C (375 °F).
  2. Heat the olive oil in a 25 cm (10 in) ovenproof non-stick skillet over medium heat. Cook the onion and zucchini 6–8 minutes until soft and lightly coloured.
  3. Add the garlic and spinach and cook until the spinach wilts and the pan is fairly dry.
  4. Beat the eggs with the salt, pepper and basil. Pour into the pan and stir gently once to spread the vegetables.
  5. Cook over medium-low heat without stirring for 4–5 minutes, until the edge is set.
  6. Move to the oven and bake 10–12 minutes, until the centre is just set. Rest 5 minutes, then loosen and cut into wedges.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Good at room temperature, which is how it is often eaten in Italy.

Where it comes from

Frittata · Rome · Southern Europe · by the 1800s

Frittata is the Italian word for a flat egg dish fried in a pan, cooked through rather than folded like a French omelette, and often filled with whatever vegetables are in season. It appears in Italian household cookbooks of the 1800s and was eaten long before.

See it on the map

Almond-flour sandwich loaf

A sliceable, sturdy sandwich loaf from almond flour and psyllium that toasts well.

Dairy-free 2.6 g net carbs Serves 14 · 1 slice prep 15 min · cook 1 h 10 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 2½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (240 g)
  • ½ cup psyllium husk powder (72 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 3 tsp baking powder (14 g)
  • 1½ tsp fine salt
  • 5 large egg whites
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 2 tbsp olive oil (27 g)
  • 1½ cups water (356 g), boiling

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.6 g
Total carbohydrate8.7 g
Fibre6.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars1.1 g
Fat11.5 g
Protein5.7 g
Energy144 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a 23 × 13 cm (9 × 5 in) loaf pan with parchment.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, psyllium, coconut flour, baking powder and salt in a large bowl.
  3. Add the egg whites, egg, lemon juice and olive oil and stir briefly, then pour in the boiling water and mix vigorously until it forms a smooth, thick dough.
  4. With wet hands, shape into a log and press into the pan, smoothing the top. Score a line down the middle.
  5. Bake 65–75 minutes, until deep golden and hollow-sounding when tapped underneath. If the top browns early, cover loosely with foil.
  6. Cool completely in the pan, at least 1 hour, before slicing; cut warm, the crumb is gummy.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Psyllium gives this dough a bread-like chew; brands differ, and some darken the crumb purple-grey — it is harmless.
  • Slice the whole loaf and freeze it; toast slices straight from frozen.

Where it comes from

Sandwich loaf · Chillicothe, Missouri · North America · 1928

The tall, square-sided pan loaf became the standard sandwich bread with industrial baking. Pre-sliced bread was first sold commercially in 1928 by a bakery in Chillicothe, Missouri, using Otto Rohwedder's slicing machine. This grain-free loaf is a modern low-carb imitation of that everyday bread.

Cheese bread puffs

A low-carb take on Brazilian pão de queijo: small golden puffs of almond flour, egg, cream cheese and parmesan.

Dairy-free option 1.8 g net carbs Serves 12 · 1 puff prep 15 min · cook 22 min · rest 5 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 1 tbsp psyllium husk powder
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 60 g full-fat cream cheese, softened
  • ½ cup grated parmesan (50 g)
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 tbsp olive oil (14 g)
  • 1 garlic clove, finely grated

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.8 g1.7 g
Total carbohydrate3.2 g3.6 g
Fibre1.4 g1.9 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g0.0 g
Sugars0.6 g0.5 g
Fat9.0 g7.4 g
Protein4.3 g4.1 g
Energy106 kcal94 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: psyllium husk powder.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 190 °C (375 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, psyllium, baking powder and salt.
  3. Beat the cream cheese, parmesan, eggs, olive oil and garlic together, then stir in the dry ingredients to make a thick, sticky dough. Rest 5 minutes.
  4. With wet hands, roll into 12 balls about 3 cm (1¼ in) across and space them on the sheet.
  5. Bake 18–22 minutes, until puffed and golden. Eat warm.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.
  • Use nutritional yeast flakes instead of grated parmesan (0.5 × the amount) — ¼ cup nutritional yeast for the ½ cup parmesan.

Nutritional yeast gives the dairy-free puffs a savoury, cheesy flavour, though milder and nuttier than parmesan, and the puffs are a little less rich. Add a pinch more salt.

Tips

  • Traditional pão de queijo gets its stretch from tapioca starch, which this version leaves out; expect a soft, tender crumb instead.

Where it comes from

Pão de queijo · Minas Gerais · Latin America & Caribbean · 1700s

Pão de queijo comes from Minas Gerais in Brazil, where it is made from tapioca (cassava) starch and local cured cheese, giving a crisp shell and a chewy, stretchy middle. Its roots are usually traced to the region's colonial era; it became a national snack in the twentieth century. This recipe is a low-carb take that replaces the tapioca with almond flour.

See it on the map

Cloud bread

Feather-light rounds of whipped egg white and cream cheese — a modern low-carb invention for sandwiches.

Dairy-free option 0.7 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 round prep 10 min · cook 30 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 3 large eggs, separated
  • ¼ tsp cream of tartar
  • 45 g full-fat cream cheese, softened
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ¼ tsp baking powder

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs0.7 g1.0 g
Total carbohydrate0.7 g1.1 g
Fibre0.0 g0.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g0.0 g
Sugars0.4 g0.2 g
Fat5.0 g4.3 g
Protein3.6 g3.5 g
Energy62 kcal58 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C (300 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Whip the egg whites with the cream of tartar to stiff, glossy peaks.
  3. In another bowl, beat the yolks, cream cheese, salt and baking powder until smooth.
  4. Fold a third of the whites into the yolk mixture to lighten it, then fold in the rest gently, keeping as much air as you can.
  5. Spoon into 6 rounds about 1.5 cm (½ in) thick on the sheet.
  6. Bake 25–30 minutes, until golden and firm to a light touch. Cool on the sheet; they firm and become flexible as they rest.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese.

Dairy-free cream cheese makes a looser yolk mixture, so the rounds spread more and bake flatter; fold very gently and bake a few minutes longer.

Tips

  • Store in a bag with the rounds separated by parchment; they turn soft and bread-like overnight.

Where it comes from

Cloud bread · United States · North America · 2000s

Cloud bread is a modern invention of low-carb home cooks, circulating in the United States from the 2000s and later popular on social media. It imitates soft sandwich bread using whipped egg whites for lift; there is no traditional dish behind it.

See it on the map

Coconut-flour tortillas

Pliable, grain-free tortillas from coconut flour and psyllium, cooked in a dry skillet for tacos or wraps.

Dairy-free 2.1 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 tortilla prep 15 min · cook 15 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • ½ cup coconut flour (56 g)
  • 2 tbsp psyllium husk powder (18 g)
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • ½ tsp baking powder
  • 1 cup water (237 g), hot
  • 2 tbsp olive oil (27 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.1 g
Total carbohydrate6.2 g
Fibre4.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars0.9 g
Fat4.3 g
Protein1.4 g
Energy62 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Whisk the coconut flour, psyllium, salt and baking powder.
  2. Add the hot water and olive oil and stir, then knead for a minute into a smooth, slightly tacky dough. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Divide into 8 balls. Roll each between two sheets of parchment into a thin round about 15 cm (6 in) across; a tortilla press also works.
  4. Heat a dry cast-iron or non-stick pan over medium-high heat. Cook each tortilla 1–1½ minutes per side, until spotted brown.
  5. Stack under a clean towel as they come off the pan; the steam keeps them soft.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • If the dough cracks when rolled, knead in another tablespoon of hot water.
  • Coconut flour varies in how much water it absorbs; adjust by the spoonful.

Where it comes from

Tortilla · Valley of Mexico · Latin America & Caribbean · ancient

Tortillas are an ancient Mesoamerican food, made from maize cooked with lime (nixtamalised) and ground into masa, then cooked on a clay griddle called a comal. Wheat-flour tortillas came later, after the Spanish brought wheat. This version is a modern low-carb stand-in that uses neither.

Flax crackers

Crisp, savoury crackers of flaxseed meal, chia and sesame with garlic and rosemary — no flour at all.

Dairy-free 0.8 g net carbs Serves 8 · 6 crackers prep 10 min · cook 35 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup golden flaxseed meal (112 g)
  • 2 tbsp chia seeds (24 g)
  • 2 tbsp sesame seeds (18 g)
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tsp fresh rosemary, chopped, very finely chopped
  • 1 garlic clove, grated
  • ½ cup water (118 g)
  • 1 tbsp olive oil (14 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs0.8 g
Total carbohydrate6.0 g
Fibre5.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars0.2 g
Fat9.6 g
Protein3.5 g
Energy118 kcal

Contains: sesame.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 160 °C (325 °F).
  2. Mix all the ingredients and let stand 10 minutes, until the flax and chia absorb the water into a thick, spreadable paste.
  3. Spread between two sheets of parchment and roll as thin as you can, about 3 mm (⅛ in), into a rectangle roughly 25 × 35 cm (10 × 14 in). Peel off the top sheet.
  4. Score into 48 small crackers with a knife and slide the parchment onto a baking sheet.
  5. Bake 30–35 minutes, until dry and firm. Turn off the oven and leave them inside 30 minutes to crisp.
  6. Break along the scores and store airtight up to a week.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Thin edges brown first; if the middle is still soft, break off the edges and bake the centre a little longer.
  • If they soften in storage, re-crisp 5 minutes in a warm oven.

Where it comes from

Seed cracker · United States · North America · late 1900s

Flax crackers are a modern health-food invention rather than a traditional dish, made popular by raw-food and low-carb cooks because flaxseed binds into a sheet when soaked. Flax itself is one of the oldest cultivated crops, domesticated in the ancient Near East.

Irish soda bread

A rustic, cross-cut round raised with baking soda and soured almond milk, best warm with butter.

Dairy-free option 3.1 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 slice prep 15 min · cook 45 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 2½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (240 g)
  • 3 tbsp coconut flour (21 g)
  • 2 tbsp psyllium husk powder (18 g)
  • 1 tsp baking soda
  • ¾ tsp fine salt
  • 1½ tsp caraway seeds, optional, for seed bread
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • 1 tbsp apple cider vinegar (15 g)
  • 2 large eggs
  • 3 tbsp unsalted butter (43 g), melted

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.1 g3.1 g
Total carbohydrate7.7 g7.7 g
Fibre4.7 g4.7 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g0.0 g
Sugars1.5 g1.5 g
Fat17.5 g17.4 g
Protein7.0 g7.0 g
Energy202 kcal201 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, psyllium husk powder.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment.
  2. Stir the vinegar into the almond milk and leave 5 minutes; it stands in for buttermilk, and the acid is what activates the baking soda.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, psyllium, baking soda, salt and caraway, if using.
  4. Beat the eggs and melted butter into the soured milk, add to the dry ingredients and mix to a soft dough. Bake promptly — the soda starts working as soon as it is wet.
  5. Shape into a round about 5 cm (2 in) high on the sheet and cut a deep cross in the top.
  6. Bake 40–45 minutes, until deep golden and hollow-sounding underneath. Cool at least 30 minutes before slicing.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Dairy-free butter makes almost no difference here; the bread is already made with almond milk.

Tips

  • Measure the baking soda exactly — too much tastes soapy in a bread with no wheat to mask it.
  • With caraway this becomes seed bread, a traditional Irish variation; leave it out for plain soda bread.

Where it comes from

Soda bread · Ireland · Northern Europe · 1800s

Soda bread spread in Ireland in the 1800s, after bicarbonate of soda became available. Irish soft wheat made poor yeast bread, but mixed with buttermilk and soda it rose well and could be baked quickly in a pot over the fire. The cross cut in the top helps the round bake through.

See it on the map

Naan-style flatbread

Soft, blistered skillet flatbreads made with yogurt, brushed with garlic butter.

Dairy-free option 3.4 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 flatbread prep 15 min · cook 20 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp coconut flour (14 g)
  • 2 tbsp psyllium husk powder (18 g)
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • ½ cup plain whole-milk Greek yogurt (114 g)
  • ⅓ cup water (78 g), warm
  • 1 tbsp olive oil (14 g)

Garlic butter

  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter (28 g), melted
  • 1 garlic clove, minced

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs3.4 g3.4 g
Total carbohydrate8.1 g8.5 g
Fibre4.8 g5.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g0.0 g
Sugars1.8 g1.2 g
Fat15.8 g17.2 g
Protein5.7 g4.2 g
Energy183 kcal193 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, psyllium, baking powder and salt.
  2. Stir in the yogurt, warm water and olive oil and knead briefly into a soft dough. Cover and rest 10 minutes.
  3. Divide into 6 and roll each between two sheets of parchment into an oval about 3 mm (⅛ in) thick.
  4. Heat a dry cast-iron pan over medium-high heat. Peel off the top sheet, flip a flatbread into the pan and peel off the second sheet.
  5. Cook 1½–2 minutes per side, until blistered and spotted brown.
  6. Mix the melted butter with the garlic and brush over the hot flatbreads.

Dairy-free version

  • Use unsweetened coconut yogurt instead of plain whole-milk Greek yogurt.
  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

Coconut yogurt is thinner than Greek yogurt; hold back a tablespoon of the water and add it only if the dough feels stiff.

Tips

  • Psyllium gives this dough a bread-like chew; brands differ, and some darken the crumb purple-grey — it is harmless.

Where it comes from

Naan · Delhi · South Asia · medieval

Naan is a leavened flatbread of South and Central Asia, traditionally slapped onto the wall of a clay tandoor oven. It is recorded at the courts of medieval Delhi, and today is closely associated with the cooking of northern India and Pakistan.

Pizza crust

A thin, crisp-bottomed pizza base from almond flour and psyllium, pre-baked so it holds any topping.

Dairy-free 4.1 g net carbs Serves 4 · ¼ pizza (crust only) prep 15 min · cook 20 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 2 tbsp psyllium husk powder (18 g)
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tbsp olive oil (27 g)
  • ⅓ cup water (78 g), warm
  • 1 garlic clove, grated, optional

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs4.1 g
Total carbohydrate11.3 g
Fibre7.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars1.7 g
Fat26.9 g
Protein9.4 g
Energy301 kcal

Contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C (400 °F) with a baking sheet or pizza stone inside.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, psyllium, baking powder and salt. Add the egg, olive oil, warm water and garlic and mix to a firm dough. Rest 10 minutes.

Where it comes from

Pizza · Naples · Southern Europe · 1700s

Flatbreads with toppings are ancient around the Mediterranean, but pizza as we know it took shape in Naples in the 1700s and 1800s, sold as cheap street food. A popular story says the Margherita was named for Queen Margherita's visit in 1889, though the details are disputed. Italian emigrants carried pizza worldwide in the twentieth century.

Rosemary focaccia

A dimpled, olive-oil-rich flatbread from a psyllium-bound almond dough, topped with rosemary and flaky salt.

Dairy-free 3.4 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1 square prep 15 min · cook 30 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 5 tbsp psyllium husk powder (45 g)
  • 1 tbsp coconut flour
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 3 large egg whites
  • 2 tsp fresh lemon juice (10 g)
  • 1 cup water (237 g), boiling
  • 4 tbsp olive oil (54 g), divided
  • 2 tbsp fresh rosemary, chopped
  • 1 garlic clove, thinly sliced

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.4 g
Total carbohydrate10.6 g
Fibre7.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars1.4 g
Fat19.6 g
Protein6.8 g
Energy224 kcal

Contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Pour 2 tablespoons of the olive oil into a 20 × 30 cm (8 × 12 in) baking pan and tilt to coat.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, psyllium, coconut flour, baking powder and half the salt.
  3. Add the egg whites and lemon juice, then pour in the boiling water and mix quickly with a spatula until a soft dough forms. Rest 10 minutes; it firms up as the psyllium sets.
  4. With oiled hands, press the dough into the pan to the edges.
  5. Press deep dimples all over with your fingertips. Drizzle with the remaining oil and scatter with rosemary, garlic and the remaining salt.
  6. Bake 28–32 minutes, until golden and firm. Cool 10 minutes in the pan, then cut into 8 squares.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Use powdered psyllium husk, not whole husks, which need different quantities.

Where it comes from

Focaccia · Genoa, Liguria · Southern Europe · ancient

Focaccia takes its name from the Latin panis focacius, bread baked on the hearth (focus), and flat hearth breads were common in the Roman world. Liguria, and Genoa in particular, is now the home of the classic oily, dimpled focaccia eaten at breakfast and through the day.

Sesame bagels

Chewy, ring-shaped rolls from a psyllium almond dough, briefly boiled and baked with a sesame crust.

Dairy-free 3.4 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1 bagel prep 20 min · cook 50 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 1¼ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (120 g)
  • 5 tbsp psyllium husk powder (45 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 3 large egg whites
  • 2 tsp fresh lemon juice (10 g)
  • 1 cup water (237 g), boiling
  • 1 large egg, beaten, for brushing
  • 2 tbsp sesame seeds (18 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.4 g
Total carbohydrate11.7 g
Fibre8.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars1.1 g
Fat12.9 g
Protein7.8 g
Energy172 kcal

Contains: eggs, sesame, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F) and line a baking sheet with parchment. Bring a wide pan of water to a gentle simmer.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, psyllium, baking powder and salt. Add the egg whites and lemon juice, then the boiling water, and mix quickly into a dough. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. With wet hands, divide into 6 and roll each into a ball. Push a finger through the middle and widen the hole to about 3 cm (1¼ in); it closes up in the oven.
  4. Lower the bagels into the simmering water two at a time for 30 seconds, then lift out with a slotted spoon onto the sheet.
  5. Brush with beaten egg and sprinkle generously with sesame seeds.
  6. Bake 45–50 minutes, until well browned and hollow-sounding when tapped underneath. Cool on a rack before slicing.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Psyllium gives this dough a bread-like chew; brands differ, and some darken the crumb purple-grey — it is harmless.
  • The short simmer sets the surface and helps a shiny crust; skip it if the dough feels too soft to handle.

Where it comes from

Bagel · Kraków · Central & Eastern Europe · 1600s

The bagel is a Polish-Jewish bread. Records from Kraków in the early 1600s are often cited as the first written mention of bajgiel. Jewish immigrants carried the boiled-then-baked ring to North America around 1900, where it became a New York staple.

Southern-style "cornbread" (no corn)

An unsweetened skillet bread with a crisp, buttery crust, in the Southern style — made without any corn.

Dairy-free option 2.7 g net carbs Serves 9 · 1 wedge prep 10 min · cook 25 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), divided
  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • 3 tbsp coconut flour (21 g)
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • ¼ tsp baking soda
  • ¾ tsp fine salt
  • 3 large eggs
  • ½ cup unsweetened almond milk (120 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.7 g2.6 g
Total carbohydrate5.0 g5.0 g
Fibre2.4 g2.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g0.0 g
Sugars1.3 g1.3 g
Fat15.5 g15.4 g
Protein6.1 g6.0 g
Energy176 kcal176 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour.

Method

  1. Put 1 tablespoon of the butter in a 25 cm (10 in) cast-iron skillet and heat it in the oven as it heats to 200 °C (400 °F).
  2. Melt the remaining butter. Stir the lemon juice into the almond milk and leave 5 minutes.
  3. Whisk the almond flour, coconut flour, baking powder, baking soda and salt.
  4. Beat the eggs, soured almond milk and melted butter, then stir into the dry ingredients. Rest 2 minutes so the coconut flour thickens the batter.
  5. Take out the hot skillet, swirl the butter to coat and pour in the batter; it should sizzle.
  6. Bake 20–25 minutes, until golden and a skewer comes out clean. Cool 10 minutes, then cut into 9 wedges.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.

With dairy-free butter the crust is crisp but less nutty; a spoonful of bacon fat in the skillet, if you eat it, adds the traditional flavour.

Tips

  • Honestly, it tastes like a savoury muffin rather than corn; serve it where cornbread would go — with chili, greens or soup.

Where it comes from

Cornbread · Charleston, South Carolina · North America · 1700s

Cornbread comes from Native American maize cooking, which European settlers adopted in North America. In the American South it became an everyday bread: usually unsweetened, made with buttermilk and baked in a hot, greased cast-iron skillet for a crisp crust. This version keeps the method but contains no corn.

Swedish-style seeded crispbread

Thin, shattering sheets of whole sunflower, pumpkin and sesame seeds, in the spirit of Swedish knäckebröd.

Dairy-free 2.2 g net carbs Serves 10 · 1 piece prep 10 min · cook 1 h · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • ½ cup sunflower seeds, hulled (70 g)
  • ½ cup pumpkin seeds (pepitas) (64 g)
  • ⅓ cup sesame seeds (48 g)
  • ¼ cup golden flaxseed meal (28 g)
  • 2 tbsp chia seeds (24 g)
  • ¼ cup almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (24 g)
  • 1 tbsp psyllium husk powder
  • ¾ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp caraway seeds, lightly crushed
  • ½ tsp fennel seeds, lightly crushed
  • 2 tbsp olive oil (27 g)
  • 1 cup water (237 g), boiling

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs2.2 g
Total carbohydrate6.4 g
Fibre4.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose0.0 g
Sugars0.4 g
Fat15.1 g
Protein5.7 g
Energy172 kcal

Contains: sesame, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: psyllium husk powder, water.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C (300 °F).
  2. Mix the seeds, flaxseed meal, almond flour, psyllium, salt, caraway and fennel. Stir in the olive oil and boiling water and rest 10 minutes until thick.
  3. Spread thinly and evenly between two sheets of parchment, about 3 mm (⅛ in), to fill a large baking sheet. Remove the top sheet.
  4. Bake 20 minutes, then cut into 10 pieces with a pizza cutter and bake 35–40 minutes more, until dry, crisp and lightly golden.
  5. Leave in the turned-off oven 30 minutes, then cool on a rack. Store airtight.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Caraway and fennel are the classic knäckebröd flavourings; leave them out for a plain crispbread.

Where it comes from

Knäckebröd · Sweden · Northern Europe · by the 1800s

Knäckebröd, Swedish crispbread, has been baked in Sweden for centuries, traditionally from rye. It was made in large rounds with a hole in the middle so it could be threaded on poles under the ceiling and kept dry for months. This grain-free version keeps the thin, crackling texture but builds it from seeds.

See it on the map

Basics, crusts & sauces

Almond-flour sweet pie crust

A short, buttery press-in crust in the spirit of French pâte sucrée, made with almond flour and blind-baked for tarts and pies.

Dairy-free option 2.2 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/8 of the crust prep 15 min · cook 15 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 2 cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (192 g)
  • 3 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (24 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), melted and slightly cooled
  • 1 large egg yolk
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.2 g2.2 g
Total carbohydrate7.6 g7.6 g
Fibre2.4 g2.4 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose3.0 g3.0 g
Sugars1.2 g1.2 g
Fat18.9 g18.8 g
Protein5.5 g5.5 g
Energy201 kcal200 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: eggs, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Have ready a 23 cm (9 in) pie dish or tart pan with a removable base.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, powdered erythritol and salt in a bowl, breaking up any lumps.
  3. Add the melted butter, egg yolk and vanilla and stir until the mixture clumps together like damp sand that holds when squeezed.
  4. Tip it into the pan and press it evenly over the base and up the sides with your fingers, then smooth it with the bottom of a flat glass. Aim for about 5 mm (1/4 in) thick, a little thicker where the base meets the sides.
  5. Prick the base all over with a fork and chill for 30 minutes so the butter firms and the sides hold their shape.
  6. Bake 12–15 minutes, until the edges are light golden. If the rim browns before the base is done, cover it with a strip of foil.
  7. Cool completely in the pan before filling. For a filling that bakes again, bake the crust only 8–10 minutes and shield the rim during the second bake.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — use a firm stick-style spread.

Dairy-free butter gives a slightly softer, less browned crust; chill it the full 30 minutes before baking so the sides do not slump.

Tips

  • Almond flour browns much faster than wheat pastry, so watch the last few minutes closely.
  • The crust is fragile while warm and firms as it cools; do not unmould it hot.

Where it comes from

Sweet shortcrust (pâte sucrée) · Paris · Western Europe · 1800s

Pâte sucrée, a sweet shortcrust enriched with sugar and egg, is one of the basic doughs of French pâtisserie and was part of the professional repertoire of Paris pastry shops by the 1800s. It gives the crisp, biscuit-like shell used for fruit and custard tarts. This almond-flour version keeps the idea of a rich, sweet, short crust but is pressed in rather than rolled, because almond flour has no gluten to hold a rolled sheet together.

See it on the map

Berry chia jam

A quick, spoonable jam of cooked berries set with chia seeds instead of a long boil with sugar and pectin.

Dairy-free 1.1 g net carbs Serves 24 · 1 tbsp prep 5 min · cook 8 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 250 g raspberries, fresh or frozen
  • 150 g strawberries, hulled and chopped
  • 3 tbsp allulose (36 g)
  • 1 tbsp fresh lemon juice (15 g)
  • 3 tbsp chia seeds (36 g)

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.1 g
Total carbohydrate3.9 g
Fibre1.3 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose1.5 g
Sugars0.8 g
Fat0.5 g
Protein0.4 g
Energy15 kcal

Contains: none of the common allergen groups.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Put the raspberries and strawberries in a small saucepan over medium heat and cook, stirring, until they release their juice and break down, 5–8 minutes. Mash with a fork for a smoother jam.
  2. Stir in the allulose and lemon juice and taste; add a little more sweetener if you like.
  3. Take the pan off the heat and stir in the chia seeds.
  4. Let it stand 30 minutes, stirring once or twice, until thickened. It firms more once chilled.
  5. Spoon into a clean jar and refrigerate up to 1 week, or freeze in portions.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • This is not shelf-stable: without sugar it must be kept cold.
  • Blueberries or blackberries work too; for a smooth jam, blend before adding the chia.

Where it comes from

Fruit jam · United States · North America · 2010s

This is a modern recipe that imitates traditional fruit jam, which in Europe was preserved by cooking fruit with large amounts of sugar or honey. Chia seeds, long eaten in Mexico and Central America, absorb liquid and turn to gel, and health-food cooks in the 2010s began using them to set quick refrigerator jams with little or no sugar.

See it on the map

Chantilly whipped cream

Softly whipped, lightly sweetened vanilla cream, the classic French finish for tarts, fruit and cakes.

Dairy-free option 0.9 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/4 cup prep 5 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g), very cold
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs0.9 g2.1 g
Total carbohydrate2.9 g4.1 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose2.0 g2.0 g
Sugars0.9 g1.0 g
Fat10.7 g10.4 g
Protein0.8 g1.0 g
Energy103 kcal100 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Chill the bowl and whisk (or beaters) in the freezer for 15 minutes.
  2. Pour in the cold cream and whip on medium speed until it starts to thicken and leaves trails.
  3. Add the powdered erythritol and vanilla and keep whipping until the cream holds soft peaks that droop at the tips. Stop for stiff peaks only if you are piping.
  4. Use straight away, or cover and refrigerate for up to a few hours; whisk briefly by hand before serving if it has loosened.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — chill the can overnight and whip only the firm solids.

Coconut cream whips to a denser, less airy foam with a clear coconut taste, and it softens faster at room temperature.

Tips

  • Powdered erythritol dissolves; granulated stays gritty in cold cream.
  • If you over-whip and it turns grainy, fold in a few tablespoons of unwhipped cream by hand.

Where it comes from

Whipped cream · Chantilly · Western Europe · 1600s

Crème Chantilly takes its name from the Château de Chantilly, north of Paris. A popular legend credits François Vatel, steward there in the 1600s, with inventing it, but that is legend rather than record: cooks across Europe were already whipping cream before his time. The name for sweetened whipped cream became standard in French cookery later.

See it on the map

Chocolate cookie crumb crust

A dark cocoa and almond-flour press-in crust that stands in for the crushed chocolate-wafer crusts of American icebox pies.

Dairy-free option 2.2 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/8 of the crust prep 10 min · cook 12 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 1½ cups almond flour (blanched, finely ground) (144 g)
  • ¼ cup unsweetened cocoa powder (22 g)
  • ¼ cup granulated erythritol (48 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 5 tbsp unsalted butter (71 g), melted
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.2 g2.2 g
Total carbohydrate11.0 g10.9 g
Fibre2.8 g2.8 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g6.0 g
Sugars0.9 g0.9 g
Fat17.0 g18.6 g
Protein4.5 g4.4 g
Energy178 kcal193 kcal

Contains: dairy, tree nuts. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Have ready a 23 cm (9 in) pie dish or springform pan.
  2. Whisk the almond flour, cocoa, erythritol and salt together, pressing out any lumps of cocoa.
  3. Pour in the melted butter and vanilla and mix until evenly moistened and dark all the way through.
  4. Press firmly over the base and up the sides of the pan (or over the base only, for a cheesecake). A flat-bottomed glass gives a compact, even layer.
  5. Bake 10–12 minutes. It will still feel soft; it crisps as it cools. Cocoa hides browning, so go by time and smell, and take it out if it smells scorched.
  6. Cool to room temperature, then chill at least 30 minutes before adding a no-bake filling.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter — melted; or use vegan_butter.

With coconut oil the crust is crisper straight from the fridge but softens quickly at room temperature, so serve chilled fillings cold.

Tips

  • For a crumb you can sprinkle on top, bake a little of the mixture loose on a tray and break it up once cool.

Where it comes from

Crumb crust · United States · North America · early 1900s

Pie shells made from crushed crackers or cookies bound with butter became common in American home baking in the first half of the 1900s, prized because they need little or no baking. Chocolate wafer crumbs became the usual base for cream and icebox pies. This version has no cookies to crush, so it bakes a cocoa and almond-flour mixture that ends up with a similar crumbly, dark crust.

Chocolate ganache

Unsweetened chocolate melted into hot cream sweetened with allulose: pour it warm as a glaze, or chill and whip it as a frosting.

Dairy-free option 2.0 g net carbs Serves 14 · 2 tbsp prep 5 min · cook 5 min · rest 30 min

Ingredients

  • 170 g unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao), finely chopped
  • ¾ cup heavy whipping cream (178 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.0 g2.5 g
Total carbohydrate8.6 g9.1 g
Fibre2.1 g2.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose4.5 g4.5 g
Sugars0.5 g0.6 g
Fat10.7 g10.5 g
Protein1.9 g2.0 g
Energy115 kcal114 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, unsweetened baking chocolate (100% cacao).

Method

  1. Put the chopped chocolate in a heatproof bowl.
  2. Heat the cream and allulose in a small pan, stirring, until the allulose dissolves and the cream just begins to steam at the edges. Do not let it boil hard.
  3. Pour the hot cream over the chocolate, add the salt, and leave it alone for 3 minutes.
  4. Stir from the centre outwards with a spatula in small circles until the mixture comes together smooth and glossy. Stir in the vanilla.
  5. For a glaze, use it while warm and pourable. For a spreadable frosting, leave it at room temperature 30–60 minutes until thick. For a whipped frosting, chill until cold but not hard, then beat until lighter in colour.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Coconut cream gives a ganache that sets slightly firmer in the fridge and tastes lightly of coconut; let it warm a little before spreading.

Tips

  • If it looks greasy or split, whisk in a tablespoon of warm cream at a time until it comes back together.
  • Unsweetened chocolate is quite bitter; taste and add a little more allulose dissolved in a spoonful of warm cream if you want it sweeter.

Where it comes from

Ganache · Paris · Western Europe · 1800s

Ganache, an emulsion of chocolate and cream, belongs to French confectionery of the 1800s. Its exact origin is uncertain: a well-known story about an apprentice spilling hot cream into chocolate is legend, and both French and Swiss confectioners have been credited. By the twentieth century it was the standard filling for truffles and a basic glaze in pastry kitchens.

See it on the map

Chocolate sauce

A pourable cocoa sauce made with coconut milk and allulose, dairy-free as written, that stays fluid in the fridge.

Dairy-free 1.1 g net carbs Serves 10 · 2 tbsp prep 5 min · cook 6 min

Ingredients

  • ¾ cup full-fat coconut milk (canned) (170 g)
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ⅓ cup unsweetened cocoa powder (28 g), sifted
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tbsp coconut oil (14 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs1.1 g
Total carbohydrate8.5 g
Fibre1.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.3 g
Sugars0.1 g
Fat5.4 g
Protein0.9 g
Energy56 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Whisk the coconut milk, allulose, cocoa and salt in a small saucepan until no dry cocoa remains.
  2. Bring to a gentle simmer over medium-low heat, whisking, and cook 3–4 minutes until it coats the back of a spoon. Keep the heat low: allulose scorches easily.
  3. Off the heat, whisk in the coconut oil and vanilla until glossy.
  4. Serve warm over ice cream or fruit. Refrigerate up to 1 week; it thickens when cold, so warm it briefly or thin with a spoonful of water.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Dutch-process cocoa gives a darker, milder sauce; natural cocoa a fruitier, sharper one.
  • Allulose is used because it stays dissolved; sauces made with erythritol turn crunchy in the fridge.

Where it comes from

Chocolate sauce · United States · North America · early 1900s

Sweet chocolate sauces for puddings and ices appear in European and American cookbooks by the 1800s. In the United States they became a fixture of the soda fountain in the late 1800s and early 1900s, poured over ice cream sundaes, whose own birthplace is disputed among several American towns. This version replaces sugar and dairy with allulose and coconut milk.

Coconut whipped cream

A dairy-free whipped topping made from the solid cream of a chilled can of coconut milk, sweetened and scented with vanilla.

Dairy-free 3.1 g net carbs Serves 6 · 1/4 cup prep 5 min · rest 12 h

Ingredients

  • 270 g coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), the firm solids from one chilled 400 ml (13.5 oz) can
  • 2 tbsp powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (16 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs3.1 g
Total carbohydrate5.8 g
Fibre0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose2.7 g
Sugars1.6 g
Fat15.8 g
Protein1.5 g
Energy151 kcal

Contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut cream (canned, unsweetened), powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Refrigerate the unopened can at least 12 hours, upright and without shaking it, so the cream separates and sets on top.
  2. Chill a bowl and whisk for 15 minutes. Open the can and scoop the solid cream into the bowl. Keep the watery liquid for smoothies; do not add it.
  3. Whip on medium-high speed for 1–2 minutes until smooth and fluffy.
  4. Add the powdered erythritol and vanilla and whip another 30 seconds, until it holds soft peaks.
  5. Serve cold. It firms up in the fridge; whisk briefly to loosen before using.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Brands differ a lot: some cans never separate because of added stabilisers. Look for one with only coconut and water, or buy canned coconut cream.
  • In a warm kitchen, set the bowl over ice while you whip.

Where it comes from

Whipped cream · United States · North America · 2000s

This is a modern dairy-free imitation of crème Chantilly. Whipping the chilled solid cream from canned coconut milk became popular with vegan, paleo and dairy-free home cooks in the 2000s and 2010s, spread largely through recipe blogs. It borrows the method and use of French whipped cream rather than any older tradition of its own.

Cream cheese frosting

Tangy, spreadable cream cheese frosting sweetened with powdered erythritol, for carrot cake, spice cakes and cupcakes.

Dairy-free option 1.1 g net carbs Serves 12 · about 2 tbsp prep 10 min

Ingredients

  • 225 g full-fat cream cheese, softened, block style
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), softened
  • ⅔ cup powdered erythritol (confectioners' style) (79 g)
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tbsp heavy whipping cream (15 g), only if needed to loosen

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs1.1 g1.8 g
Total carbohydrate7.7 g8.6 g
Fibre0.0 g0.2 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.6 g6.6 g
Sugars0.8 g0.5 g
Fat10.7 g8.9 g
Protein1.2 g1.0 g
Energy106 kcal95 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, tree nuts.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: powdered erythritol (confectioners' style).

Method

  1. Beat the butter on medium speed until creamy, about 1 minute.
  2. Add the cream cheese and beat just until smooth and combined. Over-beating cream cheese makes the frosting runny.
  3. Add the powdered erythritol, vanilla and salt and beat on low until incorporated, then on medium for 30 seconds.
  4. If it is too stiff to spread, beat in the cream a teaspoon at a time.
  5. Frost a cooled cake. Keep frosted cakes refrigerated.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free cream cheese (cashew or almond based) instead of full-fat cream cheese — firm block style.
  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream.

Dairy-free cream cheese gives a softer, less tangy frosting; chill it 20–30 minutes before spreading and add a squeeze of lemon juice for tang.

Tips

  • Both cream cheese and butter should be soft but still cool; warm ingredients make a loose frosting.
  • Erythritol can taste slightly cooling here; replacing a quarter of it with allulose softens that.

Where it comes from

Cream cheese frosting · United States · North America · mid-1900s

Cream cheese was first sold commercially in New York State in the 1870s. Frostings made by beating it with butter and sugar became an American staple in the mid-1900s, closely tied to carrot cake and, later, red velvet cake. It remains the classic topping for both.

Crème anglaise

A pourable vanilla custard sauce of milk, cream and egg yolks, cooked gently until it coats a spoon.

Dairy-free option 2.8 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/4 cup prep 10 min · cook 12 min · rest 1 h

Ingredients

  • 1 cup whole milk (244 g)
  • 1 cup heavy whipping cream (238 g)
  • 5 large egg yolks
  • ⅓ cup allulose (63 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.8 g2.2 g
Total carbohydrate10.7 g10.1 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose7.9 g7.9 g
Sugars2.6 g0.2 g
Fat14.5 g15.7 g
Protein3.5 g2.9 g
Energy160 kcal159 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Set a bowl inside a larger bowl of ice water and put a fine sieve over it.
  2. Heat the milk and cream in a saucepan until steaming and small bubbles appear at the edge.
  3. Meanwhile whisk the egg yolks, allulose and salt in a bowl until smooth and slightly paler.
  4. Pour about a third of the hot milk into the yolks in a thin stream, whisking, then pour everything back into the pan.
  5. Cook over low heat, stirring constantly with a spatula, until the sauce thickens enough to coat the spatula and a finger drawn through it leaves a clean line, 80–82 °C (176–180 °F). Never let it boil.
  6. Immediately strain it into the chilled bowl, stir in the vanilla, and stir now and then as it cools. Refrigerate, covered, up to 3 days. Serve cold or barely warm.

Dairy-free version

  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of whole milk.
  • Use full-fat coconut milk (canned) instead of heavy whipping cream — use coconut milk for both, not coconut cream, or the sauce is too heavy.

Made with coconut milk the sauce is slightly thicker and tastes of coconut; it pairs best with tropical fruit and chocolate.

Tips

  • If it starts to curdle, pull it off the heat at once and blend it smooth while still warm.
  • Churned in an ice cream maker, the same custard becomes a vanilla ice cream base.

Where it comes from

Custard sauce · Paris · Western Europe · 1800s

Crème anglaise means 'English cream', a name usually explained as a nod to the English fondness for pouring custard. Sweetened milk thickened with egg yolks was made in both countries for centuries, and the sauce was a fixture of classical French cookery by the 1800s. It is also the base of many custards and ice creams.

See it on the map

Lemon curd

A sharp, silky lemon curd thickened with egg yolks and finished with butter, sweetened with allulose so it stays smooth in the fridge.

Dairy-free option 0.9 g net carbs Serves 12 · 2 tbsp prep 10 min · cook 10 min · rest 2 h

Ingredients

  • 1 tbsp lemon zest, from about 2 lemons
  • ½ cup fresh lemon juice (122 g)
  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 large egg yolks
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 6 tbsp unsalted butter (85 g), cold, cut into cubes

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs0.9 g0.9 g
Total carbohydrate8.9 g8.9 g
Fibre0.1 g0.1 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose8.0 g8.0 g
Sugars0.3 g0.3 g
Fat7.3 g7.2 g
Protein1.6 g1.5 g
Energy78 kcal77 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose.

Method

  1. Whisk the zest, lemon juice, allulose, eggs, yolks and salt in a heavy saucepan (not aluminium, which reacts with the acid) until smooth.
  2. Cook over low heat, stirring constantly with a spatula and scraping the bottom and corners, until it thickens enough to coat the spatula and reaches 77–80 °C (170–175 °F), about 6–10 minutes. Do not let it boil or the eggs will curdle.
  3. Take it off the heat and stir in the butter a few cubes at a time until melted and glossy.
  4. Press it through a fine sieve into a jar to remove the zest and any cooked egg.
  5. Press plastic wrap onto the surface and refrigerate at least 2 hours; it thickens further as it chills. Keeps about 1 week refrigerated.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter (0.8 × the amount) — refined, for a neutral flavour.

With coconut oil the curd sets firmer when cold and has a slightly less creamy finish; let it stand 10 minutes at room temperature before spreading.

Tips

  • Erythritol crystallises in a chilled curd; allulose stays smooth.
  • A double boiler makes the cooking slower but almost impossible to curdle.

Where it comes from

Lemon curd · England · Northern Europe · 1800s

Fruit curds, also called 'lemon cheese', became popular in England in the 1800s as a spread for bread, scones and tarts. Earlier English recipes for creams and puddings made with lemon juice and eggs point the way, but the butter-enriched curd kept in jars is the nineteenth-century form. It remains a teatime staple in Britain.

See it on the map

Nut-free coconut-flour pie crust

A nut-free press-in pie shell built on coconut flour, with eggs to bind it because coconut flour soaks up so much liquid.

Dairy-free option 2.9 g net carbs Serves 8 · 1/8 of the crust prep 15 min · cook 14 min · rest 10 min

Ingredients

  • ¾ cup coconut flour (84 g)
  • 2 tbsp granulated erythritol (24 g)
  • ¼ tsp fine salt
  • 6 tbsp unsalted butter (85 g), melted
  • 2 large eggs, at room temperature
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs2.9 g2.9 g
Total carbohydrate9.4 g9.4 g
Fibre3.5 g3.5 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose3.0 g3.0 g
Sugars1.5 g1.5 g
Fat11.2 g13.1 g
Protein3.8 g3.7 g
Energy138 kcal156 kcal

Contains: coconut, dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: coconut, eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: coconut flour, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Heat the oven to 175 °C (350 °F). Grease a 23 cm (9 in) pie dish.
  2. Whisk the coconut flour, erythritol and salt together.
  3. Beat the eggs with the melted butter and vanilla, then stir them into the dry mix to make a soft dough.
  4. Let the dough stand 10 minutes. Coconut flour keeps absorbing liquid, and the dough firms up into something that presses cleanly instead of sticking.
  5. Press the dough evenly over the base and up the sides of the dish, about 5 mm (1/4 in) thick. Wet fingers help if it sticks.
  6. Prick the base all over and bake 12–14 minutes, until firm and just golden at the rim.
  7. Cool before filling. For a filling that bakes in the shell, pre-bake only 8 minutes.

Dairy-free version

  • Use coconut oil instead of unsalted butter — melted; keeps the crust nut-free, unlike many dairy-free butters.

Coconut oil makes a firmer, crisper shell when cold and a slightly greasier one when warm; it also deepens the coconut flavour.

Tips

  • Do not add more coconut flour if the dough looks wet at first; give it the full 10 minutes to thicken.
  • Check the label of any dairy-free butter if you need the crust truly nut-free: many are made with cashew or almond.

Where it comes from

Pie crust · United States · North America · 2000s

This is a modern recipe. Coconut flour, the dried and ground pulp left after pressing coconut milk, was taken up widely by gluten-free, paleo and low-carb home bakers in the United States in the 2000s. Press-in shells like this one imitate a traditional pie crust for people who avoid both wheat and nuts.

See it on the map

Salted caramel sauce

A glossy, buttery salted caramel made with allulose, which browns like sugar and stays smooth instead of recrystallising.

Dairy-free option 0.2 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1 tbsp prep 5 min · cook 12 min · rest 20 min

Ingredients

  • ½ cup allulose (96 g)
  • 2 tbsp water (30 g)
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (57 g), cut into pieces
  • ½ cup heavy whipping cream (119 g), warmed
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ½ tsp flaky sea salt, to finish

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs0.2 g0.5 g
Total carbohydrate6.2 g6.5 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose6.0 g6.0 g
Sugars0.3 g0.3 g
Fat5.6 g5.4 g
Protein0.2 g0.2 g
Energy54 kcal53 kcal

Contains: dairy. Dairy-free version contains: coconut.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, water.

Method

  1. Put the allulose and water in a heavy, light-coloured saucepan so you can see the colour change. Stir once to wet it, then cook over medium-low heat without stirring.
  2. Let it bubble until it turns a deep amber, 6–10 minutes. Allulose browns faster and at a lower temperature than sugar, so stay with it and swirl the pan rather than stirring; pull it off the heat the moment it smells toasty.
  3. Off the heat, whisk in the butter a piece at a time. It will foam.
  4. Slowly pour in the warm cream, whisking; stand back, as it sputters.
  5. Return to low heat and simmer 2–3 minutes, whisking, until smooth and slightly thickened. Stir in the salt and vanilla.
  6. Cool 20 minutes before using; it thickens as it cools. Sprinkle a few flakes of sea salt over the sauce as you serve it. Refrigerate up to 2 weeks and warm gently to loosen.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter.
  • Use coconut cream (canned, unsweetened) instead of heavy whipping cream — warmed.

With coconut cream the sauce is a little thinner and tastes faintly of coconut; simmer it a minute longer to thicken.

Tips

  • Erythritol does not caramelise and crystallises as it cools, so it is not a substitute here.
  • Add the salt in stages and taste: the Breton style is noticeably salty.

Where it comes from

Caramel · Quiberon, Brittany · Western Europe · 1970s

Brittany has long been known for salted butter, and caramel made with it is a regional speciality. The modern fame of salted-butter caramel is often credited to the Quiberon confectioner Henri Le Roux, whose salted-butter caramels of the 1970s were widely copied. The salty-sweet sauce has since spread far beyond France.

Sugar-free maple-style syrup

A pourable pancake syrup of lightly caramelised allulose and maple extract, with the colour and roasted sweetness of maple syrup but no maple sugar.

Dairy-free 0.0 g net carbs Serves 10 · 2 tbsp prep 2 min · cook 12 min · rest 15 min

Ingredients

  • 1 cup allulose (192 g), divided
  • ¾ cup water (178 g), divided
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • ⅛ tsp xanthan gum
  • 1 tsp maple extract
  • ½ tsp pure vanilla extract
  • ⅛ tsp ground cinnamon, optional, a tiny pinch

Nutrition

Per servingAs written
Net carbs0.0 g
Total carbohydrate19.3 g
Fibre0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose19.2 g
Sugars0.0 g
Fat0.0 g
Protein0.0 g
Energy8 kcal

Contains: none of the common allergen groups.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, maple extract, water, xanthan gum.

Method

  1. Put a third of the allulose and 1 tbsp of the water in a heavy saucepan and cook over medium-low heat, swirling rather than stirring, until it turns a deep amber, 5–8 minutes. Allulose browns quickly, so watch it closely.
  2. Take the pan off the heat and very carefully pour in the rest of the water; it will spit and seize.
  3. Return to low heat, add the remaining allulose and the salt, and stir until the hardened caramel has dissolved and the syrup is clear, 3–4 minutes.
  4. Sprinkle the xanthan gum over the surface while whisking hard so it does not clump, then simmer 1 minute more.
  5. Off the heat, stir in the maple extract, vanilla and the cinnamon, if using. Cool 15 minutes; it thickens as it cools. Refrigerate up to 1 month and warm before serving.

Dairy-free as written

Tips

  • Maple extracts vary in strength; add half, taste, and add the rest if needed.
  • Too thick when cold? Stir in a spoonful of hot water.

Where it comes from

Maple syrup (imitation) · Quebec · North America · pre-colonial

This sugar-free syrup imitates maple syrup; its flavour comes from maple extract, not maple sugar. Indigenous peoples of northeastern North America were boiling maple sap into syrup and sugar before Europeans arrived, and European settlers learned the practice from them. Quebec is today by far the largest producer of maple syrup.

Swiss meringue buttercream

A silky, stable buttercream made by warming egg whites with sweetener over a water bath, whipping them to a meringue and beating in butter.

Dairy-free option 0.1 g net carbs Serves 16 · 1/4 cup prep 30 min · cook 8 min

Ingredients

  • 4 large egg whites
  • ½ cup granulated erythritol (96 g)
  • ¼ cup allulose (48 g)
  • ⅛ tsp fine salt
  • 1½ cups unsalted butter (340 g), softened but cool, cut into tablespoons
  • 2 tsp pure vanilla extract

Nutrition

Per servingAs writtenDairy-free
Net carbs0.1 g0.1 g
Total carbohydrate9.1 g9.1 g
Fibre0.0 g0.0 g
Sugar alcohols & allulose9.0 g9.0 g
Sugars0.1 g0.1 g
Fat17.3 g16.8 g
Protein1.1 g0.9 g
Energy161 kcal160 kcal

Contains: dairy, eggs. Dairy-free version contains: eggs.

From typical product labels, which vary by brand: allulose, granulated erythritol.

Method

  1. Set a heatproof mixer bowl over a pan of barely simmering water, without the bowl touching the water. Make sure the bowl is completely free of grease.
  2. Whisk the egg whites, erythritol, allulose and salt in the bowl until the mixture reaches 71 °C (160 °F) on a thermometer and feels smooth, not grainy, when rubbed between your fingers, about 5–8 minutes.
  3. Move the bowl to the mixer and whip on high speed until the meringue is thick, glossy and the bowl feels cool, about 8–10 minutes.
  4. Switch to medium speed and add the butter one piece at a time, waiting until each is mixed in.
  5. If it looks curdled, keep beating: it comes together. If it stays soupy, chill the bowl 10 minutes and beat again; if it stays lumpy and cold, warm the side of the bowl briefly.
  6. Beat in the vanilla. Use at once, or refrigerate up to a week and re-beat at room temperature before using.

Dairy-free version

  • Use dairy-free butter (stick, plant-based) instead of unsalted butter — firm stick style, not a tub spread.

Dairy-free butter makes a softer buttercream that loosens quickly in a warm room; chill the frosted cake to set it and pipe only in a cool kitchen.

Tips

  • The allulose helps stop the erythritol from recrystallising into a gritty texture after chilling.
  • Butter that is too warm gives a soupy buttercream; it should dent with a finger but not look shiny.

Where it comes from

Buttercream · Switzerland · Western Europe · 1900s

Meringue appears in French and English recipes by the 1600s and 1700s; the claim that it is named after the Swiss town of Meiringen is popular but unproven. Pastry cooks call a meringue whose whites and sugar are warmed together over a water bath 'Swiss', as a classification rather than a documented birthplace. Beating butter into it gives a stable buttercream that became a standard of twentieth-century cake decorating.
